# Chapter digests, set 1: 220 chapters

### part-00-orientation/01-the-ai-factory-landscape-and-the-roles-in-it.md
Title: 0.1 The AI factory landscape and the roles in it | words 6193 | code blocks 1
Headings: Where the AI factory idea comes from / Becoming an AI company / Data comes first / The layers / Training versus inference / Learning at the speed of the factory / Why the lambda architecture exists / The speed layer and the serving layer / Cloud and edge / Cyber-physical systems and virtual factories / Machines and data at scale / Virtual factories / Cyber-physical systems in manufacturing / AI in manufacturing / Robotics and processes / The three levels of AI competency / Smart factories / Why AI adoption is accelerating / Digital twins in industry / The data-driven approach / Pros and cons / Learning failure / Implementing a data-driven approach / Enablers / Governance, business and technology / Six implementation steps / Policy and sustainability / Data-driven policymaking / Co-creating policy / Government and transport infrastructure / The triple bottom line / The roles / Common failure at this level / Checkpoint questions / Sources and review notes
Opening: An **AI factory** is a data center built to turn electricity and data into tokens: trained models and answered requests. Everything in this book hangs off that one idea. The unit of output is a token, the unit of cost is GPU-hours, and your job as an infrastructure engineer is to keep the expensive part (the GPUs) busy and healthy. Most businesses today use machine learning (ML) as their key AI technology. An ML algorithm is a statistical engine. It finds patterns in past observations and uses them to predict new outcomes. When you combine ML with data sources, experiments and software, you get what the book calls an AI factory.
Middle: The real manufacturing world is moving toward the digital one. This lets organisations plan and project the whole life cycle of products and facilities. A smart factory aims to run production with little human involvement. The book describes a pyramid of four levels: device, supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA), manufacturing operations management (MOM), and enterprise.
Exercise sample: (no Exercises section)

### part-00-orientation/02-how-to-use-this-book-and-set-up-your-labs-mac-gcp-gke-nebius.md
Title: 0.2 How to use this book and set up your labs (Mac, GCP/GKE, Nebius) | words 457 | code blocks 1

Opening: Each chapter goes basics, then intermediate, then advanced. Most contain: a mental-model diagram, the commands and configurations that matter, a "how it fails" section (symptom, diagnosis, fix), a lab, checkpoint questions with answers and sources. Output shown as *illustrative* or *untested here* was not captured from live hardware; verify it on your own system before relying on it. 1. **Mac:** install Homebrew, then Git, Python 3, Docker Desktop (or Colima), `kubectl`, `helm`, Terraform and Ansible. 2. **GCP:** create a project, set a billing budget alert **first**, install the `gcloud` CLI, and enable the Compute and Kubernetes APIs. 3. **Nebius:** create an account and a project, install its CLI, and start with the smallest GPU instance that satisfies the lab.
Middle: 1. **Mac:** install Homebrew, then Git, Python 3, Docker Desktop (or Colima), `kubectl`, `helm`, Terraform and Ansible. 2. **GCP:** create a project, set a billing budget alert **first**, install the `gcloud` CLI, and enable the Compute and Kubernetes APIs. 3. **Nebius:** create an account and a project, install its CLI, and start with the smallest GPU instance that satisfies the lab.

Opening: Think about a bad day on an AI cluster. A training run slows to a crawl, a container refuses to start, or a driver stops loading after an update. Before you can fix any of these, you need to know how much of the factory runs Linux. Every machine in an AI factory runs Linux. That includes the GPU servers, the Slurm controller, the Kubernetes nodes, the storage servers, the BMCs' companion hosts, and even the data center switches. Look inside a modern spine or leaf switch on an AI fabric and you will find a full Linux distribution driving the switching silicon. Even the switches run Linux, so a network fault can have a Linux cause. The OpenCompute Project builds Open Network Linux, or ONL, which underpins Big Switch's Switch Light and the DENT Project's dentOS. NVIDIA ships Debian-based Cumulus Linux on its Spectrum Ethernet switches. Microsoft, Intel, NVIDIA, Broadcom, and Dell collaborate on SONiC, which stands for Software for Open Networking in the Cloud. Automation tools like Ansible
Middle: Reading about layers is not enough. You need to see them on a real machine. Every command below targets a live Linux VM, so each one is marked `(untested here)`. The deterministic model checks run locally instead, in `code/part01/linux_layers_lab.py`.

You should answer before you look. 1. **Layer Classification on a GPU Node:** Classify each of the following 8 components as hardware, kernel, or user space. The components are (a) `connectx_hca`, (b) `nvidia.ko`, (c) `nvidia_uvm.ko`, (d) `mlx5_core.ko`, (e) `libcuda.so`, (f) `libibverbs.so`, (g) `systemd`, and (h) `slurmd`. Check your answers with `classify_component_layer()` in `code/part01/linux_layers.py`. 2. **Root (UID 0) versus Kernel Mode (Ring 0):** A system administrator runs a Python script as `root` (UID 0). "Argument list too long" / 6. `bad interpreter: No such file or directory` on a script that exists / 7. A failing pipeline stage hidden by a later one / 8. A "bash script" that fails on another distribution: `/bin/sh` is not bash / Hands-on lab: shell parsing, redirection, and VM drills / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a training job that fails overnight. The log is quiet, the script reports success, and nobody can say why. Often the cause is a small shell mistake. You will spend much of your career typing commands into terminals on machines you did not build: a GPU node over SSH, a Slurm login node, the shell inside a container, or a BMC console. Every cluster job script, every Dockerfile `RUN` line, every Ansible `command:` task, every systemd `ExecStart=` and every CI step is a shell command line in disguise. People who have used a shell for years but never learned *how a line is actually processed* keep getting bitten by the same things. A space in a filename breaks a copy, and an unquoted variable splits into pieces. Errors vanish into the wrong stream, a pipeline reports success while its first stage failed, and an environment variable exists in one shell but disappears in another.
Middle: The same distinction applies to any variable. A plain assignment such as `STUFF=blah` creates a **shell variable**, visible only to the shell. `export STUFF` turns it into an **environment variable** that every child inherits. Programs read their configuration from the environment. `LESS`, for example, holds your preferred `less` options. Many manual pages end with an ENVIRONMENT section that lists the variables the program honours.
Exercise sample: 1. **Redirection Order (`> file 2>&1` vs `2>&1 > file` vs `3>&1 1>&2 2>&3`):** Using the left-to-right file-descriptor binding rule, state where file descriptor 1 (`stdout`) and file descriptor 2 (`stderr`) end up for: (a) `cmd > out.txt 2>&1`, (b) `cmd 2>&1 > out.txt`, and (c) `cmd 3>&1 1>&2 2>&3 | grep -i warning`. Verify with `simulate_redirection_order()` in `code/part01/shell_pipeline.py`. 2. **Decoding Exit Statuses (0, 126, 127, 130, 137, 143):** Decode each exit status and state the exact signal number and name where applicable. An archive overwrote system files or exploded the working directory / 6. `ls` or a job takes forever in a directory with millions of entries / Hands-on lab: inodes, links, and VM drills / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a disk that `df` reports as full while plenty of free gigabytes show. Or a checkpoint directory whose `latest` link points at nothing. Or a dataset directory that takes ten minutes just to list. These problems look mysterious until you know how Linux names, stores and links files. Everything in an AI cluster ends up as files on a filesystem. That includes datasets with millions of small files, multi-gigabyte checkpoints, container image layers, Slurm and Kubernetes configuration, logs, driver modules, and the pseudo-files under `/proc` and `/sys` that expose the kernel and the hardware. Many failures that stop training runs are filesystem facts, and they only look mysterious until you know the model.
Middle: Learn the basic form until it is automatic: `find DIR -name 'PATTERN' -print`. `DIR` is where to start. `-name` matches the file name against a pattern, and `-print` prints each match. GNU `find` prints by default when no action is given, though older versions require `-print`. The pattern is single-quoted because `find`, not the shell, must interpret the `*` and `?`. Unquoted, the shell would expand the pattern against the current directory first. Reach for `-exec`, `-delete` and the other actions only once the basic form is second nature, because an action applied to the wrong set of files is how data gets lost.
Exercise sample: Work these on paper or in a scratch directory. Each one checks a single idea from the chapter, so write down your answer before you look back at the text. 1. **FHS Directory Placement:** State the exact FHS directory path where each of the following lives on a GPU node. Opening: Picture a 256-GPU job that misbehaves at 3 a.m. You will not open a dashboard first. You will pull the right ten lines out of ten gigabytes of logs, then count, group and compare them. Infrastructure produces text: logs, `nvidia-smi` tables, `squeue` and `sinfo` listings, `dmesg`, `journalctl`, `kubectl` output, configuration files, CSV exports of metrics, and JSON from every API. The tools in this chapter are old, small and ubiquitous. They are installed on every node you will ever log into, including the nodes where you cannot install anything. Fluency here turns a half-hour investigation into a one-line pipeline. It is also the foundation for the shell scripts of chapter 1.9, so the time you spend on this chapter pays back later.
Middle: Large files. These tools stream their input, so a 50 GB log does not need 50 GB of memory. Two things still need care. `sort` on a huge file spills to temporary disk, which you can control with `TMPDIR` or `-T`. And `awk` arrays grow with the number of keys. Filter early with `grep`, and use `head` only after the expensive part. A pipeline stops early when a downstream `head` closes, because the upstream process receives SIGPIPE.
Exercise sample: Work through these before you check the lab output, because you learn more from the attempt. 1. **`uniq -c` Without `sort` versus `sort | uniq -c`:** Given the input lines `["alice", "bob", "alice", "alice", "bob"]`, state the exact `(count, name)` output tuples produced by (a) `uniq -c` alone and (b) `sort | uniq -c`. Opening: You will almost never sit at the machines you manage. GPU servers live in data centres and clouds, so you reach them remotely. A typical route runs through a bastion host, then a login node, then a compute node, and along the way you move checkpoints and datasets. Everything rides on **SSH**, the protocol for secure remote logins. It carries interactive shells, file copies, port tunnels to Jupyter and TensorBoard, Ansible, Slurm launch scripts, Git over SSH, and the cluster managers behind them. Mistakes here cost you in two directions. A clumsy workflow wastes hours. Dropped sessions can kill a three-day training run, and a careless file sync can delete the wrong directory. The second cost is worse. Weak SSH habits, such as shared keys, forwarded agents and password logins, are how clusters get compromised. Sergey Zhumatiy puts the balance well in *Supercomputers for Linux SysAdmins*: *the less access, the better, yet users should not be clamped in a hedgehog's grip*.
Middle: Two classic books cover them in depth. The first is *Learning the vi and Vim Editors*, 7th edition, by Arnold Robbins, Elbert Hannah and Linda Lamb, published by O'Reilly in 2008. The second is *Learning GNU Emacs*, 3rd edition, by Debra Cameron, James Elliott, Marc Loy, Eric Raymond and Bill Rosenblatt, published by O'Reilly in 2004. A friendlier editor such as `nano` is fine for occasional edits. Habits form around the first tool you use, though, so for daily administration invest in vim or Emacs. One more distinction matters. Editors like vim run inside the terminal, using the standard terminal interface. GUI editors open their own window, independent of the
Exercise sample: 1. **OpenSSH `StrictModes` Permission Validation:** Decide whether sshd and ssh accept or reject public-key authentication under each permission set. Start with (a) `~` = 0750, `~/.ssh` = 0700, authorized_keys = 0600, and `id_ed25519` = 0600. Then try (b) `~/.ssh` = 0777 with authorized_keys = 0666, (c) `~` = 0777, a world-writable home directory, and (d) `id_ed25519` = 0644, a world-readable private key. Verify your answers with `validate_ssh_strict_modes()` in `code/part01/remote_ssh_rsync.py`. 2. **rsync Trailing-Slash Rule versus `scp -r` Nesting:** Suppose the directory `data` contains `["a.pt", "b.pt"]`, and `/scratch/data` already exists on gpu01.

AI-infrastructure patterns / How it fails / 1. "Permission denied" although the file looks readable / 2. New files in a shared directory have the wrong group or permissions
Opening: Picture a training job that starts fine on a login node, then fails on a compute node because it cannot read its dataset. Or a checkpoint directory that rejects writes from a teammate. On a shared cluster, most of these "it works for me but not for the job" problems come down to identity or permissions. Files owned by root appear in your project after a container run. A Slurm job fails because one user has a different UID on two nodes. A sudo rule locks an administrator out, or a login node freezes under one user's fork bomb. This chapter explains how Linux decides who you are and what you may do. It starts with the numeric IDs the kernel uses and ends with the policy files an administrator edits. It also shows how those ideas stretch across hundreds of cluster nodes. Finally, it covers the user environment and the shell startup files that decide what every new shell and job starts with.
Middle: Have you ever opened a terminal and found that your `PATH` or your editor setting was missing? When a shell starts, it reads configuration files that set its defaults. Those defaults include the command path, the prompt, aliases, the umask and the editor. These are the **startup files**, also called dot files because their names start with a `.`. Keep them simple, short and well commented. Over time they collect clutter, and the clutter causes confusing problems in subshells and batch jobs.

Opening: Picture a GPU node that worked on Friday. After an automatic upgrade and a reboot on Monday, its GPUs have disappeared. Almost everything you will operate arrives on a node as software somebody else built. The NVIDIA driver, the CUDA toolkit, the container runtime, `nvidia-container-toolkit`, the Slurm daemons, the InfiniBand stack (OFED or the distribution's inbox drivers) and the monitoring agents all arrive this way. Getting this layer wrong produces three kinds of incident in GPU fleets. 1. **Version drift.** One node is auto-upgraded and ends up with a kernel that the installed driver does not support, so the GPUs vanish after the next reboot. Another node has a different CUDA minor version, and a job that worked on the first fails on the second. 2. **Unknown provenance.** A library copied into `/usr/local/lib` years ago shadows the one the package manager installed, and nobody can say where it came from. 3. **Broken dependency resolution.** An engineer forces an install with `--nodeps` or `--force` to get past an error, and the
Middle: When the compiler cannot find a header, search for it with `locate`, `find`, or the package tools from section 5. Several headers can share a name in different places, so confirm you are using the right one.

Which sidecar file gets written, when the spec marks the file %config(noreplace) and when it marks it %config (replace)? Opening: It is the middle of the night, and a training run that was fast yesterday now crawls. Or a pod refuses to terminate. Or a node shows a load average of 90 while its CPUs sit idle. Each of these problems is a process problem, so you need a clear model of processes before you can fix them. Everything an AI platform runs is a process. A training script and its data-loader workers are processes. So are the NCCL communication threads, `slurmd` and `slurmstepd`, `containerd`, the NVIDIA persistence daemon and a vLLM server. When something goes wrong, the questions you ask are process questions:
Middle: `PR` is the kernel's current priority for the process. A higher number makes the kernel less likely to schedule that process when others need CPU time. The kernel can also change `PR` while the program runs, based on how much CPU the process has used. `NI` is the nice value, which is the setting you control. The kernel adds it to the current priority to pick the next time slot. Here every row shows `NI` as `0`, the default.

Opening: Most of an infrastructure engineer's day is glue work, and the glue is mostly shell. You will meet it as the entrypoint of a training container, the `sbatch` script that launches 64 ranks, or the node health check that runs before every job. It also shows up as the `user_data` that bootstraps a cloud VM, the Dockerfile `RUN` lines, the CI step that builds a driver package, and the prolog and epilog scripts that run as root on every compute node. Shell is available everywhere, starts instantly and composes other tools well. It is also unforgiving. A missing quote can delete the wrong directory. An unchecked error can let a job "succeed" with no output. A script that works on your laptop can break when `cron` or `slurmd` runs it with a different environment. This chapter takes you from the first line of a script to patterns that hold up in production. You will follow two threads. The first is the language itself: quoting, variables, exit codes, tests, loops, functions,
Middle: Much of what people reach for `sed`, `awk` or `basename` can be done in bash itself. Bash does it in the same process, which is faster and avoids quoting traps. The table lists the forms you will use most.

Opening: Picture a node that will not show all of its GPUs, or a job that suddenly runs slowly. Both are device problems, and you can only fix them if you know how Linux presents hardware. An AI node is a pile of devices. It has eight GPUs, several InfiniBand or RoCE NICs, NVMe drives, NVSwitch chips, a BMC, a PCIe switch tree, sensors, and the CPUs and memory banks they hang off. Almost every operational question about such a node is a device question: Linux answers all of these with a few consistent interfaces. The first is the device files in `/dev`. The second is sysfs in `/sys`, and the third is procfs in `/proc`. The fourth is udev, the user-space daemon that turns kernel hardware events into device files, permissions, names and actions.
Middle: Hardware identification databases come from `systemd-hwdb`. They supply the vendor names that `lspci` and `lsusb` show, along with the USB device classes. The database lives in `/usr/lib/udev/hwdb.d` and is rebuilt with `systemd-hwdb update`. Mentioned here so you recognise them. Networking details are in Part 2.

Opening: Picture a training job that will not start. The log says a shared library is missing, or that a version is too old. Many of the failures you will debug on a GPU platform look like this: the program could not start, or could not load something it needs. You will see messages such as `libcuda.so.1: cannot open shared object file`, `version 'GLIBC_2.34' not found`, `version 'GLIBCXX_3.4.30' not found`, `CUDA error: no kernel image is available`, and `error while loading shared libraries: libnccl.so.2`. A Python import that hangs, or a container that cannot find the NVIDIA driver files, belongs in the same group. So does a binary that "works on the build machine but not here". The other failures are slow or stuck programs. A training job may spin in `futex`. A data loader may make millions of tiny `read` calls. A process may block in `ioctl` on `/dev/nvidia0`. In every case, the real question is what the program asks the kernel to do. To debug both kinds, you need
Middle: Prefer software you can *observe*. A slightly slower tool with rich metrics usually beats an opaque faster one, because visibility lets you remove waste. Consider algorithmic complexity too. An O(n²) step that was fine at 1,000 items becomes the whole runtime at 1,000,000 items. Constant costs dominate only for small n.

Then you should explain why a glibc-linked binary, run inside an Alpine (musl) container, fails with `No such file or directory` even though `./train` exists and is executable. 2. **Shared Library Naming (`linker_name`, `soname`, `real_name`) and `GLIBC` Symbol Versioning:** (a) For the file `libfoo.so.1.2.3`, state its `linker_name`, `soname` and `real_name`. Opening: Picture a cluster of thousands of GPUs that suddenly stalls. The dashboards point at compute, but the real cause is often storage. Storage in AI infrastructure has two jobs. It must feed GPUs at their maximum ingestion rate. It must also checkpoint very large model states without stalling training. The questions you will ask every day are all storage questions: This chapter builds the stack from the bottom. You will start with what disks are and how they behave. Then you will see how a disk is divided into partitions, using MBR and GPT. Next comes the filesystem, which you will look at from the inside: inodes, directories, journals and extents. After that you will create, mount, tune, check and repair one. The chapter also covers the special filesystems the kernel provides.
Middle: Swap is disk or file space that the kernel uses to hold less-used memory pages. The chapter on memory (1.17) covers when and how the kernel uses it. To add swap, run `mkswap /dev/sdb2 && swapon /dev/sdb2`. A swap file also works. Create it with `fallocate -l 8G /swapfile; chmod 600 /swapfile; mkswap /swapfile; swapon /swapfile`. Avoid sparse files, and note that some filesystems (btrfs, and some XFS cases) need special handling. Then add an `fstab` line with type `swap`. Use `swapoff` to remove swap. Check the state with `swapon --show`, `free -h`, or `cat /proc/swaps`.

AI-infrastructure patterns / The full data path, layer by layer / Local NVMe as cache and staging area / DGX-style layout and build / Kubernetes and local volumes
Opening: Suppose a GPU node runs fine for a week, then slows down in the middle of a training run. Storage is one of the first places to look. A GPU server is full of fast local flash. M.2 drives hold the operating system, and a bank of U.2 or E1.S NVMe drives holds datasets, caches and checkpoints. Cloud GPU instances add local SSDs and network-attached volumes that you can resize while the machine runs. How you present those drives to the operating system decides whether the node is fast, resilient and easy to operate. This chapter covers four areas. It starts with logical volumes and the device mapper. Then it moves to software RAID with `md`, and to the other device-mapper layers: encryption, multipath and caching. Next it goes deep on NVMe, including architecture, `nvme-cli`, SMART and NVMe-oF. The last areas are disk performance and the AI-specific storage patterns, such as local NVMe staging and the data path from storage to GPU. The chapter builds on chapter 1.12, which covers
Middle: The data moves through **queues in host memory**. There is one **admin** queue pair, plus many **I/O submission and completion queue pairs**. The limits are up to 64 thousand queues, each up to 64 thousand entries deep. Linux creates **one queue pair per CPU** through blk-mq. So the CPU that issues an I/O also receives its completion interrupt (MSI-X). Nothing is shared, so there is no lock to wait on. This design is why NVMe scales to millions of IOPS with latency in the tens of microseconds. For comparison, the older SATA queue is 32 deep, and SAS is 256.

(b) Why is VG `ubuntu-vg`, LV `root` named `/dev/mapper/ubuntu--vg-root`? Opening: A node that does not boot is a node that does not train. Many of the nastiest problems on a GPU fleet begin at boot. A kernel update may come up without the NVIDIA module. A Secure Boot setting may quietly refuse an unsigned driver. A firmware setting decides whether GPUs and NICs work as designed, and the list includes IOMMU, Above-4G decoding, SR-IOV and NUMA mode. An `fstab` entry can hang the boot while it waits for an unplugged drive. A cloned image may not find its disks. An `initramfs` without the storage or network driver cannot reach the root filesystem. And when hundreds of diskless nodes PXE-boot at once, they can flatten the DHCP and TFTP servers. Many production procedures also start at boot. You set kernel parameters for hugepages, IOMMU, isolated CPUs and console redirection there. You provision stateless nodes from images at boot time. You also recover nodes out of band over a serial console or BMC.
Middle: The default is set by `id:3:initdefault:`. `init 6` reboots the machine, `init 0` halts it, and `telinit q` re-reads inittab. Actions include `respawn`, which restarts a process if it exits. That is how `getty` login prompts persist. Others are `once`, `wait`, `sysinit`, `boot`, `bootwait`, `initdefault`, `powerwait` (which shuts down on UPS power failure) and `ctrlaltdel`.

Opening: Picture a GPU node that comes back from a reboot but never runs jobs. On a node like this, almost everything that matters is a systemd unit. That list includes `nvidia-persistenced`, `nvidia-fabricmanager` (NVLink/NVSwitch), DCGM, `containerd` or CRI-O, `kubelet`, `slurmd`, `munge`, `sshd`, the node exporter, the mount units for NVMe scratch and network storage, and the timers that trim disks and rotate logs. The ordering between these units, their restart behaviour and their resource isolation decide whether the node comes up working. They also decide whether one runaway job can take the whole node down. The failures are concrete. If `kubelet` or `containerd` starts before the Fabric Manager has initialised NVLink on an HGX system, the scheduler may hand out GPUs that cannot talk to each other over the fast fabric. Distributed jobs then fall back to PCIe silently, or they hang.
Middle: Suppose a weekly trim of a data disk is due on Sunday morning, but the machine was off at that time. A cron job would simply be missed. A timer handles that case, and it gives you more than cron does. Its logs go to the journal with the unit's context. The job runs in a controlled cgroup, with limits and sandboxing. You can order it after other units, such as `After=network-online.target`. With `Persistent=true`, it catches up on missed runs. A randomised delay avoids a thundering herd, and `systemctl list-timers` shows the next and last run.

Opening: Picture a node that will not boot, a training job that has slowed to a crawl, or an on-call page at night. In each case, the first question is what the machine recorded. Three plain-looking services decide whether a cluster can be run at all. Logs come first. They are the only record of what a node did before it failed. With thousands of GPU nodes, logs must live off the node, be searchable, be rate-controlled, and be kept long enough to tie a bad training run to an Xid error, an NCCL timeout, an OOM kill or a switch flap.
Middle: Read `chronyc tracking` first. Its `System time` line gives the current offset, in the form `System time : 0.000012345 seconds fast of NTP time`. On a LAN, an offset of microseconds to low milliseconds is good. Watch `Last offset`, `RMS offset`, `Frequency`, which shows the drift in ppm, and `Leap status: Normal`. A `Stratum` of 16, or the words "Not synchronised", means no usable source exists.

Memory in cgroups, containers and GPU workloads / cgroup v2 memory controller / Pinned (page-locked) memory, `memlock`, and DMA / Host memory budget for a training node / Swap and memory policy summary / How it fails / Hands-on lab: virtual memory, NUMA, huge pages, OOM, and VM drills / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: A GPU server can look fine from the GPU side and still misbehave. The cause is often host memory, the resource that gets the least attention. The GPUs hold the model, but the host's RAM holds the dataset cache, the data-loader workers, pinned staging buffers, the framework's own heap, the page cache of everything read, the container images, and the CUDA driver's mappings. This chapter builds the whole picture, one layer at a time. You will start with virtual memory, paging, demand paging and overcommit, then move on to the page cache and reclaim. From there you will look at the memory hardware: DRAM, caches, MMU, TLB and NUMA. Huge pages and THP come next, followed by allocators and the OOM killer, both system-wide and per cgroup.
Middle: Plan capacity for a training node by adding up its needs. Each data-loader worker may hold a copy of the dataset indexes and decoded batches. Add the pinned staging buffers, the framework overhead, the page cache you want to keep for the dataset, and a margin. Compare that total with `MemAvailable` and the cgroup limits. Then test with the real job and watch `RssAnon` and PSI.
Exercise sample: 1. **Page tables, TLB reach, and huge pages (4 KiB vs 2 MiB vs 1 GiB).** (a) How many 4 KiB page-table entries (`PTE`s) does a 256 GiB host dataset need? Opening: Suppose a training job is slow, and the GPU dashboard shows the GPUs sitting idle. The cause is often on the CPU side. Every training step needs the CPU to decode and augment data, launch kernels, drive NCCL threads, handle interrupts from NICs and NVMe, and run the scheduler and container agents. A GPU that starves because a CPU thread is throttled or badly placed is a very expensive device doing nothing. At the same time, the CPU is shared. Dozens of containers, a kubelet or `slurmd`, monitoring agents and sometimes other tenants all compete for the same cores. The kernel's **scheduler** decides who runs where and when. **Control groups**, or **cgroups** for short, are the mechanism that gives Slurm jobs, systemd services and Kubernetes pods their CPU, memory and I/O shares and limits.
Middle: A **control group**, or **cgroup**, is a kernel mechanism. It groups processes and applies resource accounting and limits to the group as a whole. Where `nice` and `ulimit` act on a single process, a cgroup gives a set of processes a combined budget. These 40 processes together may use at most 4 CPUs and 16 GiB. Cgroups are the basis of container resource limits, such as Docker `--cpus` and `--memory`, and of Kubernetes requests and limits. They also confine Slurm jobs, and systemd uses them for resource control, such as `CPUQuota=` and `MemoryMax=`. Chapter 1.15 covers that. Cgroups are a kernel feature that does not depend on systemd, but on

Opening: Almost every AI workload on a modern cluster runs inside a container. That might be a Kubernetes pod, an Enroot or Pyxis container under Slurm, or a Docker image on a developer's machine. If you do not know what a container is at the kernel level, you will struggle to debug one. Most of the failures below trace back to this chapter: Chapter 1.18 explained how to limit what a process can *use*, with cgroups. This chapter explains how to limit what a process can *see*, with namespaces. Together with a root filesystem, capabilities and seccomp (chapter 1.21), they make up a container. Chapter 1.20 puts them together in a hands-on lab that builds a container by hand.
Middle: A PyTorch job crashes with a bus error, and the logs say nothing about memory. You may suspect the code, but a common cause is the IPC namespace and the size of `/dev/shm`. The four namespaces in this section are smaller than the ones before, but each one still changes what a process sees.

Plain chroot, and why it is not enough / Step 3. Namespaces with unshare / Step 4. Layers with OverlayFS and a proper root switch / Step 5. Put it in a cgroup (CPU, memory, PIDs) / Step 6. Networking / Step 7. Reduce privilege / Step 8. Compare with a real container / Step 9. Break it on purpose / Step 10. Opening: Sooner or later, every engineer who runs containers sees something odd and realizes the only honest answer is "I don't know what Docker is actually doing." This lab removes that moment for you. Chapters 1.18 and 1.19 explained the parts one at a time. Here you pick those parts up and assemble them yourself. The goal is something that behaves like `docker run`. By the end, you will place your hand-made container next to a real one. Start the real one with `docker run` or `ctr run`. Both show the same namespaces, the same cgroup and the same overlay mount. After that, a container is no longer magic. You will also be ready for the classic interview question, "what happens when a pod starts?"
Middle: A container needs four kernel mechanisms working together. The first is **namespaces**: `mnt`, `pid`, `uts`, `ipc`, `net`, `cgroup` and `user`. The second is **cgroups**: `cpu`, `memory`, `pids`, `io` and `devices`. The third is an isolated **root filesystem**, built from `OverlayFS` and `pivot_root`. The fourth is **privilege restrictions**, which include dropped capabilities, `no_new_privs`, `seccomp`, and AppArmor or SELinux. All four run on one shared host kernel. </details>
Exercise sample: Answer these on paper before you rerun any step. Opening: Picture a GPU cluster that holds model weights, proprietary data, and SSH keys that reach hundreds of nodes. Compute on that cluster can also be stolen and used for crypto-mining. That makes it an expensive, shared, high-value target. Hardening helps, but the same hardening can silently destroy performance or break GPU, InfiniBand and container access. A good AI-infrastructure engineer has to do both jobs: harden the node, and know which controls to relax, which to keep, and why. This chapter builds the security model from the kernel's point of view. It starts with users and identity, then setuid and discretionary access control, meaning the owner, group and other file permissions. Next come capabilities, which split root's power into pieces, and seccomp, which filters system calls. Mandatory access control follows with SELinux and AppArmor. The chapter then covers PAM and sudo for authentication, SSH hardening, auditing, kernel and system hardening, Secure Boot, and the AI-cluster-specific adjustments. It connects to containers in chapter 1.19, to kernel modules in chapter 1.22, and to
Middle: Journald and syslog hold authentication logs, in `/var/log/auth.log` or `/var/log/secure`, along with `sudo` I/O logs and the output of `last` and `lastb`. Forward these logs off the node, because an attacker with root deletes local logs.

Opening: Picture a GPU node that comes back from maintenance. It boots, but it has no GPUs. The most fragile single dependency on such a node is the pairing **running kernel ↔ NVIDIA kernel modules ↔ user-space driver libraries ↔ CUDA in the container**. A routine `apt upgrade` can install a new kernel, a reboot can follow maintenance, or a driver version can drift. Any one of these breaks the chain. Slurm then drains the node, Kubernetes shows `nvidia.com/gpu: 0`, and `nvidia-smi` reports that it "couldn't communicate with the NVIDIA driver". Fleet operators therefore treat the kernel and the GPU and NIC drivers as one versioned artefact, and so should you. The modules are not the whole story. The node's behaviour is also shaped by a few hundred **kernel parameters** (`sysctl`) and **boot parameters**. Wrong values cause memory allocation stalls, dropped connections, failing `mmap` calls in data loaders, or swap thrashing. Right values are cheap, repeatable and testable, which is why this chapter spends time on them.
Middle: Start with the self-checking Python lab. You can run it on your own machine, and it checks the logic before you touch a real node. If the lab fails, fix that before you go near a real node. It verifies the NVIDIA kernel module unload dependency order, `vermagic` and Secure Boot MOK signature load eligibility, CUDA `12.x` (`R525`) and `13.x` (`R580`) host driver branch compatibility, `Driver/library version mismatch` detection, and GPU-node `sysctl` auditing.
Exercise sample: Work through these after you read the chapter. They ask you to explain how the pieces fit together, so try to answer in your own words before you check the text. 1. **Kernel Module Mechanics, `vermagic`, `depmod`, and `insmod` vs. `modprobe`:** (a) Where do `.ko` files live on disk for a given kernel release? What happens when `insmod` or `modprobe` encounters a `vermagic` mismatch (`validate_module_load_eligibility()`)? (b) Contrast `insmod` with `modprobe`. A node that benchmarked well last month is now 15% slower. Most performance failures on GPU clusters are not exotic. Without a method, engineers tend to reach for the first explanation that comes to mind. They open `nvidia-smi`, see "GPU-Util 100%" and decide the GPUs are fine. Or they blame the network. Some change a random kernel parameter they found on a forum. Another job scaled from 8 GPUs to 64 GPUs and gained 3x.
Middle: Plot the performance you deliver, such as tokens/s, samples/s or requests/s, against a scaling dimension, such as GPUs, nodes, threads or clients. The shape of the curve tells you what limits you. Learn the shapes first.

Opening: The method chapter (1.23) tells you which question to ask. This chapter gives you the instruments to answer it. Picture a training job that runs far slower than its benchmark. On a GPU node, the cause often sits in a CPU or kernel question that no GPU tool can answer. Which threads burn host CPU launching tiny kernels? Why does a data-loader worker block in the kernel? Where does a process spend time off-CPU, waiting on a lock or a filesystem? Which file reads come from the network filesystem? Why do TCP retransmits spike, which function in the NVIDIA driver is slow, and why does a syscall take 400 ms every few minutes? This chapter explains the tools and, more importantly, their **data sources** and **costs**. Once you know those, you can choose the cheapest tool that answers the question. You also learn what a tool cannot see, and you can avoid disturbing a production workload while you measure.
Middle: For example, a trigger can freeze the buffer the moment a large I/O arrives. Triggers run an action when an event fires. `traceoff` and `snapshot` preserve the buffer around an error condition. `stacktrace` records the call path. `enable_event` chains events together. `hist` builds histograms, which the next subsections cover.

Opening: An AI platform engineer is asked for numbers almost every week. Is this new node healthy? Did the driver upgrade slow training? Which instance type should we buy? Does this vendor's 400 Gb/s fabric really deliver? What tokens/s can one H100 serve at a 200 ms TTFT? What will 1,024 GPUs cost per run? Each answer is a benchmark. Done well, benchmarks catch bad hardware before a customer job runs on it. They validate a cluster acceptance test, size capacity, justify a purchase, and gate regressions in CI. Done badly, they cost millions. You might buy the wrong instance family, accept a cluster with a flaky link, or chase a "regression" that was only thermal throttling. A vendor claim you repeat in a design review may also fail to survive contact with your workload.
Middle: An out-of-the-box PCIe link may be x8 or Gen3, so check `lspci -vv`. The reported line rate is not the same as goodput, because headers take a share. Be careful to compare averaged numbers with steady ones.

Opening: Almost every GPU you rent is attached to a virtual machine, and many on-premises AI platforms run VMs too. You will meet Kubernetes nodes on a hypervisor, GPU passthrough VMs for tenant isolation, KubeVirt for running VMs inside Kubernetes, Kata Containers for stronger pod isolation, development and test clusters on KVM, image-build farms, and CI for drivers and kernels. The people who design, debug and cost these systems need to know what a hypervisor does to CPU, memory, I/O and GPUs. A VM can be as fast as bare metal for compute and still be slow for networking. Why? That is what this chapter explains, along with why vCPU pinning and huge pages matter, why `%steal` appears, why a GPU needs an IOMMU before it can be passed through, and why features such as NVLink fabric management, MIG and P2P behave differently in guests. You may never operate a hypervisor yourself, because cloud providers do that for you. You will still be asked to explain, size and troubleshoot virtualised GPU
Middle: Here is how to read the results, using Gregg's example. A mostly idle guest shows mainly `HLT` exits. Their durations look large, but they cost nothing, because the vCPU is idle. `MSR_WRITE` exits for timer programming, and `EXTERNAL_INTERRUPT` exits, are frequent but cheap. Many `IO_INSTRUCTION` or `MMIO` exits mean heavy emulated device I/O. A histogram of `EPT_VIOLATION` exits shows memory faulting. When the exit rate is high and the exit time is large, the VM is paying a heavy price. Exits that return to user space in QEMU cost more than exits the kernel handles on its own. For deeper views, attach kprobes to KVM exit handlers, such as `handle_halt`.
Exercise sample: 1. Compare emulation with hardware-assisted virtualisation, and Config A with Config B. (a) Contrast software emulation (QEMU TCG) with hardware-assisted virtualisation (KVM plus VT-x or AMD-V). Then contrast Gregg's Config A (native hypervisor plus dom0) with Config B (the kernel-module hypervisor KVM, where each VM is a `qemu-system-x86_64` process and each vCPU is a `CPU n/KVM` thread). (b) Use `classify_kvm_exit_reason()` in `code/part01/kvm_qemu_libvirt.py` to classify `HLT`, `MSR_WRITE` and `IO_INSTRUCTION`. For each one, decide whether it represents idle sleep or active overhead, and whether the host kernel or user-space QEMU handles it.

This is the kind of problem the handbook prepares you for, because it sits where bare-metal GPU clusters meet cloud economics. You will be asked to compare GPU instance types and to decide between virtual machines and bare metal. You will also need to explain why a cloud node behaves differently from an on-premises one. You will diagnose performance in environments where you do not control the hypervisor, and you will size and cost a training run. You will design for neoclouds, such as CoreWeave and Nebius and others, and for hyperscalers alike.
Middle: That is the trade-off to weigh. Cloud operators get the best visibility from containers. End users get it from VMs, because they have kernel access for all tracing tools. Containers with kernel access give everyone full visibility. That holds only when the customer also runs the container host, because there is no isolation between containers in that case. If you need to trace the kernel as a tenant, choose a VM or microVM over a container.

This chapter is about the work that keeps such a machine fast: making a Linux server with GPUs deliver the hardware's full capability, proving it, and keeping it that way across a fleet. This is the capstone of Part 1. Every earlier chapter contributes a piece: processes, memory and NUMA, scheduling and cgroups, namespaces, security, kernel modules and ``sysctl``, methods, tracing, benchmarking and virtualisation. Here they all come together in one practical task, and you will meet that task again and again in your career. **Take a Linux server with GPUs, make it deliver the hardware's capability predictably, prove it, and keep it that way across a fleet.**
Middle: PyTorch allocates GPU memory only as needed. Its caching allocator keeps freed blocks for reuse instead of returning them to the OS. Watch the usage with `nvidia-smi` or the NVML APIs during training and inference. When you get close to the limit, reduce the batch size, use activation checkpointing for training, or reduce precision.

Opening: Chapter 1.5 teaches you to *work* remotely. This chapter is the **reference and depth companion** you come back to when you configure a cluster. It covers every important SSH option on both sides, and it shows how the protocol proves identity. It also shows how to run one command on hundreds of nodes without flooding your network, and how to reach a machine whose operating system is dead. Read chapter 1.5 first. Picture a GPU cluster with hundreds to thousands of nodes and only a handful of people to run it. The same job, such as a driver check, a mount repair or a process hunt, has to happen on every machine, and quickly. The machines that most need attention are exactly the ones that stop answering SSH. Three skills get you through this. You configure SSH precisely, deciding who may log in, from where and with what. You fan commands out in parallel without overloading the head node or the network. And you reach a console through the baseboard
Middle: `tmux` is similar, but it is more flexible. Its most valuable feature is starting a new window directly with a command. The default hotkey is `Ctrl-b`, and you can reconfigure it in `~/.tmux.conf`. To imitate `screen -xRR`, use `tmux new -A -s 0`. That attaches if the session exists, and creates it otherwise.

You need to know which tool fits, and why none of them works for every case. This chapter covers every way to copy and share files between Linux, Windows and cloud systems. It also covers how users reach a cluster remotely, including graphical programs when a command line is not enough. Data moves all the time in AI work. Code goes out to nodes, datasets come in, checkpoints go out, and results return to a laptop. The tool you pick decides whether a 2 TB transfer takes an hour or a week. It also decides whether a deleted directory can be recovered, and whether a home directory is available on 1,000 nodes.
Middle: Windows users need different tools. PuTTY works well. Use `puttygen` to create and convert keys, which are saved in `.ppk` format, and use the `putty` client. Load the `.ppk` key, then enter the cluster address. For file transfer, use WinSCP or its plug-in for FAR. FileZilla also works, but it needs the PuTTY agent loaded with the key, because it does not otherwise support key authentication.

### part-02-networking/01-what-happens-when-you-curl-a-url-a-packet-s-journey-end-to-end.md
Title: 2.1 What happens when you curl a URL: a packet's journey end to end | words 8586 | code blocks 12
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. The whole journey in one picture / 2. Step 0: the process and the URL / 3. Step 1: name resolution (what is the IP address?) / 4. Step 2: the socket, the route and the source address / 5. Step 3: from IP to Ethernet (ARP or NDP) and out of the NIC / 6. Step 4: the switch, the router, NAT and the Internet / 7. Step 5: TCP connection establishment / 8. Step 6: TLS handshake (HTTPS) / 9. Step 7: the HTTP request and response / 10. Step 8: transfer, acknowledgements and teardown / 11. Timing: reading `curl -w` / 12. A map from symptom to layer / 13. The same journey in AI infrastructure / 14. Hands-on lab: trace one request end to end / 15. Common mistakes / 16. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Almost every network problem you will ever debug in an AI cluster is a question about *where in a path something went wrong*. Picture a few of them. A container image does not pull. A training job cannot reach the dataset bucket. `torchrun` hangs at rendezvous. NCCL falls back from InfiniBand to sockets. A node cannot be reached by SSH after a switch change. A pod resolves a name to the wrong address. Each of these is a failure at one particular step of a journey. That journey starts in a process and ends in another process on another machine. Start by naming the step that failed. Interview loops ask the classic question "what happens when you type a URL into a browser, or `curl` it?" A candidate who can narrate that journey in detail, with the commands that prove each step, has the mental model for everything in this Part. That model covers name resolution, routing, ARP/ND, switching, NAT, TCP, TLS, HTTP and teardown.
Middle: In step 2, the server replies with a **ServerHello**, its key share and its certificate chain, followed by **Finished**. The server chooses the parameters and proves its identity with an X.509 certificate chain that runs leaf → intermediate → root. It signs the handshake in a CertificateVerify message. Both sides then derive the session keys, and ephemeral Diffie-Hellman gives forward secrecy. In TLS 1.3 this costs one round trip before application data can flow. TLS 1.2 needs two round trips. 0-RTT resumption can send data earlier, but it has replay caveats.
Exercise sample: Work through these six exercises in order. Each one uses the commands from the earlier sections. 1. Opening: Think about the terms you will meet in an AI cluster. RoCE, InfiniBand, VXLAN, EVPN, ECMP, PFC, MTU, MSS, GRE, NAT and CNI each name something at a particular place in a stack. Once you can say which layer a performance or reliability problem lives at, and what that layer is responsible for, the problem becomes manageable. The vocabulary of layers is also the shared language of network engineers. When a switch engineer says "that's an L2 problem" and a platform engineer says "the pod can't reach the service", you need to translate between them in your head. This chapter builds the foundation for that translation.
Middle: In traditional networks, the control plane is distributed. Every router runs the protocol and computes its own table. A RIB holds the candidate routes, and the best ones go into the FIB. In SDN, a centralised controller computes and installs the forwarding state instead. OpenFlow was the first widely known interface for this.

Title: 2.3 Ethernet and L2: frames, MAC addresses, switching, ARP and NDP | words 10738 | code blocks 13
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Ethernet in one page / 2. MAC addresses / 3. The Ethernet frame / Frame types you will see on a network / 4. L2 on Linux: the objects behind the concepts / 8. Wi-Fi (802.11) in contrast / Connecting Wi-Fi from the command line / 9. AI fabrics at layer 2 / 10. Failure modes and diagnosis / 11. Hands-on lab: a switched L2 network in network namespaces / 12. Common mistakes / 13. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: A training job that ran at 200 Gb/s when it should have run at 400 Gb/s does not look broken. It looks slow. The cause often sits below IP, in the link layer. A transceiver with rising bit errors can turn into NCCL timeouts. A switch port can flap. Two NICs on one subnet can answer ARP for each other's addresses after a careless clone. Each of these faults starts at layer 2 and shows up much higher, as a timeout or a slow step. A broadcast storm after a cabling mistake, jumbo frames set on the hosts but not on one switch, and a lossless RDMA class that is not actually lossless all follow the same pattern. Cloud networks emulate Ethernet semantics too. ARP still exists inside a VPC, and the switch there is software.
Middle: An ARP entry can go stale when an IP address moves to a different network card. Each card has its own MAC address, so the cache may still point at the old card. Linux invalidates idle entries after a while, so a small delay is the usual result. If you need the entry gone now, run `ip neigh del host dev interface`, with the IP address as `host` and the interface name as `interface`.

Opening: Think about the address plan before any cable goes in. You decide how many subnets each rack gets, how large the Kubernetes pod and service ranges will be, and how the rails are numbered. The rails are the per-GPU back-end networks. You also decide which ranges must never overlap with the corporate network, the VPN or a peered cloud VPC. Finally, you leave room to grow from 64 to 4,096 nodes without renumbering. A wrong choice shows up months later, and it usually appears as an error message. You might see "pod CIDR overlaps with the on-prem network", "we ran out of IPs in the node subnet", "GKE cluster cannot be created: secondary range too small", or "VPC peering rejected because ranges overlap". Subnetting is also a core interview skill. You are expected to do it on a whiteboard without a calculator. It is equally a daily debugging tool. When a packet goes missing, you ask whether 10.20.37.130 sits inside 10.20.36.0/22, or what the broadcast of 172.16.5.77/27 is.
Middle: The IPv6 header is a fixed 40 bytes. It has no checksum, because the link and transport layers cover errors. Its base header has no fragmentation fields. Fragmentation uses an extension header, and only the source host may do it. The Traffic Class byte holds DSCP and ECN, as the IPv4 byte does. The 20-bit flow label helps routers keep a flow on one path when ECMP spreads traffic. A fixed header keeps hardware parsing simple, and extension headers keep the design flexible. The hop limit plays the role of the TTL.

Opening: Picture a storage mount that goes over the management network and saturates it. The fault sits in the routing table, not in the storage. A GPU server is a router in miniature. It has a routing table that decides which NIC every packet leaves by, and a default gateway for the world beyond its subnet. It often has several NICs on several networks: management, front-end, storage, and one NIC per GPU for the back-end fabric. In Kubernetes it also forwards packets for pods. Misunderstood host routing causes many baffling failures. SSH works over one NIC and not another. NCCL picks the wrong interface. A second default route silently takes over. Pod traffic cannot leave the node. Replies leave through a different NIC than the request arrived on, which is called asymmetric routing, and a reverse-path filter drops them. A stale static route sends traffic into a black hole after a network change.
Middle: If you want flows spread across your links, the hash policy is the first setting to check. It decides what the kernel hashes. Policy `0` hashes the source and destination IP. Policy `1` hashes the 5-tuple. Policy `2` hashes the inner L3 header of encapsulated traffic, and policy `3` uses custom fields. The `fib_multipath_use_neigh` setting skips next hops whose neighbour has failed.
Exercise sample: 1. Start with the routing table and longest-prefix match. Opening: Picture a cross-region checkpoint copy that runs far slower than the link should allow. Or picture an inference API that is fast most of the time, then stalls for no obvious reason. Both problems are TCP problems, and you will meet them often. Almost everything outside the GPU fabric rides on TCP. SSH sessions, HTTPS, the Kubernetes API and container image pulls all use it. So do object-store downloads of datasets and checkpoints, the control paths of NFS and many parallel filesystems, inference APIs over gRPC and HTTP, and metrics scraping. NCCL depends on TCP too, for its bootstrap and as a fallback socket transport.
Middle: The first lines show the current limits. The commented lines show the maxima to raise on long, fast paths. Setting `SO_RCVBUF` in an application disables autotuning for that socket, and that is almost always a mistake. Huge buffers on short-RTT paths do not help. They add memory pressure and queueing delay, which is bufferbloat. Size the buffers for the paths you actually have. Memory is the other limit. A 128 MB buffer on each of thousands of connections exhausts RAM, and `tcp_mem` guards against that. Choose buffer sizes from the BDP of the path you use, and leave autotuning on.
Exercise sample: Work through these six exercises after reading the chapter. Each one pairs a calculation with an explanation in your own words. 1. Start with transport problems and flow control. Use chapters 2 and 3 of *Computer Networking Problems and Solutions* as your reference. (a) Contrast the three digital marshaling grammars: fixed-length fields, Type-Length-Value (TLV), and shared schemas or dictionaries. Then say where TCP uses the first two. (b) Distinguish flow control (`rwnd`) from congestion control (`cwnd`). State the formula for the most bytes a TCP sender may have in flight

Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. UDP: the minimal transport / 2. ICMP: the network layer's error and diagnostic channel / 3. ping / Reading ping output / 4. traceroute, tracepath and mtr / 5. MTU, MSS, fragmentation and Path MTU Discovery / The PMTU black hole / Encapsulation overhead (compute, don't guess) / Jumbo frames for AI clusters / 6. More tools / 7. A layered reachability checklist / 8. Hands-on lab: UDP, ICMP, traceroute, and an MTU black hole / 9. Common mistakes / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: A training job has hung, or an inference endpoint has suddenly slowed down. The first question is whether the network is the problem, and if it is, where. A small toolkit built on UDP and ICMP answers that. You use `ping` to check reachability and latency. You use `traceroute` or `mtr` to see the path and the loss at each hop. For path MTU, `tracepath` and `ping -M do` do the work, and for UDP behaviour you reach for `nc -u` and `iperf3 -u`. UDP also carries traffic you depend on every day. It is the carrier for DNS, VXLAN and Geneve overlays, WireGuard, QUIC and RoCE v2, so understanding it helps when you debug the fabric. A large share of outages look the same way: small requests work, but large ones hang. Many of those are **path MTU** problems in overlays, VPNs and clouds, where blocked ICMP stops Path MTU Discovery from working. When a slow job lands on your desk, decide early whether you are looking at reachability,
Middle: To detect a black hole, start with a step-down test. Try `ping -M do -s 1472 host && echo ok`. Then step down through 1452, 1432, 1400 and so on until you find the largest size that passes.
Exercise sample: These exercises check the ideas in the chapter. Some ask for a calculation, and others ask you to explain a failure in your own words. 1. Start with UDP semantics. State the size of the UDP header, `8` bytes, and name its four fields (RFC 768). Explain how UDP keeps message boundaries, unlike TCP. Then name the `nstat` counter that goes up when a receiver's UDP socket buffer overflows. Finally, explain how a sender learns that a remote UDP port has no listener, and list the metrics `iperf3 -u` reports

Opening: When you face a cluster that is slow for no obvious reason, check DNS first. It is often the cause. DNS is the first dependency of nearly everything, and it is one of the most common hidden causes of outages. "The cluster is slow" can turn out to be a 5-second DNS timeout on every call. "Pods cannot reach the registry" can mean CoreDNS is overloaded, or that `ndots:5` expands every lookup into six queries. A multi-node job may fail to start because its node names do not resolve, or resolve to the management NIC rather than the fabric NIC. A cloud endpoint may change its IP while a long-lived process keeps the old one. DNS also underpins service discovery, including Kubernetes services, headless services for StatefulSets, Slurm node names and NCCL/MPI hostfiles. It supports certificate validation, which relies on names, and traffic steering with GeoDNS, weighted records and anycast resolvers.
Middle: Use configuration management, such as Ansible from the Part 4 chapters, to generate `/etc/hosts` for tiny bootstrap sets. The same tool can render zone files or cloud DNS records from inventory, so DNS never drifts from reality.
Exercise sample: 1. Start with the hierarchy. First, explain the difference between a DNS domain and a DNS zone. Then explain why a parent zone must include glue `A` and `AAAA` records when it delegates `example.com.` to `ns1.example.com.`. Last, trace the messages exchanged between an application, its local stub resolver, a recursive resolver, and the `.` (root), `.com` (TLD) and `example.com` authoritative servers on a cold cache. 2. ### part-02-networking/09-dhcp-nat-and-pat.md
Title: 2.9 DHCP, NAT and PAT | words 7304 | code blocks 7
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. DHCP: automatic configuration / DORA / Relays, scopes and options / PXE boot of bare-metal GPU nodes / Linux clients and servers / DHCPv6, SLAAC and IPv6 / Hardening and diagnostics / 2. Private addressing and why NAT exists / 3. Types of NAT / How PAT works / 4. NAT on Linux / Container and Kubernetes NAT / Cloud NAT / Routers that run Linux: OpenWRT and BusyBox / 5. NAT and the fabric: where NAT does not belong / 6. Troubleshooting / 7. Hands-on lab: DHCP server and NAT gateway with namespaces / 8. Common mistakes / 9. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a GPU node that will not boot after its rack is re-cabled. Before it can load anything, it has to ask the network for help. Bare-metal GPU nodes are provisioned by network boot. The node's BMC and NIC send DHCP requests, and a provisioning server (Base Command Manager, MAAS, Foreman or Ironic) answers with an address plus PXE/iPXE boot options. The whole fleet comes up through a DHCP/TFTP/HTTP chain, so one broken link stalls every node that depends on it. NAT is the other half of the story. It is how private cluster nodes reach package mirrors and registries through a gateway. It is how cloud VMs with no public IP get outbound access (Cloud NAT, NAT gateway). It is also how Kubernetes services and Docker publish ports, using iptables DNAT and masquerade.
Middle: You have two fixes. You can also SNAT, or masquerade, such traffic on the way, using `-s 10.0.0.0/24 -d 10.0.0.20 -j MASQUERADE`. Or you can use split DNS, so inside clients resolve the server's inside address. Split DNS is usually the better choice.
Exercise sample: 1. Start with DHCPv4 lease timers (T1/T2), relays and Option 82. If you are new to DHCP, read the DORA exchange first. (a) Trace the four DHCPv4 DORA messages (UDP 68 -> 67). Explain why DISCOVER and REQUEST stay broadcast (255.255.255.255) even after the client receives an OFFER. (b) Use `compute_dhcp_lease_timers_sec()` in `code/part02/dhcp_nat_pat.py` to compute T1 (50%) and T2 (87.5%) for a 12-hour (43,200 s) lease. Then explain how a router DHCP relay (`ip helper-address`, `giaddr` and Option 82) lets one central DHCP server serve dozens of routed rack VLANs.

Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture an inference endpoint that goes down at 3 a.m. because its certificate has expired. That is a common page, because nearly every control-plane and serving interaction in an AI platform runs as HTTP over TLS. Model-serving endpoints (REST and gRPC over HTTP/2), the Kubernetes API server, container registries, artifact and dataset stores, MLflow and experiment trackers, NGC and Hugging Face downloads, webhooks, Prometheus scrapes and Redfish BMC APIs all work this way. Other failures in this layer are just as familiar. A kubelet cannot join the cluster when its client certificate rotated incorrectly. Behind a corporate proxy, `x509: certificate signed by unknown authority` can block image pulls. HTTP/2 head-of-line blocking or keepalive timeouts can degrade token streaming. An mTLS mesh can reject calls because of clock skew.
Middle: Certificate Transparency (CT) requires public CAs to log every issued certificate in append-only public logs. Monitor those logs for unexpected certificates issued for your domains. CAA DNS records restrict which CAs may issue certificates for a domain.
Exercise sample: 1. If you run an LLM inference gateway, start with status codes and protocol versions. Finally, explain why connection keep-alive pools must align with load-balancer idle timeouts (`60–350 s`).

Opening: Suppose a GPU cluster runs slowly while the servers look healthy. The problem may sit in the network, because a GPU cluster is mostly switches. To reason about latency, buffering, drops and microbursts, you need to know how a switch moves a frame. VLANs divide one physical network into several logical ones. Management, storage, provisioning and tenant networks can share the same cabling. Trunks carry those VLANs to the servers, and the server NIC often carries tagged traffic to a bridge or to Kubernetes CNIs. Spanning Tree prevents a miswired cable from melting the network with a broadcast storm.
Middle: Explicit hellos are used by OSPF, IS-IS, EIGRP, LDP, LLDP and LACP. Each sends periodic multicast hello packets that carry capabilities and identifiers. A neighbour is declared dead when no hello arrives within the dead interval. Centralised registration works differently. Devices register with a controller or service, which then tells the others about them. Examples are SDN controllers, service registries and the Kubernetes API for nodes. The service itself must be discoverable.

### part-02-networking/12-link-aggregation-lacp-and-mlag.md
Title: 2.12 Link aggregation, LACP, MLAG and ECMP | words 8035 | code blocks 3
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. The problem: many parallel links, one conversation / Hash functions and why balance is imperfect / 2. Link aggregation and LACP / 3. Linux bonding / 4. Multi-chassis link aggregation (MLAG, vPC, EVPN multihoming) / 5. Routed parallel links: ECMP / 6. Cluster roles and networks (a practical map) / 7. Failure behaviour and troubleshooting / 8. Hands-on lab: build and break bonds and ECMP / 9. Common mistakes / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a GPU server whose storage connection goes dark because the switch it plugs into reboots. A GPU server needs bandwidth and resilience at the same time. A management or storage connection must survive a switch reboot. A leaf pair must be upgradable without draining racks, and every parallel link in the fabric should carry traffic. The first tool is link aggregation, built from bonding and LACP on the host and at the first hop. Multi-chassis variants such as MLAG, vPC and EVPN multihoming stretch the same idea across two switches. ECMP at layer 3 spreads traffic across the spines.
Middle: The third detail concerns elephant flows. Standard ECMP cannot split one flow, so a large flow stays on one path. Several techniques work around that. Flowlet switching re-hashes when a gap in the flow exceeds the path-delay difference, so reordering is impossible. Packet spraying works with reordering-tolerant endpoints. Adaptive routing lets the switch pick the least-loaded path per packet or flowlet, as in InfiniBand AR and NVIDIA Spectrum-X for Ethernet. Host-side multipathing uses multiple connections or QPs with varied ports, which is what NCCL and MPTCP do. These techniques are central to AI fabric performance, covered in chapter 2.19 and Part 11.
Exercise sample: You can answer most of these on paper, using the chapter as your reference. 1. Start with why per-packet round robin fails for TCP, and why flow hashing and hash polarization matter. Explain why `xmit_hash_policy=layer2` pins all 16 parallel TCP streams destined for an off-subnet NFS server onto a single bond member, while `layer3+4` spreads

### part-02-networking/13-ip-routing-static-routes-dynamic-routing-and-convergence.md
Title: 2.13 IP routing: static routes, dynamic routing and convergence | words 9047 | code blocks 6
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Static versus dynamic routing / 2. What a control plane does / Learning reachable destinations / Distribution models / 3. Choosing a protocol (preview) / 8. Hands-on: routing on Linux with FRR / 9. Hands-on lab: static routes, distance-vector failure, link state, and convergence / 10. Common mistakes / 11. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a leaf-spine data centre where a link fails in the middle of a large training run. How fast the routers send traffic another way decides how much work is lost. Every large AI fabric is routed. Leaf-spine data centres run BGP or OSPF between switches, and increasingly between switches and hosts. Because every link stays active under ECMP, a failure is absorbed in milliseconds. Spanning tree, the older approach, needs tens of seconds. To operate, design or debug such a fabric, you need to answer four questions. How do routers learn where things are, and how do they avoid forwarding loops? How do they notice failures, and how quickly do they converge? What happens when two routing systems are joined, which is called redistribution? And which behaviour belongs to one protocol, while which is fundamental to all of them?
Middle: BFD runs in asynchronous mode, with continuous packets, and it can add an optional echo mode. A session must be verified in both directions. The packets carry what the sender sees, which is the two-way check from chapter 2.11. Aggressive timers and many sessions load the control CPUs. If congestion drops BFD packets, the link can flap and cause outages, so prioritise BFD in QoS.
Exercise sample: Work through these to check that you can trace and write the ideas, not just recall them. Where you can, check your answers against the lab script in section 9. 1. Start with static versus dynamic routing, administrative distance (`AD`) and floating static routes. First, explain when static routing is the better choice. Then explain how BFD tracking and floating static routes overcome the blackhole failure mode of static routing. Second, list the default Cisco and FRR administrative distance values for connected, static, eBGP, EIGRP, OSPF, IS-IS, RIP and iBGP.

Opening: Picture an AI cluster where one rack suddenly cannot reach the others. Training jobs stall, and the first question is which router has the wrong view of the network. In many AI data centres, the **underlay** is the routed network that gives every switch loopback and link reachability. That underlay is often OSPF, IS-IS or eBGP. You will be asked to read an OSPF neighbour table. You may need to explain why an adjacency is stuck in EXSTART, which is usually an MTU problem. You will also design areas, explain DR and BDR elections on a shared segment, and compare IS-IS with OSPF for a new fabric. This chapter explains the protocols that implement the algorithms of chapter 2.13, and it prepares you for those tasks. It covers how Spanning Tree, RIP and EIGRP work as the distance-vector family, and how OSPF and IS-IS form neighbours, flood reliably and calculate paths. Configuration and troubleshooting are shown in FRR.
Middle: 1. Hello comes first. The neighbours must agree on hello and dead intervals, area ID, and network type and subnet mask on multi-access links. They must also agree on authentication, stub flags and MTU. The MTU appears in DBD packets. A mismatch leaves the adjacency stuck in ExStart or Exchange. 2. Two-way is confirmed when each router sees its own ID in the neighbour's list of neighbours seen. On broadcast networks, only the DR and BDR reach Full with everyone. Other pairs stay in 2-Way. 3. In ExStart and Exchange, the master and slave are chosen. The DBDs summarise each database, with headers that carry the LS age, ID, advertising

Opening: Suppose you need to know how a Kubernetes pod address reaches the rest of the network. The protocol that does that advertising is BGP. BGP is also the protocol that connects the Internet, and cloud providers use it at their edges. Direct Connect, Cloud Interconnect and ExpressRoute all rely on it. Since RFC 7938, it has carried most hyperscale and AI data-centre fabrics too. Its reach extends inside the cluster. Bare-metal GPU nodes can run routed to the top-of-rack switch, using ECMP across both uplinks. Calico, Cilium, MetalLB and kube-vip use BGP to advertise pod and service addresses. EVPN uses it to distribute MAC/IP reachability, which chapter 2.17 covers.
Middle: Hot-potato routing hands traffic to the neighbour at the nearest exit, which is the cheapest choice for you. You achieve it through the IGP cost step, or by setting LOCAL_PREF on the nearest peers. Cold-potato routing does the opposite. It carries traffic across your own network as far as possible and exits near the destination. Content providers use it for tighter control of the user experience. You achieve it with high LOCAL_PREF on routes at the far exit, by honouring MED from neighbours, or by selective advertisement.

Opening: Picture a 1,000-GPU training job that should run at line rate but does not. The routing design of the fabric decides whether it can. The same design controls how the network reacts when a link or switch fails. It also decides whether tenants stay isolated, and how quickly you can add a rack. Cloud operators have settled on a small set of practices. They build a Clos topology and run eBGP as the only protocol, with a deliberately simple ASN plan. They use ECMP everywhere and leave links unnumbered. Their route policy limits how far each prefix travels.
Middle: Across groups, the spines link to each other. SA1 and SA2 are interconnected, and SA1 connects to SB2 and SC1. SA2 connects to SB2 and SC2. SC1 and SC2 are interconnected, and SC1 connects to SB1. SB1 and SB2 are interconnected, and SB1 connects to SA2, SC1, and SC2. Those cross-group links are the part that the routing protocol must understand.

Overlays for AI clusters and containers / 6. Segment routing / SR-MPLS / SRv6 / Signalling and control / 7. SD-WAN / 8. Complexity and virtualization / 9. Hands-on lab: tunnels, VXLAN, and EVPN / 10. Common mistakes / 11. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a multi-tenant GPU cloud where several customers share one physical fabric. Each customer must see only its own network, and overlays make that possible. An overlay turns one physical fabric into many logical networks. A GPU cloud isolates customers with VXLAN and EVPN VRFs. Kubernetes CNIs (Flannel, Calico VXLAN or IPIP, Cilium and Antrea) build pod networks as overlays. Cross-site links run over WireGuard, IPsec or SD-WAN, and segment routing and SRv6 are being proposed to steer AI traffic over chosen paths. The trade-off is that an overlay hides the physical topology from tenants, and it also hides tenant traffic from the physical network. That is powerful and dangerous at once. MTU shrinks, ECMP hashing loses entropy, and troubleshooting needs two views, one of the overlay and one of the underlay. A failure in the underlay can show up as mysterious loss in the overlay.
Middle: Labels come from the SR Global Block (SRGB). They are distributed through the IGP, as IS-IS or OSPF extensions, or through BGP-LS. Take the stack [103,104,202,105,106,109,110]. Each hop pops the top label that names it or its adjacency, then forwards the packet. ASICs can recycle packets, which makes multi-label processing possible.

Opening: Picture a design review where someone asks why the back-end network is built the way it is. You need a real answer, and it starts with the topology. The topology is the one decision that is hard to change after racks are cabled. It fixes how many GPUs a cluster can hold and the latency between any two of them. It also sets how much bandwidth survives a failure, whether collectives run at line rate or stall behind a hot link, and what the whole thing costs in switches, optics and cables. NVIDIA, Meta, Google, Microsoft and others publish reference designs, such as rail-optimised leaf-spine, multi-plane Clos and dragonfly variants. You need to read them, size them in ports, switches and oversubscription, and then defend or critique them in a design review or interview. This chapter follows that path. It starts with general network design patterns and what makes a network a fabric. Next come the leaf-spine and Clos scaling math, and oversubscription with contention. After that it covers the
Middle: The one-dimensional form can also build a rail topology. Each server's NIC1 connects to leaf1, NIC2 to leaf2, and so on for every server. The leaves link to each other in a shared ring, which carries inter-rail traffic in place of spines. To connect many rails, a two-dimensional or three-dimensional torus fits better.

Opening: Distributed training sends synchronized bursts at line rate between thousands of NICs. Even in a 1:1 non-oversubscribed fabric, several senders can target one receiver port at the same instant. That event is called incast. Two elephant flows, which are very large transfers, can also land on one link through a hash collision. A single dropped RoCE packet can stall a whole collective. RoCEv2 relies on a nearly lossless Ethernet. The gap between 95% and 40% of line-rate all-reduce comes down to how well the network uses queueing, classification, ECN marking, Priority Flow Control and DCQCN. Interviewers tend to ask about the details. Why can PFC cause head-of-line blocking and storms? Why do ECN thresholds sit below PFC thresholds? What does a PFC watchdog do? Which DSCP and priority should a RoCE class use?
Middle: Deployments differ on where PFC runs. Some configure it only on leaves and NICs, and others configure it on all nodes. Some run ECN-only, with no PFC, and rely on good load balancing and NIC loss recovery. The ECN-only option depends especially on newer NICs that tolerate loss. The right answer depends on the SLA, the hardware and your measurements.
