Opening: Suppose an inference endpoint is slow while most of its GPUs sit idle. The cause can sit in the load balancer, the layer that spreads requests across the GPUs. A generic round-robin balancer in front of long-lived gRPC or Server-Sent-Event token streams can leave one GPU at 100% and nine idle. Round robin deals requests in turn and ignores how long each one runs. Other failures look different but share the same root. A proxy reload can sever hour-long generations. An HTTP/2 connection pinned to one backend turns a balanced cluster into a hot spot. A health check that only tests TCP routes traffic to a server whose GPU has an Xid error.
Middle: In Kubernetes, the details sit in Part 5. A Service (ClusterIP, NodePort, LoadBalancer or Headless) gives a stable virtual IP. kube-proxy implements it with iptables rules (a random probability chain), with IPVS (a real L4 scheduler), or with an eBPF replacement such as Cilium. EndpointSlices list the ready pods, and readiness probes control membership. Ingress and Gateway API provide L7 HTTP routing and TLS through controllers. Those controllers include nginx, HAProxy, Envoy-based Contour, Emissary and Envoy Gateway, Traefik and cloud controllers. Service meshes add per-pod proxies for mTLS, retries and L7 balancing between services. MetalLB, kube-vip and Cilium provide LoadBalancer VIPs on bare metal, using ARP or BGP (ECMP).
Exercise sample: 1. Compare the load balancer families. Opening: What would an attacker want from a GPU cluster? The answer is more than one thing, and that is why these clusters draw attention. Compute is valuable on its own, because it can be used for cryptomining or resold. Data is valuable too, including training sets, model weights and customer prompts. Privileged access paths such as BMCs, schedulers, registries and CI let an attacker reach everything else. The networks are unusual as well. Flat, high-speed RDMA fabrics bypass the host kernel. Management planes use out-of-band controllers that run their own firmware. Multi-tenant environments let tenants share switches. Fast-moving software stacks, such as drivers, container runtimes and serving frameworks, bring a steady stream of CVEs.
Middle: There are two main types of vulnerability: direct attacks and cleartext password sniffing. A direct attack tries to take over a machine without subtlety. A common start is an unprotected service, such as an administrator account with no password. Once an intruder controls one service, the intruder can try to compromise the whole system.

Opening: A GPU server can lose its network in the middle of a training run. When that happens, the first questions are always the same. Which interface is up, which address does it hold, which route does traffic take, which sockets are open, which counters are moving, and at which layer are packets being dropped? This chapter gives you the Linux operator's toolkit for answering those questions quickly. It covers the kernel's view of interfaces, the `ip` command family, `ss` for sockets, and `ethtool`, `nstat`, `sar` and `nicstat` for counters. It also covers the network configuration managers (NetworkManager, systemd-networkd, Netplan, cloud-init) that decide what the interfaces look like at boot. Then it turns to the socket API behind every application, a performance methodology (tools method, USE, workload characterization, latency analysis, TCP analysis), and eBPF-based tools for deeper visibility. Keep those questions in mind as you read, because each section answers one of them. Later chapters cover the filtering layer (2.23), policy routing, bonding and tuning (2.24), namespaces and containers (2.25), packet
Middle: Read each counter by its type. **Errors** with CRC, frame or length labels point to the physical layer, such as a cable, the optics, FEC, or a speed or duplex mismatch. **Dropped**, **missed** and **no_buffer** counters mean the host could not keep up. The fixes there are larger rings, more RSS queues, correct IRQ affinity, lower per-packet cost, more CPU, or XDP. Softnet `dropped` and `time_squeeze` point to softirq starvation, which you tune with `net.core.netdev_budget` and `netdev_max_backlog`, or with RPS and RFS. TCP retransmits point to loss somewhere on the path. Compare them with a baseline, because Internet-facing flows retransmit more than rack-local ones. `ListenOverflows` means the accept queue is

Opening: Imagine a node that looks healthy. Its CPUs are idle, its bandwidth is spare, and yet new connections simply stop working. The cause is often in netfilter, the kernel framework that inspects and filters packets. Almost every packet on a Kubernetes node passes through it. Many components add rules to that same path. kube-proxy installs thousands of NAT rules, and container runtimes add masquerade and port-publish rules. CNIs add policy chains, cloud agents add their own, and the host firewall sits underneath all of them.
Middle: Some traffic never reaches netfilter at all. Cilium attaches eBPF programs at the XDP and TC hooks, so it performs load balancing and policy before netfilter. It can run many flows without conntrack, because it keeps its own maps. For GPU and RDMA traffic, the fast path uses the NIC and bypasses the kernel stack entirely, as RoCE does. Host netfilter never sees that traffic, so apply isolation at the switch or the NIC instead (chapter 2.21).

Opening: A GPU server is a multi-homed machine. It has several NICs, and each one sits on a different network: management, front end, storage and compute rails. The hardware can reach a 100 to 800 Gb/s capability, but a default Linux configuration will not deliver it. Collective communication, checkpoint writes and dataset reads then expose every inefficiency in the packet path. Getting the last 50% of bandwidth and the last 50 microseconds of latency means understanding how packets leave and enter the host. You need to know which routing table applies to which source address. You also need to know how interrupts and queues map to cores and NUMA nodes, which offloads are on, how big the rings and buffers are, what the MTU is end to end, and which TCP knobs matter.
Middle: Most TCP defaults are sane, because the stack autotunes buffers for you. Measure first, and change a setting only when a number tells you to. Tune the stack for your bandwidth-delay product, your connection rates and your memory. Chapter 2.6 explains the mechanisms behind each one.

Opening: Picture a training pod that cannot reach the parameter server. Or picture a job that runs, but where NCCL keeps choosing `eth0` instead of the RDMA interface. Both failures come from the networking that Kubernetes builds around each container. Every containerised GPU workload gets its networking from a handful of kernel primitives. These are a network namespace per pod or container, a veth pair into the host, a bridge or routed path, and NAT or an overlay between nodes. When a training pod cannot reach the parameter server, or when a CNI upgrade breaks pod-to-pod traffic, you debug these primitives. CNI is the plugin system that wires up pod networking.
Middle: Traffic from a pod to a `ClusterIP` goes through kube-proxy, using iptables or IPVS, or through eBPF. These DNAT the traffic to a backend pod, as chapters 2.20 and 2.23 explain. Traffic from a pod to the outside is masqueraded, meaning SNAT to the node IP, unless pod IPs are routable and `ipMasq` is off. External traffic reaches pods through NodePort, LoadBalancer or Ingress.

### part-02-networking/26-packet-capture-from-scratch-tcpdump-tshark-and-wireshark.md

Opening: Counters tell you *that* something is wrong. Packets tell you *what*. When a connection resets or a rail goes quiet, you can settle the argument with a capture. Did the SYN leave the host? Did the reply come back? Was the MSS clamped, and did a middlebox strip window scaling? Who sent the RST? You can also check whether PFC pause frames are flooding a rail, whether RoCE traffic carries the right DSCP, and whether the CNP was generated. GPU clusters make this harder. You cannot take a capture on a 100 or 400 Gb/s link with a naive `tcpdump`. RDMA traffic bypasses the kernel entirely. Containers hide their interfaces in namespaces, and overlays wrap each packet twice. This chapter shows how capture works in Linux and how to write precise capture filters. It covers `tcpdump`, `tshark` and Wireshark, including TLS decryption and VXLAN and RoCE decoding. It also explains capture at scale, from containers and from switches. Last, it shows how eBPF tools such as BCC and bpftrace complement
Middle: BCC, the BPF Compiler Collection, gives you a Python front end with BPF programs written in embedded C. It also comes with many ready-made tools under `/usr/share/bcc/tools`, or with a `-bpfcc` suffix on Debian and Ubuntu. BCC suits complex tools that need argument parsing and custom output. Its single-purpose tools include `tcplife`, `tcpconnect`, `tcpaccept`, `tcpretrans`, `tcpdrop`, `tcpstates`, `tcpconnlat`, `tcptop`, `solisten`, `sockstat`, `biolatency`, `runqlat`, `execsnoop` and `opensnoop`. Its multi-purpose tools, `trace`, `argdist`, `funccount`, `stackcount` and `funclatency`, attach to any function you name. You install the set with `apt install bpfcc-tools linux-headers-$(uname -r)` or `dnf install bcc-tools`. Modern libbpf-tools, which use CO-RE, are lighter, and they need no compiler on the target.

Opening: Picture an AI fabric with thousands of ports, hundreds of switches and tens of thousands of cables. One wrong DSCP-to-queue map, a missing PFC priority, one MTU of 1500 among nine thousands, or a leaf with the wrong ASN can degrade a 10,000-GPU job. Nothing raises an alarm when that happens. Humans typing CLI commands cannot keep a configuration that large consistent. That is why you automate the network. Instead of hand-typed configuration, you describe the network as **data (inventory and intent)**. Templates and models turn that data into configuration, and APIs deliver and verify it. Everything lives under version control and is tested before deployment.
Middle: Model families mature at different speeds. IETF models are standards-track but slow to change. OpenConfig models move fast, with vendor deviations. Native models expose everything a platform offers, but they are not portable. Pragmatic automation uses OpenConfig or IETF where the platform supports them well. It falls back to native models or the CLI where it does not, and it hides those choices behind a thin abstraction layer.

Opening: Chapter 2.28 gave you the building blocks: data, models, templates and APIs. This chapter puts them together into working systems. Consider a fabric of hundreds of switches and thousands of GPU servers. You need to know which tool fits which job, whether that is Ansible, Nornir, Terraform or a vendor controller. You also need playbooks and inventories that stay manageable at that size. Network changes should go through the same review, test and deploy discipline as software, which is continuous integration and delivery. The whole architecture has to fit together too: a source of truth, an automation engine, observability, orchestration and user interfaces. The chapter also covers virtualised network functions and on-box automation, which appear in service-provider and telco-style AI edge deployments. The end state is a network declared in Git, validated in emulation, rolled out in stages and checked continuously against telemetry. For an AI fabric of meaningful size, that is the only practical way to run it. Before you pick a tool, you need to know which job
Middle: The automation engine connects the pieces. You choose it based on the network interfaces it supports, the tooling you already have, your team's skills and the integrations it offers. Its core job is configuration management, in four stages. Backup collects running configs and state into Git regularly and before every change. Tools such as oxidized and rancid do this too. Rendering combines data and templates into the intended config. Compliance compares running configs with the intended state or policy, then reports or remediates. Deployment applies the change in a controlled way.

Opening: At 2 a.m. a training job has been running on 4,000 GPUs for nine days, and then it stops making progress. The scheduler says the nodes are healthy. The GPUs say they are idle. NCCL logs show a timeout, so everyone suspects the network. The gap between a fifteen-minute fix and a six-hour outage is rarely knowledge of one command. It is a **method**. You need to know what the system should do, build the right mental models, and choose the next question with care. You also measure instead of guessing, change one thing at a time, and record what you learn. Troubleshooting is rarely taught, yet senior infrastructure interviews probe it hard. A typical question is "walk me through how you would debug...". This chapter teaches a general method. That method covers purpose, components, models, half-split, manipulation, simplification, fixing and technical debt. You also get a practical data-collection kit and decision trees for the most common failure classes. Finally, there are fourteen case studies. They run from a Linux
Middle: Busy Kubernetes nodes show intermittent timeouts for new connections, while CPU and bandwidth look fine. In `dmesg` you find `nf_conntrack: table full, dropping packet`. The count from `conntrack -C` equals `nf_conntrack_max`, and `conntrack -S` shows insert_failed. Two problems combine here. The established timeout of 5 days keeps dead flows around forever, and the table is too small for the load.

Title: 2.31 Network monitoring and telemetry for AI data centers | words 8322 | code blocks 7

Correlation and corrective actions / Correlating switch and server telemetry / Corrective actions / 7. Operating the monitoring system / 8. Hands-on lab: a network observability stack / 9. Common mistakes / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Most network trouble in an AI cluster does not look like an outage. It looks like a slowdown. Consider a single flapping optic, a rail running at half bandwidth, or a switch queue that overflows for 50 microseconds every few seconds. A PFC storm can freeze a pod, and an ECMP imbalance can slow every collective by 15%. Each of these leaves the network technically up. Traditional once-a-minute SNMP graphs average these problems away, so the picture looks calmer than the job feels. This chapter shows you how to **see** the network. It covers the monitoring options: SNMP, syslog, streaming telemetry, flow export, active probes, in-band telemetry and packet mirroring. Then it builds a practical open-source stack. Exporters feed Prometheus, Prometheus feeds Grafana and Alertmanager, and Loki handles the logs. You will then look at the signals that matter in AI and ML fabrics: egress buffers, drops, PFC, ECN, CNPs, link quality, per-rail bandwidth skew and latency.
Middle: Congestion mapping looks at a wider picture. ECN and CNP rise on many NICs, while a spine uplink shows high utilisation and a growing egress queue. That pattern points to an ECMP collision or an oversubscribed tier. Check the flow entropy through sFlow, the adaptive routing settings and the job placement.
Exercise sample: 1. Start by stating the three core questions of network monitoring. Explain how USE, RED and the four golden signals map onto switches, NICs and cluster services. Then compare SNMP polling and traps, syslog, gNMI streaming telemetry (`sample` vs `on_change`), active probes (`TWAMP`/`blackbox`), `sFlow`/`IPFIX`, mirror-on-drop and In-Band Flow Analyzer (`IFA` / `INT`). Finish by contrasting historical and real-time monitoring, as chapter 11 of *AI Data Center Network Design and Technologies* does. 2. Opening: Suppose a training run on rented H100 nodes is slower than planned, and the bill is higher than the team expected. Both problems can trace back to network decisions. Much AI work now runs in public clouds, or in a mix of cloud and on-prem GPU clusters. A typical setup looks like this. Training bursts run on rented H100/H200/B200 nodes, inference endpoints sit in several regions, and datasets live in object storage. Checkpoints are replicated across sites, managed Kubernetes (GKE, EKS, AKS) hosts the serving layer, and private links connect everything to corporate data centres.
Middle: VPC-native clusters, which use alias IPs, are the standard. Nodes take IPs from the subnet's primary range. Pods take IPs from a secondary range, and each node gets a `/24` from that range by default, which you can tune with the maximum pods per node. Services (ClusterIPs) come from another secondary range. Pod IPs are routable inside the VPC and in peered networks. There is no overlay and no NAT inside the VPC, so pods work with VPC firewall rules and flow logs. Pods appear on-prem only if you advertise the pod range through Cloud Router with a custom advertisement. Keep the ranges non-overlapping with on-prem.

Opening: Python is the connective tissue of AI infrastructure. Training code, data pipelines, Kubernetes operators, Slurm wrappers, Prometheus exporters, Redfish clients, network automation, Ansible modules, benchmark harnesses and inference servers are all written in it. An infrastructure engineer does not need to be a language lawyer, but needs a precise mental model: what an object is, why a "variable" is only a label, why a default argument can leak state between calls for the lifetime of a long-running service, how scoping decides which name you are touching, and how to write small functions whose behaviour you can predict and test. This chapter builds that foundation from first principles and ties each idea to infrastructure code. Chapters 3.2 to 3.11 build on it: packaging, idioms, classes and typing, files and subprocesses, concurrency, APIs, CLIs and exporters, testing, data/ML essentials, and the machinery behind frameworks. 1. The interpreter reads the source and **compiles** it to **bytecode**, a compact instruction list for a stack-based virtual machine. Bytecode for imported modules is cached in `__pycache__/*.pyc`;
Middle: C-like languages make you write three things that Python does not need. The first is parentheses around the test in an `if`. In C-like code you write `if (x < y)`. In Python you write `if x < y`, and it works the same way. Python accepts extra parentheses, since any expression can sit inside them. The Python way is to leave them out, because they only add typing.

Opening: A training job fails on one node and runs fine on another, even though the code is identical. Most failures like this are packaging failures. A NumPy build may not match the binary interface that other packages expect. A PyTorch wheel built for CUDA 12.1 may land on a node with a 12.4 driver. A transitive dependency may move overnight. A distribution upgrade may change the system Python under a cron job. Packaging is also a security and compliance question, because you need to know exactly what runs in production. It is a performance question too. Container image size, import time and cold start all depend on how the code is packaged.
Middle: You have four ways to break such a cycle. Move the shared helpers into a third module. Or use `import recur1`, and qualify names such as `recur1.Y` inside functions, which run after both modules finish loading. You can also move the `from` import to the bottom of the file, or into a function body. For imports used only in type annotations, guard them with `if typing.TYPE_CHECKING:`.

Opening: "Pythonic" is not decoration. It is how the language and its libraries expect you to behave, and that is why frameworks such as pandas, PyTorch, FastAPI and the Kubernetes clients feel consistent. The data model is the set of special methods, such as `__len__`, `__getitem__`, `__iter__` and `__enter__`, that decide how your own objects work with `len()`, `for`, `with`, `sorted` and slicing. Comprehensions and generators are the tools that let you stream those logs, paginate cloud APIs and transform metrics without exhausting memory. Getting them right changes the size of your code. A 40-line loop with a bug can often become a three-line pipeline that you can test.
Middle: In `make_averager()`, the outer scope stores `series = []`. The inner `averager(new_value)` calls `series.append(new_value)`, so the list is kept in `avg.__closure__[0].cell_contents`, and `avg.__code__.co_freevars == ('series',)`. The better version, `Example 9-14`, stores only the running values `count = 0; total = 0`. It declares `nonlocal count, total` inside `averager(new_value)`, so the memory use stays O(1) instead of growing with every value in a `series` list.

Opening: Picture a small script that grows over a few months. First it gains a Redfish client with a session and a retry policy. Then it gains a Slurm wrapper with typed job records, a provider interface that hides GCP, AWS and on-prem differences, a configuration model validated from YAML, and an exporter that holds metric state. At that point it is no longer a script. It is software, and it needs the same care. Object-oriented design, data classes and type hints are what keep that software maintainable. They make state explicit. They make interfaces enforceable. They catch errors before the code reaches production. Modern Python also relies on types at runtime. pydantic models validate API input, FastAPI builds documentation from annotations, and dataclasses generate boilerplate, so you need to understand how that machinery works.
Middle: 1. Universal deployment: every class in the MRO that implements the method before the anchor must call `super().method()`. The first class that omits `super()` stops the chain. In the book's example, if `class C(B, A)` calls `super().act()` and `B.act` does not call `super().act()`, then `A.act` is never reached. 2. Call-chain anchors: the chain must end at an anchor class that defines the method without calling `super()` again. For `__init__`, the built-in root `object` serves as the anchor, through `object.__init__(self)`. For a custom method such as `act(self)`, calling `super().act()` in the last class before `object` raises `AttributeError: 'super' object has no attribute 'act'`. A common base class with its own `act`

Opening: Infrastructure code lives at the edge of a messy world. Files may not exist, or they may be half-written. Commands fail with partial output. Hosts disagree on encodings, signals arrive in the middle of an operation, and under cron or systemd there may be no terminal at all. A script that runs fine on a laptop can still corrupt a checkpoint directory at 3 a.m. The fault usually lies in one of these places. This chapter teaches the discipline of failure: exceptions, cleanup, retries, exit codes and logging. It then covers file and path handling, including `pathlib`, atomic writes, permissions, temporary files and locking. Next come text versus bytes and the serialisation formats JSON, YAML, CSV, TOML and struct. You will see how to run external programs safely with `subprocess`, and how to interact with the OS through environment variables, signals, processes, `psutil`, `/proc` and `/sys`. The chapter ends with a template for robust operational scripts.
Middle: The coder assumes that a dictionary lookup can fail in only one way, with a missing key. The name `myditctionary` is misspelled, so Python raises a `NameError` instead. The handler catches that error and silently fills in None, which hides the bug. Catching `Exception` here would do exactly the same thing.

Title: 3.6 Concurrency: threads, processes and asyncio | words 9118 | code blocks 12
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Processes / `multiprocessing` and `ProcessPoolExecutor` / GPUs and data loading / 4. asyncio / Why asyncio / Core concepts / A worker-pool template / 5. Patterns for infrastructure fan-out / Retries, backoff and jitter / Rate limiting and circuit breaking / Aggregating results / Streaming and pagination / Scheduling and periodic tasks / Combining models / Distributed concurrency / 6. Pitfalls and diagnosis / 7. Lab / 8. Common mistakes / 9. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Suppose you have to query 5,000 BMCs, health-check 800 nodes and tail logs from every pod. Other jobs upload 10 million objects, pre-process shards of a dataset, or serve many inference requests while a model runs. This is fan-out work: one job touches many machines or items at once. Doing it one item at a time is far too slow. Doing it concurrently without understanding the model, however, produces hangs, resource exhaustion, race conditions and runaway memory. This chapter explains the models from first principles and shows you how to choose between them. It then covers the production patterns that matter most: bounded concurrency, backpressure, timeouts, cancellation, error aggregation, retries, graceful shutdown and diagnosing hangs. The ground it lays also prepares you for FastAPI services in chapter 3.7, exporters and agents in chapter 3.8, and data pipelines in chapter 3.10.
Middle: A coroutine is an `async def` function. Calling it returns a coroutine object without running it. `await` suspends the caller until the awaited coroutine, future or task completes. You can use `await` only inside an `async def` function.

Opening: Sooner or later, almost every platform component grows an HTTP API. An inventory service sits in front of NetBox and Redfish. A job-submission gateway fronts Slurm or Kubernetes. A webhook receiver takes alerts from Alertmanager. A model-serving front end handles authentication, batching and streaming before it calls Triton or vLLM. An internal control plane manages GPU quotas, and an "agent" exposes health and metrics. FastAPI is the dominant Python framework for these services. It is asynchronous, built on ASGI, and type-driven. Request validation and OpenAPI documentation come from Python type hints and pydantic models. If you run control-plane services, it is fast enough for most of them, and for many data-plane services too.
Middle: The OWASP API Top 10 gives you a checklist. It covers broken object-level authorisation, which means checking ownership on every `/{id}` access. It also covers broken authentication, excessive data exposure (use response models), lack of resources and rate limiting, function-level authorisation, mass assignment, misconfiguration, injection, improper asset management, and unsafe consumption of upstream APIs.

Opening: Picture a GPU node that you must drain before maintenance. An operator runs a small script, and the script either does the job cleanly or leaves the node half-done. Most fleet operations work this way. A CLI drains a node. A script collects `nvidia-smi` or DCGM data from 500 machines. An exporter turns BMC sensors into Prometheus metrics, and a Redfish client sets boot order across a rack. A node agent reports health to a control plane. Humans run these tools at 3 a.m., and automation runs them every minute. That means the tools must be predictable, resilient, observable and easy to distribute. Predictable means clear exit codes, idempotent runs and a dry-run mode. Resilient means timeouts, retries, backoff and pagination. Observable means structured logs and metrics. Distributable means a pinned version that works on every operator's laptop and every jump host. This chapter covers the engineering patterns behind such tools, with a bias toward GPU fleets, BMCs and Prometheus. This chapter assumes you have covered 3.1 to 3.6 (functions,
Middle: Vendors differ, and you should plan for it. Schemas change over time. Thermal and Power are deprecated in favour of ThermalSubsystem, PowerSubsystem and EnvironmentMetrics. OEM extensions sit under `Oem`. Some BMCs are slow, rate-sensitive or return 500 errors under concurrent load. Serialise requests to each BMC, retry carefully, cap concurrency across the fleet, and test against every platform you run. The DMTF publishes a mockup server and the `redfish` Python library (`python-redfish-library`), which handles sessions and OData. NVIDIA tooling, such as `nvfwupd` on the DGX BMC, builds on the same standard.

Opening: Suppose a bug hides in the drain script that moves work off a GPU node. The same is true of an exporter or a provisioning helper. Each of these tools changes real machines, and one bad change can take down a GPU cluster worth millions. These tools often cannot be tested against production, so you need to catch the bug somewhere safer. Good engineering turns that risk into a fast feedback loop. Unit tests with fakes check the logic. Contract tests check the integration surfaces. Linters and type checkers catch whole bug classes before runtime, and CI refuses to merge anything that regresses. Interviewers for platform roles probe exactly this: "how do you test code that talks to hardware, Kubernetes or a cloud API?" Infrastructure code mostly runs in degraded conditions. Timeouts, partial outputs, `[N/A]` fields, empty lists, 500s and permission errors are the cases you will meet most often. Your test strategy has to cover them, not only the happy path. The diagram shows the layers, from fast unit
Middle: Test suites need monitoring too, and you should keep an eye on them. Track how long the tests take over time, how often they flake, and how much of the changed code they cover, using diff-cover. Track time-to-green as well. Fail slow tests loudly with `--durations`, and parallelise with xdist when isolation allows it. Keep the pipeline under about ten minutes for PR feedback, or split and shard it. Keep logs and JUnit reports as artefacts, and print reproduction commands for failing tests, including seed values and `pytest -k` hints.
Exercise sample: 1. Apply the test pyramid to GPU fleet tooling, from unit, to contract, to integration, to end-to-end. Opening: You will not be hired to design neural networks. You will be paged when a training job runs slowly, when a data loader starves the GPUs, when an out-of-memory error kills a run at hour 40, or when a notebook quietly holds 200 GB of RAM. Diagnosing those problems takes working fluency in the stack ML engineers use. Three parts of that stack matter most: **NumPy**, the array model underneath everything; **pandas and Arrow**, for tabular data; and **PyTorch**, for tensors, autograd, modules, devices, data loading, mixed precision and distributed hooks. This chapter stays on the infrastructure side. It looks at memory layout and copies, at where time goes, at what runs on the CPU and what runs on the GPU, and at how to measure each of them. It is not a machine-learning course. Model theory is in Part 14.
Middle: Starvation shows up as low `nvidia-smi` utilisation, with CPU workers at 100%, or as high I/O wait. To diagnose it, time `next(iter(loader))` against the model step. Then check the CPU per core with `top` or `mpstat`, check I/O with `iostat` or the network file system throughput, and check the decoding cost, such as JPEG decode or tokenisation.

Second, how right-aligned broadcasting stretches `(1000, 1) + (1, 64)` to `(1000, 64)`, and why adding `(N,)` to `(N, 1)` silently builds an `(N, N)` matrix.

Opening: Python programs are built in layers. Programs are made of modules, modules contain statements, statements contain expressions, and expressions create and process objects. Chapter 3 introduced the top layer, modules. This part of the book starts at the bottom, with built-in objects and the expressions that create and use them. Statements mostly exist to manage the objects you meet here. Later, classes let you define new object types by using and extending the types you learn now. That is why built-in objects are the starting point for every Python program.
Middle: Sets are built-in objects that hold unique, unordered items. They support operations from mathematical set theory. A set can grow and shrink, and it can hold many types of objects. Sets sit between collections and math, but they lean toward math enough to belong here.

Opening: Assignment is how Python attaches a name, or a slot inside a data structure, to an object. The target on the left of the equals sign can be a name or an object component. The right side can be any expression that creates an object. Most assignments are simple, but a few properties are worth knowing from the start. Assignments create object references. An assignment stores a reference to an object in a name or a component. It never copies the object. That makes Python variables behave more like pointers than like boxes that hold data.
Middle: This trick is not the same as the ternary expression. Python does not short-circuit here. It always evaluates both `Z` and `Y`, whatever `X` is. The `if`/`else` expression is simpler and easier to read. Like any nestable tool, the ternary expression can produce code that is hard to read. If you struggle to pack logic into one, stop and think about how hard it will be to unpack later. Your teammates will thank you.

Opening: Once you write your own functions, you need a clear idea of what a name means. When your code uses a name, Python creates it, changes it, or looks it up in a namespace. A namespace is simply a place where names live. In relation to code, each namespace corresponds to a scope, which is the part of the program where a name is visible. Almost everything about names in Python happens at assignment time. A name springs into existence when you first assign it a value, and you must assign it before you use it. Python does not ask you to declare names ahead of time. The place where you assign a name in your source code decides which namespace it binds to, and so where it can be seen.

Opening: A module usually matches one Python source file. Every file of code is a module automatically. Modules import other modules to use the names those modules define. A module can also stand for an extension written in C, Java or C#, or for a whole directory in a package import. Three tools handle modules in all of these forms. A module organizes code into self-contained packages of variables called namespaces. Every name defined at the top level of a module file becomes an attribute of the imported module object. Those names stay hidden until another file imports them, so they do not clash with names in other files.
Middle: Directory paths become real nested object paths after an import. In the earlier demo, `dir1.dir2` works and returns a module object. Its namespace holds all the names assigned by `dir2`'s `__init__.py` file. A folder has no module file of its own, so these assignments give it a place to define names.

Opening: The `try` statement is how your code catches exceptions. If an exception happens while the main block runs, Python jumps to one of the handlers and carries on from there. Those handlers can be `except`, `else`, `finally`, and `except*` clauses nested under the `try`. Each clause has its own job and its own rules for how it may be combined. Because of that, we will learn the common roles of `try` one at a time. We will put the pieces together at the end of this section. Python once used separate statements for these jobs, so the combined form is easier to read once the parts are clear.
Middle: The `codecs` module also has its own `open`, which returns a file that decodes and encodes automatically. It was used for Python 2 compatibility. New code has no clear reason to use it, though legacy code may:

Opening: Picture the on-call engineer at two in the morning. A training job has stopped reaching its storage network, and the engineer finds that an interface MTU changed earlier that day, from `9000` to `1500`. Nobody remembers who made the change, what else changed in the same session, or which value worked before the incident. The cluster is expensive and the training deadline is close, and all the engineer has is a few shell histories and an unreviewed terminal note. In an AI factory, almost everything that matters is, or should be, text in a repository. That includes Terraform that creates networks and GPU VMs, Ansible roles that install drivers and tune kernels, Slurm configuration, Kubernetes manifests and Helm values, Dockerfiles for training images, CI pipelines and runbooks. Git is the evidence trail for all of it. It tells you who changed the NCCL settings, when the MTU went from 9000 to 1500, which commit introduced the regression that halved training throughput, and how to go back. A cluster that cannot
Middle: The file turns team boundaries into enforcement. A Slurm change needs HPC approval, and a VPC change needs the network team. Pair the file with a pull request template that asks what changed, what the risk is and what the rollback plan is, plus a link to the plan output. Add labels that show the blast radius.

Opening: Picture a Friday afternoon change advisory board (CAB) meeting. An engineer has filled out a change ticket for a BGP peering and kernel sysctl update across the GPU cluster. She spends forty minutes on a conference call reading a rehearsed summary to managers who have never seen the switch configuration. She gets a verbal thumbs-up. Then she opens twenty SSH windows at midnight and pastes the commands by hand. One typo later, a missing colon in a YAML file or a BGP route leak takes the storage fabric offline, and a thousand GPUs sit idle. As Christian Adell, Scott Lowe, Matt Oswalt, and Jason Edelman observe in *Network Programmability and Automation*, Chapter 13, keeping humans in the direct manual execution path is what they call **Change Management Theater**. The process is slow and exhausting. It also gives only an illusion of safety, because the prose in the ticket is not the change that actually gets typed onto the machine.
Middle: Schedule rollouts inside a maintenance window. A rollout should never drain more than the SLO allows, and every step should be recorded for the post-incident timeline. Firmware and BIOS changes need extra care. Stage them by hardware generation, keep the previous version available, and confirm the BMC is reachable before and after the change (Part 7).
Exercise sample: 1. You start with application versus infrastructure CI/CD. (a) Contrast the two across statefulness, failure cost, rollback and credentials. These are the `Templatizer` peer review, build automation with `validate_yaml.py` and `tox`, 3-layer deployment validation with NAPALM, Batfish and ThousandEyes, virtual staging topologies, and deployment strategies with Junos `<bgp operation="replace">`. 2. Opening: Picture a GPU cluster with hundreds of nearly identical Linux machines. Every one of them needs the same kernel parameters, the same driver and CUDA stack, the same container runtime, the same monitoring agents and the same NCCL environment. When a security advisory or a driver release arrives, you have to change all of them in a controlled way. Doing that by SSH-ing into each machine is slow and error-prone. Writing bespoke shell scripts is brittle too. That is why Ansible has become the de facto tool for configuration management and fleet operations in HPC and AI environments. NVIDIA's DeepOps and many BCM/Slurm deployments are driven by Ansible, and Kubespray uses it to install Kubernetes.

Then move change control into Git peer review, staging environments and Ansible Automation Controller RBAC. Ansible also logs every executed module and its arguments, such as `ansible-command Invoked with _raw_params=...`, to `/var/log/syslog` or `journald` on the target host. That helps with auditability.

Opening: Almost every control surface in this handbook is YAML, JSON or a template that produces one. That covers Ansible playbooks and inventories, Kubernetes manifests, Helm values and charts, Slurm and NCCL config generated from templates, GitHub Actions and GitLab CI pipelines, Prometheus rules, Docker Compose, cloud-init user data and Argo CD applications. When one of these files misbehaves, the cause is usually small. Most production incidents blamed on automation are really YAML mistakes, such as bad indentation, a type change like `no` becoming a boolean, or a tab character. The rest are template mistakes: an undefined variable, wrong whitespace or an unescaped brace. Learning YAML's data model and Jinja2's expression language removes a large class of these errors. It also makes you faster at everything that follows. You need only basic scripting familiarity to follow this chapter. By the end, you should be able to write correct YAML. That includes scalars, sequences, mappings, multi-line strings, anchors, aliases and merge keys, multi-document files and comments, all without falling into type-coercion traps.
Middle: Notice that you can pass either a Python `dict` (`interface_dict`) or a custom Python class instance (`NetworkInterface("GigabitEthernet0/1", "Server Port", 10)`) into `template.render()`. Jinja2 resolves `interface.name` on both. The same chapter contrasts Jinja2 with **XSLT** XML transformations, which use `lxml.etree.XSLT`, and with **Go `text/template`**, which uses `{{range $i, $interface := .Interfaces}}` and `template.FuncMap{"ifparse": IfParse}`.

Opening: Picture a driver update that must reach every H100 node in rack 3 of cluster B, and nothing else. Your inventory decides whether that is one command or a risky guess. The inventory is the model of your fleet. It records which machines exist and what role each one plays, such as login, compute, storage, BMC or switch. It also records the rack and fabric rail of each machine, the hardware it carries, and the environment it belongs to. Hardware means the GPU count and model and the NIC type. Every playbook is only as good as that model. A clean inventory lets you roll that driver update out with one `--limit`. A messy one sends the wrong NCCL settings to the wrong hardware. For a large cluster, generate the inventory from a source of truth such as NetBox, a CMDB, cloud APIs, Terraform state, Slurm or BCM. A generated inventory does not drift from the fleet it describes.
Middle: Set `inventory = inventory` in `ansible.cfg`, where `inventory/` is a directory. Suppose that directory holds `vagrant.py`, `aws_ec2.yml` (`plugin: aws_ec2`), `gcp.yml` (`plugin: gcp_compute`), `azure_rm.yml` (`plugin: azure_rm`, `plain_host_names: true`), `group_vars/` and `host_vars/`. Ansible then runs and merges **every** static file, dynamic script and inventory plugin in that directory into one unified inventory.

Opening: Suppose a driver update has to reach every node in your fleet on the same day. Logging in to each server by hand is slow, and it is easy to miss one. A playbook is the unit of operational change on a fleet. It can install a driver, set kernel parameters, deploy a Slurm configuration, rotate a certificate, or drain and patch a node. A well-written playbook reads like a runbook. It is safe to run twice, it shows you the diff before it changes anything, and it stops at the first sign of trouble. A badly written one is a shell script in YAML. It always reports "changed", it cannot be dry-run, and it breaks differently on every host.
Middle: Most core modules support check mode. `command`, `shell` and `raw` skip in check mode, though, unless they have `creates` or `removes`. You can set `check_mode: false` to run them anyway, but only do that for read-only commands. Modules that do not support check mode are skipped with a note.

Opening: Suppose you have to configure a whole fleet with one playbook. Some nodes have 8 GPUs and others have 4. Some carry H100 cards and others carry A100s. The NICs differ too, with ConnectX-7 on some nodes and BlueField on others, and each rack uses its own management subnet. You could write a playbook for every machine type, but data is a better answer. Three things make that work. Variables describe what you intend. Facts describe what each host actually is. Templates turn that data into Slurm, NCCL, chrony, netplan or Prometheus configuration. Knowing where a value comes from, and which one wins when two sources disagree, is what separates a playbook that is correct on 1,000 nodes from one that is only correct on your laptop.
Middle: Prefer `loop` with filters such as `dict2items`, `subelements`, `product`, `zip`, `flatten` and `batch` over hand-written logic. Use `until` and `retries` for polling. If a module accepts a list, pass the whole list: `package: name: [a, b, c]` runs as one transaction, which is much faster than a loop of three calls.

Title: 4.8 Ansible handlers, conditionals, loops, blocks and error handling | words 6414 | code blocks 15
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Handlers / 2. Conditionals: when / 3. Loops / 4. Error handling / Default behaviour / Overriding judgement / Blocks: rescue and always / Stopping the play and fleet-level policies / Retries and eventual consistency / 5. Imports versus includes / 6. Patterns for operational flows / Drain → change → validate → return / Idempotent restart-if-needed / Waiting for the world / 7. Debugging control flow / 8. Lab / 9. Common mistakes / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a fleet change that goes wrong halfway. A config file changes on a GPU node, a later step fails, and the node is left half-configured. Real fleet changes are not straight lines. A service should restart only if its file actually changed. A GPU task should run only on nodes that have a GPU. A node should be drained before you change it, and if the driver install fails, the node must be left in a known state with a clear message. Ansible's control-flow features turn a list of tasks into a reliable procedure. These features are handlers, `when`, loops, `block`/`rescue`/`always`, failure keywords and retries. Subtle bugs also hide in them. Handlers that never fire, conditions that evaluate strings instead of booleans, rescue blocks that hide errors and loops that quietly skip are all easy to write and hard to spot.
Middle: For fleet changes, the order of defence is this. Validate the inputs with `assert`. Canary the change with `serial`. Check health after the change. Limit failures with `max_fail_percentage` or `any_errors_fatal`. Roll back in `rescue`, and then alert.

Opening: The earlier chapters introduced the pieces. Real work means putting them together so that a service deploys the same way every time. The pattern in this chapter has a fixed set of steps: user and directories, virtual environment, application release, configuration from templates, systemd service, reverse proxy with TLS, database, firewall, health checks and rolling update. Those steps stay the same whether the payload is an internal inventory API, a model-serving gateway, an Alertmanager webhook receiver or the Slurm accounting stack. Working through one complete deployment also shows you the operational concerns that matter on a cluster: zero-downtime releases, rollback, secrets, verification and idempotency. Consider what happens when one machine runs everything. A single failure takes the whole service down, and a change to one part forces you to touch the rest. The layout below splits the service into groups of hosts, so each part can change on its own.
Middle: Once you run a fleet of containers, Kubernetes (Part 5) takes over this job. Ansible still helps with the node layer, such as the container runtime, GPU drivers and kubelet prerequisites, and with bootstrapping clusters through Kubespray. You can also deploy Compose files with `community.docker.docker_compose_v2`.

### part-04-automation/10-debugging-ansible-playbooks.md
Title: 4.10 Debugging Ansible playbooks | words 6684 | code blocks 8
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. A layered method / 2. Verbosity and output / 3. The `debug` and `assert` modules / 4. Controlling the run while investigating / The interactive debugger / 5. Classifying common failures / Parse and templating errors before execution / Connection layer (UNREACHABLE) / Privilege and module layer / Application layer / 6. Reproducing a module run by hand / 7. Debugging variables and templates / 8. Performance debugging / 9. Debugging at scale and in CI / 10. Prevention habits / 11. Lab / 12. Common mistakes / 13. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Automation failures tend to arrive at the worst moments. A driver rollout stops halfway through 200 GPU nodes. A maintenance window has a deadline. Or the run happens in CI, where you cannot attach a debugger. In each case, fast diagnosis decides how the day goes. Ask first whether the problem is the connection, the privileges, a variable, a template, the module or the target. That question saves hours, and it keeps you away from the dangerous reflex of "just rerun it" or "just add `ignore_errors`". This chapter gives you a layered method, with the exact tools for each layer. Your playbook fails with a long message that does not say much. Where do you look first? The flowchart below sorts the failure into a layer, so you check the most likely cause before anything else. Read it from the top, and let each yes or no send you one step closer to the layer.
Middle: 1. Run `ansible-lint` and `yamllint` in pre-commit and CI, and always run `--syntax-check`. 2. Keep playbooks small, and test one change per run on a canary. 3. Name every task, use `assert` for preconditions, and avoid cleverness in Jinja. 4. Run `--check --diff` before real runs, and add verification tasks after changes. 5. Pin the versions of ansible-core, collections and target packages. 6. Keep `command` and `shell` tasks rare and guarded, and be explicit about `changed_when` and `failed_when`. 7. Avoid `ignore_errors`, and use a targeted `failed_when` instead. 8. When you find a bug, write the failing case as a Molecule test (4.13).
Exercise sample: Each exercise uses a debugging tool from this chapter. Work through them in order, and run them on a disposable host, so that a mistake costs you nothing. Repository layout for a platform team / 7. Role patterns specific to GPU infrastructure / 8. Lab / 9. Common mistakes / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Think about a GPU cluster whose configuration lives in one giant playbook. Every node repeats the same concerns: base OS hardening, time sync, drivers, container runtime, monitoring agents, Slurm clients, filesystem mounts and NCCL tuning. When each of these is an ad-hoc block, nothing can be reused, tested or versioned. A role packages one unit of configuration behind a clean interface. A collection goes a step further and packages roles, modules and plugins for distribution. NVIDIA's DeepOps, Kubespray and most vendor automation are built from roles and collections, and your own platform repository will be too. Good role design is what lets a team of five operate a thousand nodes. You are about to write a role for the NVIDIA driver, and the first question is where each piece goes. A role is a directory with a fixed layout, and each subdirectory has one job. Once you know the layout, anyone who opens the role can find the part they need. The tree below shows what a driver role looks
Middle: Upgrades must be idempotent. A role that supports changing versions detects the installed version, unloads modules if needed, installs the new one and verifies it. It should refuse to downgrade unless downgrades are explicitly allowed.

### part-04-automation/12-ansible-vault-and-secrets-management.md
Title: 4.12 Ansible Vault and secrets management | words 6427 | code blocks 12
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Threat model / 2. Ansible Vault / Files / The `vault_` indirection pattern / Encrypting single values / Vault IDs: multiple passwords / Providing the vault password without a human / 3. Keeping secrets out of output / 4. External secret managers / HashiCorp Vault (or OpenBao) / Cloud secret managers / SOPS (Secrets OPerationS) / Other options / Comparison / 5. SSH keys, sudo and BMC credentials / 6. CI/CD integration / 7. Rotation and incident response / 8. Lab / 9. Common mistakes / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Think about what your automation can reach. Automation needs the keys to everything. That means SSH keys and sudo passwords for every node, and BMC credentials for Redfish/IPMI, which are root-equivalent over the hardware. It also needs registry tokens, cloud service-account keys, database passwords, Slurm and Munge keys, TLS private keys, and Hugging Face and API tokens. A leaked automation repository or CI log is therefore a fleet-wide compromise. At the same time, pipelines must get these secrets without a person typing a password, and sometimes at 3 a.m. That tension is what this chapter works through.
Middle: 1. Treat it as compromised. Revoke or rotate it immediately, before you clean up the history. 2. Work out the exposure window and what was used, using audit and access logs. If the leaked secret could mint other credentials, rotate those too. 3. Remove the secret from current code. Purge history only after rotation, with `git filter-repo`, covered in 4.1. Then invalidate forks and caches, and notify people per policy. 4. Add the detection that would have caught it, such as a hook, a scanner or a policy. Then write a short post-incident note.
Exercise sample: 1. Start with the mechanics of Ansible Vault, which you will use most. Use `parse_ansible_vault_header()` in `code/part04/ansible_vault_secrets.py` to work through the `create`, `encrypt`, `view`, `edit`, `rekey` and `encrypt_string` commands, the header format, and the `vault_` indirection pattern from Ansible: Up and Running, chapter 10. Explain how AES-256 encryption works in Ansible Vault, and what separates `$ANSIBLE_VAULT;1.1;AES256` from `$ANSIBLE_VAULT;1.2;AES256;prod`. Then explain why keeping `group_vars/all/vars.yml` (`db_password: "{{ vault_db_password }}"`) apart from the encrypted `group_vars/all/vault.yml` beats whole-file encryption, and beats scattered `encrypt_string` blocks. 2. Move on to multi-environment vault IDs and non-interactive client

### part-04-automation/13-testing-ansible-ansible-lint-molecule-and-ci.md
Title: 4.13 Testing Ansible: ansible-lint, Molecule and CI | words 6390 | code blocks 12
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Test pyramid for Ansible / 2. Static analysis / yamllint / ansible-lint / 3. Syntax, check mode and render tests / 4. Molecule / Install and initialise / Scenario files / The test sequence / Verification options / Multi-platform and multi-host scenarios (`docker_networks` and `desired_state`, `AUR` Chapter 14) / Drivers and what containers cannot do / Testing handlers, errors and upgrades / 5. Testing playbooks and collections / 6. CI integration / 7. What to test: a checklist per role / 8. Troubleshooting Molecule / 9. Lab / 10. Common mistakes / 11. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a role that you merge on a Friday and that runs against a whole cluster on Monday. Drained nodes, broken drivers and unplanned reboots are the cost when nobody has tested it. You cannot unit-test a role against a mock, because its behaviour is the effect it has on an operating system. You can, however, test it cheaply and repeatedly in disposable containers and VMs. You can check that a second run changes nothing, and you can verify the state it leaves behind. This chapter builds a test pyramid for automation: lint, syntax, dry-run, converge-and-verify, idempotence, upgrade paths, and the small tier that needs real GPUs. Each layer catches different bugs. Lint finds style problems, deprecated syntax and risky constructs. Syntax and check tests find structural errors. Container Molecule scenarios find logic and idempotency bugs in user-space configuration. VMs cover kernel, systemd and network behaviour, and hardware tests cover drivers, firmware and fabrics. Keep most of your tests low in the pyramid, and save the expensive tiers for risks
Middle: For speed, use prebuilt Molecule images that already have Python and systemd configured. Run scenarios in parallel, and reuse instances during local development with `molecule converge` rather than `test`. Security matters too. Tests run arbitrary code, so keep untrusted PRs on runners without credentials, and keep secrets out of your Molecule inventories. Make lint and the Molecule matrix required checks, and keep the GPU tier on its own schedule.
Exercise sample: Each exercise asks you to explain or compare something from the chapter, using the code in the lab. Read the referenced functions before you start, so you know what each one checks. 1. Work through the 5-Layer Ansible Test Pyramid and static analysis, using `yamllint`, `ansible-lint` and `ansible-later` from *Ansible: Up and Running*, Chapter 14. Using `check_ansible_lint_rules()` in `code/part04/ansible_testing_molecule.py`, explain what each tier of the Ansible test pyramid catches. Opening: Run a default Ansible job against a large GPU cluster and you will wait for hours. A modern GPU cluster has hundreds to tens of thousands of nodes. A default run uses five forks, gathers facts on every host, and opens one SSH connection per task. Other tools help too. Set `ANSIBLE_LOG_PATH` to keep a log, and run `ansible-playbook -vv` to see connection timing. Use `time` for the whole-run wall clock. If the controller is CPU-bound, `strace -c -f -p` or `py-spy dump` shows where its time goes. For socket and file descriptor limits, check `ss -s` and `ulimit -n`.
Middle: For web-based reports without setting up AWX or AAP, the same book also covers ARA, which stands for *ARA Records Ansible*. ARA records every playbook, task, host and result in a searchable database with a web UI. Install it with `pip3 install "ara[server]"`, then run `export ANSIBLE_CALLBACK_PLUGINS="$(python3 -m ara.setup.callback_plugins)"`, and start the UI with `ara-manage runserver`.
Exercise sample: 1. Use `estimate_play_wall_clock_seconds()` in `code/part04/ansible_scale_performance.py` to work through the SSH settings. Explain why long EC2 hostnames exceed the 104-byte Unix socket limit without `%C`, and how `pipelining = True` cuts SSH sessions per task from 3 to 1. The benchmark comes from *Ansible: Up and Running*, chapter 20. Second, explain how `bash -c "cat /etc/sudoers /etc/sudoers.d/* %s | visudo -cf-"` validates `/etc/sudoers.d/disable-requiretty`. 2. Opening: Sooner or later the modules on the shelf will not match your hardware or your process. You may have a vendor tool with a CLI but no module. You may need a site-specific scheduler operation, such as "drain nodes in this rack if no jobs", a Redfish OEM extension, a custom inventory source, or a filter that compresses node lists into Slurm hostlist syntax. A custom module or plugin turns a fragile shell snippet into an idempotent, testable, documented task. Once more than a handful of people run automation, you also need a **platform**. A platform gives you central credentials, role-based access, audit trails, scheduling, approvals and an API. Ansible Automation Platform (AAP, and its upstream AWX) is that layer. This chapter shows how to extend Ansible and how to operate it as a shared service.
Middle: The controller exposes a REST API. In AAP the base path is `/api/controller/v2/`, and in AWX it is `/api/v2/`. There is also an official `awx` and `controller` CLI, along with the `ansible.platform` and `awx.awx` collections. Use those collections to manage the platform as code, including templates, credentials and projects.

Opening: Your AI platform is more than a set of Linux servers. It is container runtimes and Kubernetes clusters, cloud projects with GPU quotas, VPCs and managed storage, and a fabric of Ethernet and InfiniBand switches. Ansible reaches past the host. Modules that call APIs and drive network operating systems let one tool and one review process span all of these layers. The same reach shows where Ansible stops. For cloud resources that have a lifecycle and state, Terraform is the better tool (4.18). For workloads inside a cluster, Helm and GitOps are the better tools (4.26). Your practical skill is knowing which tool owns each layer, and using Ansible well where it fits.
Middle: Design the fabric as data. Leaf-spine addressing, BGP unnumbered, ECMP, MTU 9216, PFC, ECN, RoCE QoS and adaptive routing settings become variables in a `fabric.yml` data model. Those variables render into per-switch config. Validation tasks then compare LLDP neighbours with the intended cabling. They also check link speeds (400G/800G), error counters and ECMP path counts. Sections 2.16 to 2.19 and Part 12 cover the settings.

Opening: Picture a GPU node that has just arrived. The GPUs are in place, but nothing on the machine works yet. Someone has to bring up a long stack of software in the right order. The stack starts with BIOS and firmware settings, and a kernel with matching headers. It continues with the RDMA networking drivers (MLNX_OFED/DOCA), the NVIDIA driver, `nvidia-persistenced`, and Fabric Manager on NVSwitch systems. After that come the GPUDirect modules (`nvidia-peermem`), DCGM and its exporters for telemetry, the container toolkit, kernel tuning, filesystem mounts, and the scheduler or Kubernetes agent. Each link in that chain has to be correct, and the links have to work together. Get the order wrong and you spend your time debugging `modprobe` failures. Get the versions wrong and a fleet can silently run at half speed. Skip validation and the first sign of a bad node may be a failed 64-node training job. Ansible's job is to make node bring-up, upgrade and repair **deterministic, ordered, verified and repeatable** across hundreds or thousands of
Middle: Use your standard burn tool, pinned by commit SHA and built into your image. Do not compile it from a moving branch at test time. Xid errors appear in the kernel log, not in the burn tool's output, so the kernel log check is not optional.

Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Declarative versus imperative / The Five IaC Categories and Six Trade-Off Axes from *Terraform: Up and Running* (`TUR` Chapter 1) / 2. Architecture / 3. HCL language essentials / Files and blocks / Variables, locals, outputs / Resources and data sources / 4. The workflow and CLI reference / Targeted and partial runs / 5. Drift, import and refactoring / Detecting and handling drift / Importing existing infrastructure / Refactoring safely / State commands (use with care) / 6. Providers and authentication / 7. Opening: Picture a team that builds a GPU cluster by clicking through a cloud console and running a few one-off scripts. A month later, nobody can rebuild that cluster the same way, and nobody can explain the bill. Infrastructure built by hand leaves you with environments you cannot reproduce, reviews that cannot see the real change, and costs nobody can trace. Terraform, and its open-source fork OpenTofu, takes a different approach. You describe the infrastructure as code, and the tool shows you what it will change before it changes anything. Three pieces make that work. The plan step shows exactly what will change. The state records what exists today. The providers speak to hundreds of platforms, including all major clouds, Kubernetes, Helm, GitHub, DNS, Vault, Nebius, CoreWeave and bare-metal tooling.
Middle: A few symbols carry most of the meaning. `+` means create, `~` means update in place, and `-` means destroy. `-/+` means destroy and then create, while `+/-` means create before destroy. `<=` marks a data read. The phrase "known after apply" marks a value Terraform cannot know until apply runs, and "forces replacement" names the attribute that caused a replacement. Always read the summary line, which reads "Plan: X to add, Y to change, Z to destroy". Then look for unexpected destroys and replacements, especially of stateful objects.

Secrets and sensitive data in state / 11. State in CI/CD / 12. Lab / 13. Common mistakes / 14. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: State is the most dangerous file in your platform. It maps names in your code to real GPU clusters, buckets full of checkpoints and databases. It also holds secrets in plaintext. If it is corrupted, or two people write it at once, resources can be orphaned or destroyed. In a team that runs training infrastructure across dev, staging and production, state design is a core architectural decision. You have to decide where state lives, how it is locked, how it is split and who can read it. This chapter covers state mechanics, remote backends, locking, layout strategies, migration, recovery and drift in practical depth.
Middle: Assume that **anyone with state access can read every secret in it**. As you set up access, control it with IAM. Separate read from write, use one bucket per environment, use KMS keys with audit trails, and allow no human access in production except break-glass.

Opening: Think about what your platform team builds each time a new AI environment appears. You create a VPC with a training subnet and jumbo MTU, a bucket for checkpoints with versioning and encryption, a GPU node pool with quotas and labels, an IAM role for a training service account, and a Kubernetes cluster baseline. If you copy-paste HCL between environments, the clusters slowly drift apart. Then one change to a security default means editing fifty files. A module solves this by packaging one piece of infrastructure behind a small, documented interface. You write it once, version it, test it and review it once, and then reuse it everywhere. Module design is where Terraform codebases succeed or fail at scale. Good modules make the next environment a ten-line file. Bad modules become a tangle of conditionals that nobody dares to touch. When you run `terraform plan` in a directory, Terraform reads every `.tf` file in it. Some of those files call other directories as modules. Every Terraform configuration is a module,
Middle: 1. Turn a flat configuration into a first module. Extract a "checkpoint-bucket"-like module from it, using `local_file` or `random_pet` if you have no cloud account. Define typed variables with validation and outputs, then call the module twice with different inputs. 2. Review the interface. Write down which inputs are required and which have defaults. Add `precondition` and `validation` blocks, then test the failure messages with invalid values. 3. Define pools in YAML and read them with `yamldecode`. Create one module instance per key. Add a key, then remove one, and read the plan each time. Only that instance should change. 4. Create three resources with `count`. Remove the middle one

Opening: Picture a GPU platform with eight node pools that differ in two attributes. Add per-team namespaces and quotas, per-rack firewall rules, and N identical training nodes plus a few special ones. Writing all of that by hand is slow and easy to get wrong. Loops and conditionals let you describe these patterns compactly. Lifecycle rules protect what you cannot replace, such as checkpoint buckets, databases and clusters. They also orchestrate safe replacement of what you can replace, such as GPU instances and launch templates.
Middle: Create-before-destroy has its own gotchas. Resources with unique naming constraints, such as security groups, node pool names and IAM roles, collide unless the names include a random suffix. The suffix comes from `name_prefix` or `random_id`. Some resources cannot coexist, such as a static IP attached to two instances. Capacity-limited GPUs may not be available for the old and new nodes at the same time, so destroy-first with disruption may be your only option, and you should plan for it with drains.

Opening: Which tool in your organisation can create and destroy GPU clusters, IAM roles, networks and databases? It is Terraform, and it holds the strongest credentials in your organisation. Its configuration and state are also where secrets leak most quietly. A database password generated by `random_password`, a service-account key created by a resource, or a token read through a data source all end up in plaintext in state, plan files and CI logs. AI platforms are inherently multi-everything. You may need several regions to chase GPU capacity, and several accounts or projects for isolation. There are several clouds too, such as a hyperscaler alongside a GPU cloud like Nebius or CoreWeave. Kubernetes, Helm, Vault, GitHub and DNS providers sit in the same estate as well.
Middle: Terraform initialises providers before it plans resources. So if a provider argument depends on a value that does not exist yet, such as a cluster endpoint, the plan either fails or quietly uses empty settings. You have four ways out. Layered stacks are the preferred fix. You can also read an already-created cluster with data sources. For first-time creation only, you can bootstrap with `-target`, as a documented one-off. Or you can split create and configure into separate pipelines. Avoid using one provider to create resources and then depending on the credentials it created, all within one plan, wherever you can.
Exercise sample: You will trace the chapter's code in six exercises, and explain what each piece does. 1. Start with where secrets leak in Terraform: `state`, `tfplan`, `outputs` and `TF_LOG`. This exercise also covers the 7-tier mitigation hierarchy from `TUR` chapter 6. Use `classify_secret_state_exposure()` in `code/part04/terraform_secrets_providers.py` to help you trace why `sensitive = true`, `data "aws_kms_secrets"` and `data "aws_secretsmanager_secret_version"` still leave plaintext secrets inside `terraform.tfstate` when they are passed to standard resource arguments. Then compare them with four alternatives: (a) workload identity, (b) `manage_master_user_password = true`, (c) a secret container without a

Opening: Consider a GPU training platform that changes constantly. New capacity turns up in a new region, a new node pool arrives for a new accelerator generation, quotas get adjusted, and a new team needs its own network policy. Each change can cost tens of thousands of dollars per day or interrupt a multi-week training run. Terraform that works on a laptop is not enough for this. You need a repeatable process. That process has a repository layout that limits blast radius, standards that make changes reviewable, and tests at several levels. It also has pipelines that produce evidence (4.2), policies that stop expensive or insecure mistakes, and operating practices that keep dozens of engineers from stepping on each other. This chapter turns individual Terraform skills into a production practice.
Middle: A plan that looks harmless can still delete a production node pool or open an SSH port to the world. Policy as code reads the plan before anything is applied, so it can stop those changes first. Each rule below is a check that a policy engine runs against the plan (4.2), and the examples are specific to AI infrastructure.

Opening: Think about the bill for a GPU platform. Most of that money goes to GPU capacity, and that capacity comes from two very different kinds of cloud. Hyperscalers such as GCP give you rich building blocks, including VPC, IAM, GKE, managed storage and reservations. Those blocks surround a limited set of GPU machine families. GPU-focused providers such as Nebius, CoreWeave or Lambda work differently. They expose clusters with InfiniBand fabrics, shared filesystems and Kubernetes or Slurm layers, often through simpler but different APIs. As a platform engineer, you need to express both kinds of cloud as code. That covers the network, the capacity, the clusters, the storage and the identity, all within the guardrails of 4.23. This chapter builds a representative GCP stack from end to end. It then maps the same design onto a Nebius-style GPU cloud, so you can see which concepts transfer and which stay provider-specific. Products and APIs change quickly, so verify every identifier here against current provider documentation.
Middle: 4. If the provider offers managed Kubernetes with GPU node groups and GPU Operator integration, Terraform can handle the cluster and its node groups. A separate Helm or Argo layer then installs add-ons (4.25, 4.26). For Slurm, use the provider's automation, such as Helm charts on Kubernetes or Terraform modules, or provision VMs and run Ansible (4.17, Part 6).

Opening: Suppose your platform team has just handed over a fresh Kubernetes cluster. It is not an AI platform yet. Someone still has to install the GPU Operator or device plugins, choose a scheduler such as Kueue or Volcano, and set up storage classes and CSI drivers. Ingress, monitoring (Prometheus and the DCGM exporter), Argo CD, cert-manager, team namespaces with GPU quotas, RBAC and network policies come next. Terraform's Kubernetes and Helm providers can manage these platform layers in the same workflow as the cloud infrastructure underneath. But Terraform is a poor fit for continuously reconciling application workloads. Mixing cluster creation with in-cluster resources in one state is a classic source of pain.
Middle: OpenTofu (`tofu`) is an MPL-licensed fork of Terraform 1.5.x, maintained under the Linux Foundation. For the content of this chapter, it behaves the same way. The sections below cover the four areas that matter when you consider the switch.

Argo CD / Architecture and objects / CLI / 3. Flux / Argo CD vs Flux / 4. Opening: Picture a GPU Kubernetes platform. It is a large stack of layers: operators, CRDs, schedulers, monitoring, ingress, storage, policies, team namespaces and quotas, inference services and pipelines. All of these change constantly. They also have to stay consistent across dev, staging and production clusters, and across regions. GitOps handles this by making Git the source of truth and an in-cluster controller the executor. Every change becomes a reviewed commit, and rollback is `git revert`. Drift, meaning the live cluster no longer matches Git, is detected and optionally healed. Credentials never leave the cluster, and the audit trail is the Git history. For platform teams that run many clusters, this is the default delivery model for everything above the infrastructure layer. Picture a cluster that someone changed by hand on a Friday night. Nobody wrote the change down, and nobody can say what the cluster should look like on Monday. GitOps is designed to catch exactly this kind of drift. The OpenGitOps principles describe a system that is declarative. Its desired
Middle: A bug fix to a release is a little different. The pull request must change the YAML file in every relevant release directory. That is more work than one cherry-pick, but it has a benefit. A single change request shows that every affected version gets the same fix, so no version is missed.

Use `expand_applicationset_matrix()`

Opening: Imagine a training job that sits for an hour while its image downloads. Every training job, inference server and platform component in this handbook ships as a container image, so any of them can hit this kind of delay. A PyTorch image is routinely 10 to 30 GB. Pulling it onto 500 nodes at once can saturate a registry and delay a job by an hour. A stale CUDA userspace inside an image can break against a newer host driver. A layer ordering mistake can rebuild a 20-minute dependency install on every code change, and an image from an unverified registry is a supply-chain risk. Kubernetes, the subject of the rest of Part 5, schedules containers. It cannot fix any of these problems on its own. You need to know what a container actually is, how images are structured and distributed, and how to build small, fast, reproducible, and secure images. Everything after this chapter rests on that foundation.
Middle: Careless builds cause bloat, configuration problems and failures. A container may spare you some of the intricacies of systemd, but many other things can still go wrong. Isolation does not protect you from your own mistakes. Adding packages at random until a problem disappears leaves a mess, so understand each change you make.

### part-05-kubernetes/02-container-runtimes-containerd-runc-cri-and-oci.md
Title: 5.2 Container runtimes: containerd, runc, CRI and OCI | words 9588 | code blocks 21
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. CRI-O and alternatives / Choosing a runtime / CRI-O and the other runtimes / CRI-O in practice / Docker Engine and the dockershim / Virtual Kubelet / 4. OCI runtimes and RuntimeClass / The OCI project and runc / The OCI runtime configuration / Kata Containers in more detail / 5. GPU runtimes: NVIDIA Container Toolkit with containerd / 6. Node-level tools / 7. Cgroup drivers and cgroup v2 / 8. Operations: upgrades, GC, logs, security / 9. Failure modes and diagnostics / 10. Lab / 11. Common mistakes / 12. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a training pod that will not start. The cause is often below Kubernetes itself. An image pull may stall, a runtime may be misconfigured for the NVIDIA toolkit, or the cgroup driver may be wrong. A snapshotter might run out of space, a shim might leak, or a runtime upgrade might restart every container. The kubelet does not run containers. It asks a **container runtime** to do that, through the Container Runtime Interface. That runtime relies on an **OCI runtime** to create the namespaces and cgroups. Knowing these layers, and the tools that talk to each one, lets you debug a node when `kubectl describe` is not enough.
Middle: runc implements the OCI runtime spec, and it lives in the OCI project. Docker, containerd and CRI-O all use runc to spawn containers according to the spec. Leaning on runc lets those runtimes focus on higher-level work, such as pulling images, configuring networking and handling storage. The OCI was founded in 2015 to collaborate on container specifications. Docker donated runc to it, and CoreOS pushed container runtimes forward with rkt.

Opening: Imagine a large training job starting on a busy cluster. An MPI or PyTorch job can create hundreds or thousands of pods at once. At the same time, thousands of nodes publish their status, and controllers and schedulers reconcile state without pause. Batch systems such as Kueue and Volcano add their own load on top. When this goes wrong, jobs sit Pending, deployments stall, and `kubectl` times out. The control plane is a shared, stateful system, and it takes that pressure directly. To reason about these failures, you need to know each component, what it reads and writes, which links are synchronous, and which are eventually consistent watches. This chapter builds that map first. Then it shows where GPUs, device plugins, schedulers and operators plug in.
Middle: The book's DNS Service has the cluster address `10.96.0.10`. Any container in the cluster has that address written into its `/etc/resolv.conf` file. If you open a shell in a running container, you can check that file yourself. It is the quickest way to confirm which DNS address a container uses.
