Opening: Suppose you inherit a GPU cluster and nobody left a runbook. You need to know what a healthy node looks like before you can say what is wrong with one. Many GPU clusters leave you to work that out yourself. On-premises DGX and HGX systems, colocation sites and some GPU clouds are not managed Kubernetes. You install and operate Kubernetes yourself, often with kubeadm, Kubespray, BCM or a vendor distribution. Even if you later move to a managed service, building a cluster by hand once teaches you what each component does. It shows what must be true of a node: kernel modules, sysctls, swap, the runtime and cgroups. It also shows how certificates and kubeconfigs fit together. You will meet the same pieces again when a worker refuses to join or the CNI will not start. This lab is the foundation for the following chapters on the API, controllers, the kubelet, networking, storage and upgrades. It also prepares you for adding GPUs in Part 11.

### part-05-kubernetes/05-the-api-server-and-etcd-how-a-request-becomes-cluster-state.md
Title: 5.5 The API server and etcd: how a request becomes cluster state | words 10306 | code blocks 18

The request pipeline / 3. resourceVersion, generation and concurrency / 4. Writing objects: create, update, patch, apply / 5. Audit, authn/z debugging and security notes / 6. API Priority and Fairness (APF) / 7. etcd internals / Deployment models for etcd / Data model and Raft / MVCC, revisions, compaction, defrag / What lives in etcd / Encrypting Secrets in etcd / Performance factors and tuning / Backup, restore, and member operations / 8. Opening: Picture a 512-GPU training job that starts all at once. In seconds, it creates hundreds of pods and thousands of objects: pods, services, configmaps and events. Each node's kubelet and each controller keeps a watch open. Schedulers and batch systems list large collections, and monitoring tools poll. The control plane absorbs all of this load, even though no GPU is faulty. When the API server or etcd becomes the bottleneck, the whole platform degrades. Pods linger in Pending, `kubectl` times out, and controllers fall behind. In the worst case, leader elections thrash and workloads are evicted. A senior platform engineer must understand exactly how a request flows through the API server. That means knowing how etcd stores and serves data, which metrics reveal trouble, and how to scale and protect these two components.
Middle: An object can be up to about 1.5 MiB, the etcd request size limit. Large ConfigMaps and Secrets, and huge custom resources such as Argo Workflows with a large status, hurt performance. Huge Pod lists do the same. Avoid three patterns. Do not store blobs, logs or metrics in objects. Do not update status every second across tens of thousands of objects. And do not create thousands of Events per second.
Exercise sample: These exercises connect the chapter's ideas to the code and to the cluster. Work through them in order, and note what you observe. 1. Opening: Picture an inference server that crashes at 2 a.m. and comes back on its own. Something noticed the failure and fixed it. In Kubernetes, that something is a controller. Deployments keep inference servers at the right replica count. Jobs retry failed training pods, and StatefulSets give distributed workers stable identities. The node controller evicts pods from dead GPU nodes, and the Endpoints controller keeps load balancers pointed at ready replicas. The GPU Operator, Kueue, KubeRay and the Training Operator are controllers too, and you will install, debug and sometimes write them. If you understand the reconcile loop deeply, you can predict what the system will do after a failure. You can explain "why did it restart my pod?", read operator logs, and design custom controllers (5.29 to 5.33) that behave well at scale. You delete a pod that belongs to a Deployment, and a replacement appears within moments. Nobody typed a command to create it. That behavior comes from the controller pattern, and it is the idea behind everything else
Middle: The second mechanic is client compatibility during a `RollingUpdate`, from *Kubernetes: Up and Running*, Chapter 10. During a Deployment `RollingUpdate` with `25% max unavailable, 25% max surge`, both `v1` and `v2` `ReplicaSet` pods serve traffic at the same time. Even after the `v1` pods terminate, external clients, mobile clients or JavaScript clients may still run `v1` code. Every service API and database schema must therefore stay backward compatible across adjacent versions. `kubectl rollout undo` works in an emergency. You can also inspect a revision with `kubectl rollout history deployment kuard --revision=2`. Reverting the YAML in Git and running `kubectl apply` keeps source control aligned with the live cluster.
Exercise sample: 1. In your own words, explain why Kubernetes controllers are level-triggered reconcile loops, not edge-triggered event handlers. Describe how the loop compares observed cluster state against `spec` in `Reconcile(namespace/name)`. Then trace how a controller converges after it was stopped while 3 pods were deleted and `replicas` was scaled. 2. Opening: Suppose a training job runs for an hour, and then one worker is evicted for no obvious reason. Or an inference server sits in a restart loop while its model loads. In AI infrastructure, problems like these usually trace back to a pod. Every AI workload ends up as pods. A training worker with eight GPUs needs a large shared-memory volume. An inference server needs probes tuned for slow model loads. A data-preparation Job and a node agent DaemonSet also run as pods. The pod spec holds the decisions that make a job fast, make it fail mysteriously, get it evicted under memory pressure, or leave GPUs idle. You make those decisions in resource requests and limits, the QoS class, the security context, volumes, lifecycle hooks, termination grace and scheduling constraints. The workload controllers add replica management, identity, per-node placement and run-to-completion behavior: Deployment, StatefulSet, DaemonSet, Job and CronJob. This chapter is the practical reference for writing those objects correctly.
Middle: Each DaemonSet needs a name that no other DaemonSet in the same namespace uses. It also needs a Pod template spec, which the controller uses to create Pods when needed. This is where DaemonSets part ways with ReplicaSets. Unlike a ReplicaSet, a DaemonSet creates Pods on every node by default, unless you use a node selector.

Opening: Picture a 64-node training job that takes 12 minutes to start, instead of 90 seconds. Or picture one pod that sits in `ContainerCreating` and never moves on. In both cases you need to know what happens between the YAML and the first line of your training script, and where the time goes. The stages include scheduling, image pull and unpack, CNI attachment, CSI mounts, device plugin allocation, the RDMA and InfiniBand plumbing, cgroup creation, and the probes that gate readiness. Knowing the kernel objects at each stage also lets you debug from the node. Those objects are namespaces, veth pairs, mounts, cgroups and device nodes. They matter most when Kubernetes' own abstractions are not enough. This chapter is the end-to-end trace. It ties together Part 1 on Linux and Part 2 on networking with 5.1 to 5.7.
Middle: A pod can stay in `Terminating` for several reasons. The kubelet may be unresponsive or partitioned, so the pod stays until the node returns or is deleted. A finalizer on the pod, which is rare, can also hold it. Processes in `D` state on a hung NFS or CSI mount cannot be killed. A volume can fail to unmount with `Device or resource busy` while files are still open. A runtime shim can hang, CNI DEL can fail, or a `preStop` hook can run past the grace period. Do not use `--force --grace-period=0` on StatefulSet pods or on pods with RWO volumes unless you are certain the node cannot still

Opening: Picture a GPU server with 8 GPUs, dual-socket CPUs, multiple NICs and NVMe drives. The kubelet is the node agent on that machine, and its settings decide how the hardware behaves. Cluster policy reaches the hardware through the kubelet. It decides how many pods a node accepts, and whether a training pod gets exclusive CPU cores aligned with its GPUs' NUMA node. It also decides how much memory the system and Kubernetes daemons keep for themselves, and what gets evicted when memory or disk runs low. Two more decisions sit with the kubelet: how long a node can lose connectivity before its pods are rescheduled, and how containers are killed on shutdown. Left at defaults, these settings leave a lot of performance and reliability on the table. You will see the symptoms as cross-NUMA traffic, CPU throttling, noisy neighbours, nodes that die from OOM because nothing was reserved for the OS, and long recovery times after failures. This chapter explains the kubelet's responsibilities and how to tune them for AI
Middle: When memory runs short, the kubelet picks victims in a fixed order. It starts with pods whose usage exceeds their requests, and BestEffort counts as request 0. Among those, lower priority goes first, then the pods furthest over their requests. For disk pressure, the order uses ephemeral storage usage over request, and then priority. Guaranteed pods are evicted last, and only if system daemons are the problem and nothing else is left. Evicted pods end `Failed` with the reason `Evicted`, and controllers recreate them elsewhere. Critical pods, meaning pods with priority `system-node-critical`/`system-cluster-critical` and static pods, are protected.

Opening: Pods are ephemeral, and their IP addresses change whenever a pod is replaced. Still, everything that talks to inference servers, vector databases, parameter servers, feature stores, metrics endpoints or the control plane needs an address that stays put. Services provide that address. At small scale, a Service looks like plain load balancing. At scale, it becomes much harder. With thousands of Services and tens of thousands of endpoints, iptables rule updates take seconds and CPU. Conntrack tables fill during burst traffic from training clients or inference gateways, and long-lived gRPC/HTTP2 streams pin to a single backend. `externalTrafficPolicy` also decides whether your LLM gateway sees client IPs and balances evenly. Understanding how a ClusterIP becomes a pod IP in the kernel is essential for debugging latency spikes, connection resets, uneven load and "Service works from some nodes but not others". Suppose a vLLM deployment serves requests from several pods, and those pods come and go all day. Clients cannot keep track of which pod IP is alive at any moment. A
Middle: This example comes from a cluster on Google Cloud, run through GKE. How a load balancer is set up depends on the cloud. Some clouds, such as AWS with Elastic Load Balancing (ELB), give a hostname instead of an IP address. Creating a cloud load balancer can take a few minutes on most providers.

### part-05-kubernetes/11-dns-and-service-discovery-with-coredns.md
Title: 5.11 DNS and service discovery with CoreDNS | words 8049 | code blocks 10
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Records Kubernetes creates / Reading a Service name / 2. Resolver behaviour inside pods / The ndots:5 problem / 3. DNS-based discovery for distributed AI workloads / 6. Observability / 7. Debugging method / 8. Lab / 9. Common mistakes / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: DNS is the first dependency of almost every pod. A training worker resolves its rendezvous address, a data loader resolves object-store endpoints, and an inference gateway resolves model servers. MPI and PyTorch use headless Services for peer discovery. Every external API call, to registries, Hugging Face or telemetry, begins with a lookup too. If you run training jobs, you will meet these names every day. Cluster DNS is also a classic hidden bottleneck. Kubernetes' default `ndots:5` multiplies queries, and UDP conntrack races add 5-second stalls. One CoreDNS pod serving a 2,000-pod job start causes timeouts. When DNS fails, the whole platform can look as if the network is down. Knowing how records are generated, how resolvers behave inside pods, how CoreDNS is configured, and how to scale and debug it is basic platform hygiene.
Middle: External dependencies such as object stores, registries and model hubs are a risk too. Pin them to IP-addressed mirrors or internal resolvers for stability, and consider a local caching proxy. When external DNS fails, the symptom is a training job that hangs at startup. Add DNS health to your preflight checks in Part 17.

Opening: Suppose your team has just put a model behind an endpoint, and users report that long answers stop halfway through. The cause often sits at the edge, the point where traffic first enters the cluster. Inference endpoints, notebooks, experiment trackers, dashboards, model registries and internal tools all need a controlled entry point. That entry point handles TLS and authentication. It routes requests by hostname, path or header, for example by model name or tenant. It also splits traffic between model versions for canary and A/B tests. Long-running answers need long timeouts and streaming. Token streaming can use Server-Sent Events, WebSockets or gRPC. The edge also applies rate limiting per API key and exposes metrics.
Middle: The second setting is host networking with `hostNetwork: true` and `ClusterFirstWithHostNet`. Binding the Ingress proxy container directly to the node's network namespace lets incoming packets reach the proxy without traversing the Kubernetes Service `iptables`/`IPVS` fabric. When `hostNetwork: true` is enabled, you must set `dnsPolicy: ClusterFirstWithHostNet`. That lets the proxy still resolve in-cluster `*.svc.cluster.local` Service names through CoreDNS, as in the book's Pod template snippet (untested here):

Opening: A distributed training job is only as fast as its network. Collective operations move gigabytes on every step. How each pod attaches to the network decides whether it gets line rate or only a fraction of it. The default pod network relies on veth, bridge or overlay devices, and on iptables or eBPF. It is built for general microservices connectivity and policy, not for 400 Gb/s RDMA. So GPU clusters use a layered design. A CNI handles the control and general traffic. Secondary high-performance interfaces, such as SR-IOV VFs, macvlan on RDMA NICs or host networking, carry the NCCL traffic.
Middle: BlueField DPUs can run OVS or OVN-Kubernetes datapaths in hardware. With switchdev and hardware flow offload, pods get VF representors. Policy and encapsulation then run on the DPU instead of the host CPUs. This matters for multi-tenant AI clouds, which Parts 9 and 12 cover.

Opening: Your GPU cluster's primary pod network carries more traffic than most people expect. It handles control traffic, service-to-service calls, logs and metrics, and data-loader traffic over TCP in non-RDMA setups. It also carries checkpoint transfers and, for many teams, some NCCL traffic over TCP sockets. The design of this network decides several things at once. It sets the failure domains, which can be a flat L2 network or a routed L3 network. It decides whether you can see which pod talked to which, how security policy is enforced, and how much throughput each core delivers. It also decides how the network fits the datacentre fabric that Part 2 and Part 12 describe, a leaf-spine design running eBPF and BGP.
Middle: On bare metal with a BGP fabric, such as NVIDIA Spectrum or Cumulus, Arista or Cisco, Calico BGP or Cilium native routing with BGP integrates cleanly. Avoid overlays there, because they would hide the fabric's ECMP and visibility. Align your ASN scheme with the fabric design in 2.16 and Part 12.

Testing and debugging / 7. Lab / 8. Common mistakes / 9. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a shared GPU platform where one team's notebook can open a connection to another team's model server. That is the default. Kubernetes networking is flat and open: any pod can connect to any other pod and to the node. A compromised dependency in a training job could then scan the cluster, the metadata endpoints and the API server. Data could also leave through paths nobody has closed. NetworkPolicy is the baseline control for this problem. You use it to segment tenants and workloads into inference, training, data services and system components. You also use it to constrain egress to approved storage and registries, and to meet compliance requirements.
Middle: For a long time, teams used the network itself as identity. IP addresses, VPNs and firewalls decided which application could reach which service. Cloud native systems break that model. Containers share network stacks and machines, and workloads move between nodes often. The result is a constant churn of IP addresses. In a multicloud, API-driven setup, calls also leave the cluster for providers that need proof of who is calling.

Configuration management patterns for AI workloads / 6. Configuration management patterns for AI workloads / 7. Failure modes and troubleshooting / 8. Lab / 9. Common mistakes
Opening: Consider a notebook that carries one token. Whether that token can read every team's checkpoints depends on how you injected it and how far you scoped it. AI platforms collect a lot of sensitive material. They hold object-store credentials for datasets and checkpoints, model hub tokens and API keys for LLM providers. They also hold registry credentials, database passwords for metadata stores, signing keys, and service tokens that operators use to reach the API. Configuration matters just as much: model parameters, tokenizer paths, NCCL environment, scheduler settings and feature flags all live there too. How you inject, rotate and scope these values decides how far a single failure can reach. A leaked notebook can read every team's checkpoints, and a compromised job can create GPU pods without limit. RBAC is the authorisation layer for the Kubernetes API itself. A mistaken ClusterRoleBinding to `system:authenticated`, or a broad `nodes/proxy` grant, is a cluster-wide compromise. This chapter covers configuration objects, secret handling practices and RBAC design.
Middle: Environment variables have two downsides. A change to the ConfigMap or Secret does not reach a running Pod until it restarts. Also, some frameworks print their whole environment at startup or during a crash. For a Secret, that can leak the password into a log file.
Exercise sample: 1. Work through ConfigMap and Secret consumption, the key naming rules (*Kubernetes: Up and Running* Chapter 13, Table 13-1), live updates and `immutable: true`. Then explain how `$(VAR)` substitution works in `command` and `args`. Explain why volume-mounted ConfigMaps update through the `..data` symlink swap, while env vars and `subPath` mounts do not. Opening: Imagine a training run that stalls because the dataset cannot reach the GPUs fast enough. Data is half of every AI job. Training reads terabytes of tokenised data, writes multi-hundred-gigabyte checkpoints on a schedule, and inference servers load tens or hundreds of gigabytes of weights at startup. Storage choices decide whether 1,000 GPUs sit idle waiting on a dataset. They also decide whether a checkpoint takes 30 seconds or 15 minutes, which sets how much compute you lose on each failure. They decide whether a pod can move to another node and keep its data, and whether a node failure loses state. Kubernetes hides most of this behind abstractions: volumes, PersistentVolumes, StorageClasses and CSI drivers. Those abstractions still carry mechanics that affect performance, failure behaviour and cost. This chapter explains the abstractions, the CSI protocol and the mount mechanics. It also covers the patterns that suit datasets, checkpoints, caches and model weights. Part 13 covers the storage systems themselves. Every pod needs a place to put its files, and the
Middle: Each CSIDriver object declares its behaviour. The fields include `attachRequired`, `podInfoOnMount`, `volumeLifecycleModes`, `fsGroupPolicy`, `storageCapacity`, `requiresRepublish` and `seLinuxMount`. Each node also registers itself in a `CSINode` object, which lists `topologyKeys` and `allocatable.count`, the maximum number of volumes that node can attach.

Opening: Picture eight 8-GPU training pods waiting to be placed. The default scheduler handles them one at a time, and it can start seven and leave the eighth with nowhere to go. Those seven pods then hold their GPUs and wait, and nothing finishes. GPUs are scarce, expensive and tied to hardware topology, so each placement decision costs real money. Other failures are just as costly. The default scheduler can put a latency-sensitive inference service onto the same failing node. It can fragment the cluster so that no node has 8 free GPUs, even though 40 are free. It can let background jobs hold GPUs that production inference needs. It can also put two GPUs of one pod across NUMA nodes, which halves their bandwidth.
Middle: Cluster Autoscaler and Karpenter react to Pending pods by adding nodes, and they remove underused nodes. GPU node scale-up takes minutes, and capacity is not guaranteed. Combine them with a Kueue ProvisioningRequest for atomic multi-node provisioning (5.23).

Picture a GPU fleet where a GPU fails every few weeks: you are constantly swapping hardware, while also planning driver, kernel and Kubernetes upgrades. GPU fleets grow in waves as capacity is purchased, and they serve teams with conflicting demands. Early choices decide how painful each later change will be. Think about the control plane (managed or self-managed), one big cluster or many, how nodes are provisioned and replaced, and how add-ons are versioned. Opening: Picture an upgrade that stalls halfway through, with the API server down and a training run waiting on cluster state. Kubernetes releases a minor version about three times a year, and it supports each one for roughly a year. So a GPU platform is always one or two upgrades away from running an unsupported version. Every upgrade touches the control plane, the container runtime, CNI/CSI, the GPU Operator and driver stack, kernels, and sometimes API versions that your operators still use. At the same time, training clusters hold state you cannot easily replace. That includes etcd, which holds all object state. It also includes PersistentVolumes, which hold datasets, checkpoints, registries and metadata DBs, along with secrets, keys and the knowledge stored in GitOps repos.
Middle: On GKE, EKS and AKS, you upgrade the control plane through the API, the console or Terraform, and the provider handles the HA rollout. Then you upgrade node pools by surge or by blue/green node-pool replacement. Use maintenance windows, exclusions and release channels (rapid, regular or stable) to control the timing. For GPU node pools, set `maxUnavailable` and surge according to spare capacity. Disable auto-upgrade for pools with reservations, and pin node image versions until the driver stack is validated.

Opening: Picture a Monday morning when a shared GPU cluster suddenly stops working. An expired certificate has silently taken down the entire control plane. A shared GPU cluster is attractive to attackers, and it is easy for users to misconfigure. A privileged container can read the host. A `hostPath` mount can expose model weights and credentials. Unbounded GPU requests can starve a team, and unsigned images can inject malware into training pipelines. Three mechanisms keep this under control. The PKI and identity layer decides who is speaking and whether they can be trusted. Admission control decides what is allowed to enter the API. Policy as code writes the organisation's rules down and enforces them. This chapter explains certificate management in Kubernetes, including rotation and failure modes. It also covers identity federation, the admission chain, Pod Security, and practical policies for AI platforms. Suppose an API call fails with a certificate error, and you need to know which certificate caused it. That question is much easier to answer once you know how
Middle: Applying these controls by hand is hard. Every field must be right for every Pod, and a wrong AppArmor, seccomp or SELinux profile can break the application. Tools such as the Security Profiles Operator can generate and manage seccomp profiles for you.

Opening: Picture a shared GPU cluster on a busy morning. One team's job floods the API or the storage, and its neighbours slow to a crawl. Nobody can say who is holding which GPUs, and nobody knows what any of it costs. A GPU cluster is rarely built for one team. Research groups, product teams, external customers and platform engineers all share the same scarce, expensive hardware, and each of them expects fairness, security, performance isolation and self-service. Without a deliberate tenancy model, those expectations collide. Noisy neighbours appear when one job floods the API or the storage. Cross-tenant data access becomes a security incident. One team squats on GPUs that others need, the support load grows beyond what you can manage, and there is no cost visibility at all.
Middle: The second way uses a custom resource such as `PinterestService`. Every needed detail lives in that object. The operator creates the `Ingress` and configures the DNS system itself. No underlying detail leaks to the developer, and the platform keeps full flexibility in how it is built.

Opening: Picture an inference endpoint that suddenly gets a burst of traffic. Traffic like this is bursty, and over-provisioning for it is expensive. Each replica of a 70B model occupies several GPUs and takes minutes to start, so you cannot add replicas in a hurry. Scale too late and you break your latency SLOs. Scale too early, or never scale down, and you burn thousands of dollars per day. Classic CPU-utilisation autoscaling is nearly useless for GPU model servers. The CPU stays low while the GPU saturates. Throughput depends on request queues, batch sizes and KV-cache pressure, and none of those show up in CPU load.
Middle: Several things block scale-down. Pods without controllers block it, and so do pods with local storage (`emptyDir` blocks unless annotated), PDBs, kube-system pods, and pods annotated with `cluster-autoscaler.kubernetes.io/safe-to-evict: "false"`. GPU nodes need extra care. GPU requests are all-or-nothing, so a node with a single running pod is not low utilisation if that pod holds all of its GPUs. Set scale-down thresholds with GPU requests in mind.

Opening: A GPU platform is only as reliable as your ability to see inside it. Picture a training job that silently runs at 40% of its expected throughput. The cause turns out to be one node's NIC, which negotiated a lower speed. Other failures hide in the same way. An inference service's p99 latency can double when its KV-cache fills. A node's GPU can throw Xid errors for days before the job finally fails. A runaway controller can slow the whole control plane. Observability turns these mysteries into dashboards and alerts. The Kubernetes ecosystem has now largely converged on a common toolkit. It uses Prometheus-compatible metrics, structured logs shipped to a central store, OpenTelemetry for traces, and Grafana for visualisation. This chapter walks through the architecture and the exporters that matter. It then shows how to design alerts you can act on, and how to scale and secure the stack. GPU-specific signals appear along the way, and Part 17 covers them in more depth.
Middle: Logs once lived in a file on a server's disk. That worked when there were fewer workloads and fewer instances. Today, applications usually write to standard out and standard error, the same way a command-line tool prints. Workloads are ephemeral and often have no disk to keep logs, so the logs must leave the workload as they are written.

Opening: Kubernetes failures on GPU platforms rarely show up in `kubectl describe`. The pod reports `Running`, the GPU sits idle, and the cause could be almost anything: a mount namespace, a missing device node, a NIC that negotiated the wrong speed, a conntrack table full of stale entries, an IB link that keeps flapping, or a process stuck in uninterruptible I/O on a parallel filesystem. Speed matters here, because each minute a 1,000-GPU job stays stalled costs real money. This chapter gives you a repeatable method: isolate the layer, gather evidence, and then reduce the problem. It also covers the tools that work when Kubernetes' own abstractions are not enough. Those are node-level tools that inspect the pod's namespaces. The chapter ends with playbooks for the commonly seen failures. Before you start, you should have worked through the whole of Part 5 so far, especially 5.8, 5.9, 5.10 and 5.13, along with the Linux and networking material in Parts 1 and 2. When you finish, you will be able to apply
Middle: Start with the self-checking Python lab, which you can run on your own machine. It verifies the container exit-code and OOM classifier, `classify_container_exit_code`, which decodes exit codes `137`, `139`, `143`, `126` and `127`, along with the `/dev/shm` cgroup OOM. It also verifies the 4-hop Pod connectivity bisection engine, `diagnose_pod_connectivity_bisection`, which isolates Pod/App, Cross-Node CNI, Service/kube-proxy and CoreDNS. Finally, it checks the GPU, PCIe and Xid hardware triager, `triage_gpu_node_symptoms`.

Opening: Suppose a container on a GPU node is killed with exit 137, or a pod sees only some of its GPUs. Your first look usually goes to Kubernetes, but the cause often sits lower. Kubernetes does not isolate, schedule or limit anything by itself. It asks the Linux kernel to do that work. So every behaviour you debug on a GPU node is a kernel mechanism that shows up through a Kubernetes API. A throttled data loader, pods that cannot connect after a rule update, and memory that seems to disappear all come from this layer. So do an RDMA NIC that is unusable inside a pod and a kernel upgrade that breaks the GPU driver. This chapter maps each Kubernetes concept to its kernel feature. It also covers the kernel parameters, versions and failure modes that matter for GPU hosts. It consolidates Parts 1 and 2 (Linux and networking) with Part 5.
Middle: The GPU driver is a set of kernel modules: `nvidia.ko`, `nvidia_uvm.ko`, `nvidia_modeset.ko`, `nvidia_drm.ko`, `nvidia_peermem.ko`, and `nvidia_fs.ko` for GDS. It creates `/dev/nvidia0..N`, `/dev/nvidiactl`, `/dev/nvidia-uvm` and `/dev/nvidia-caps/*`. User space talks to the driver through an ioctl-based interface. That interface is versioned, so userspace `libcuda` must match the kernel driver's major interface, as covered in 5.1. Fabric Manager manages NVSwitch. You can choose the open kernel modules (`nvidia-open`) or the proprietary ones. Modules are tied to the kernel version through DKMS or precompiled builds. Secure Boot requires signed modules. Module parameters named `NVreg_*` live in `/etc/modprobe.d`, and persistence mode keeps driver state initialised.
Exercise sample: Each exercise asks you to explain a behaviour and to check it against the lab code. Use How Linux Works (chapter 17), Networking and Kubernetes (chapters 2 to 5) and Production Kubernetes (chapters 3 to 5) as background. 1. Build the complete map from Kubernetes to the Linux kernel. For each Kubernetes primitive in section 1, name the exact kernel subsystem and the `/proc`, `/sys` or CLI command that verifies it on the node. The primitives are Pod Sandbox, CPU, memory and PID requests and limits, Exclusive CPUs, Volumes, Images

Opening: AI stacks have one of the widest and least-audited supply chains in computing. A single training image can pull in a multi-gigabyte base image from a vendor, thousands of Python packages, and PyTorch and CUDA wheels. Some of those wheels compile custom CUDA extensions at install time. Pretrained models often arrive from hubs as pickles, and datasets may have been scraped from the Internet. Build scripts sometimes run `curl | bash`, GPU drivers and kernel modules run with kernel privileges, and notebooks may `pip install` whatever their users type. Those gaps have real consequences. A compromised dependency in a training image can exfiltrate proprietary data and model weights, or tamper with the model itself. A poisoned model file can execute code the moment it loads. A malicious package in a CI environment can steal cloud credentials.
Middle: Other options include the Sigstore policy-controller with `ClusterImagePolicy`, Ratify with Gatekeeper, Connaisseur, and cloud-native services such as Binary Authorization on GKE, ECR signing with Notation and Kyverno or Gatekeeper, and Azure Policy for Kubernetes with Notation.

Opening: Picture an inference request that reaches a model. It may pass through a gateway, a router, a retriever, a reranker, a vector store, a tool server and finally a model server. Every hop is a network call, and each one needs encryption, a verified identity, sensible retries and timeouts, and a way to send a slice of traffic to a new model version. Teams want the same things from every service, so they also want consistent metrics and traces across languages. They often want a rule such as "only the router may call the model server." A service mesh moves these concerns out of every application and into infrastructure.
Middle: Rate limiting can be local, using Envoy's local rate limit, or global, using a rate limit service. Either way it applies per client identity or per API key. For LLMs, token-based limits generally need the application or gateway to be aware of tokens.

Opening: Picture an on-call morning. A GPU platform has just been upgraded, and new training jobs no longer start. To find the cause, you need to know which custom resources exist and which controller acts on each one. Almost every AI-specific component on Kubernetes is a custom resource plus an operator. You will meet these resources. `ClusterPolicy` belongs to the GPU Operator, and `NicClusterPolicy` to the Network Operator. `NetworkAttachmentDefinition` comes from Multus, and `Workload` and `ClusterQueue` come from Kueue. Training runs use `PyTorchJob`, `MPIJob` and `JobSet`, while `RayCluster` and `RayJob` cover Ray. `InferenceService` comes from KServe, and `Application` from Argo. It also includes `Notebook`, `Certificate` and `ServiceMonitor`.
Middle: Code running inside a Pod works differently. The Pod has a service account, and Kubernetes places its token and certificate authority into the Pod as a volume. The API server is always reachable at a fixed DNS name, generally `kubernetes`. Because all the data is already in the Pod, no kubeconfig is needed. In Python you call `config.load_incluster_config()`. In Java you call `ClientBuilder.cluster().build()`. In .NET you call `KubernetesClientConfiguration.InClusterConfig()`.

Finally, explain how CEL rules enforce cross-field validation, such as `CordonAndDrain` requiring `maxUnavailableNodes`, and immutability with `self == oldSelf`. Opening: Suppose a controller pod crashes and its logs contain a Go stack trace. If you can read Go, that trace leads you straight into the source code. Kubernetes and most of its ecosystem are written in Go. That includes containerd, etcd, Prometheus, Terraform providers, the NVIDIA GPU Operator, device plugins, CSI drivers, Kueue, Argo and Cilium. Platform engineers who read Go can debug operator behaviour from source. They can patch or extend components. They can also write small controllers and CLI tools, such as a node-health remediator, a quota reporter or an admission webhook.
Middle: The lock is a Lease in the `platform` namespace, and each replica identifies itself with its hostname. `RunOrDie` runs the work only while this replica holds the lease. `ReleaseOnCancel` lets the replica give the lease up when its context is cancelled. If the replica loses leadership, it logs the event and exits, so a restart can rejoin the election cleanly.
Exercise sample: 1. Start with the Go type system, pointer versus value receivers, and error wrapping (`%w`, `errors.Is`/`As`, `k8s.io/apimachinery/pkg/api/errors`). Explain why a method with a pointer receiver (`func (r *Router) Trim()`) does not satisfy an interface when you pass it by value, as Network Programmability and Automation, Chapter 7, describes. Opening: A GPU node goes bad late at night, and something has to notice it, stop new work from landing on it and report the fault. Very often that something is an operator. GPU clusters are run by operators: the NVIDIA GPU Operator, Network Operator, Kueue, the Slurm and Ray operators, and node-remediation controllers. Being able to write a small operator, and to read the large ones in 5.33, is what separates a platform engineer from someone who only applies YAML. This chapter teaches controller-runtime, the library underneath kubebuilder and Operator SDK. It also covers the design rules that keep an operator safe on an expensive, shared fleet. 5.32 then applies these ideas to a concrete project, so decide early which operators your own cluster depends on. Suppose you are asked to build a controller that cordons unhealthy GPU nodes. The chapters before this one give you the foundation for that work. You should have covered the API and etcd in 5.5, controllers in 5.6, CRDs and operator concepts in 5.29,
Middle: Here is a defaulting example. You set `deploymentTier` to `dev` when it is missing. `dev` is the most common and least expensive choice. Defaulting also keeps old manifests working. When you add a new field, the default fills the gap for resources that never set it.

Opening: Picture a GPU that starts throwing errors while its node stays schedulable. The scheduler keeps sending work to that node, and nothing announces the problem. Xid errors, ECC faults, NVLink faults and thermal throttling can all look like this. A failing GPU that stays in the pool silently ruins training jobs. A multi-hundred-GPU job may restart, or worse, slow down because one rank is degraded. Large operators automate remediation to deal with this. The dangerous failure then becomes the remediator itself. A bad signal can mark every node unhealthy and drain the fleet. This project builds the small, safe version of that controller. Its decisions are pure and tested. Its destructive actions are budgeted. It only undoes what it did itself, and every refusal is visible.
Middle: Two rows ask you to make a decision. The detector crash row leaves open whether you want a safeguard timeout. The `Ignore` row asks whether that mode should release cordons. Decide both questions and write down the answers.

Opening: Suppose a new GPU node joins the cluster and never becomes schedulable. The answer is almost always in how the GPU Operator sequences its operands: driver, container toolkit, device plugin, validator, DCGM exporter, GFD and MIG manager. When RDMA fails to appear on a Pod, look at the Network Operator's OFED, device-plugin and secondary-network states. Documentation tells you what to configure. The source tells you what the controller will actually do: in what order, gated by which label, and what "ready" means. An engineer who can read these repos can diagnose stuck rollouts, understand upgrade behaviour, answer interview questions about design, and write comparable controllers, as in 5.31 and 5.32. This chapter is a map and a method, not a line-by-line commentary. The code changes every release.
Middle: The not-found path does cleanup. When the policy is deleted, the primary object is gone and nothing else would remove the `mofed.wait` and NIC-configuration wait labels from the nodes. The controller therefore clears them. This replaces a finalizer for node-label cleanup.

Opening: Suppose someone asks you to stand up GPU capacity on GKE. You will need to know how the platform fits together before you touch a single setting. Google Cloud is one of the three clouds this role touches, alongside CoreWeave and Nebius. GKE is the managed Kubernetes service most teams meet first. Its GPU support combines a managed control plane, managed drivers, several ways to consume capacity (on-demand, reservations, Spot, and flex-start), and specialised networking for large training. The Kubernetes concepts are the same as on bare metal. Device plugins are covered in 5.8, scheduling in 5.9, and GPU on Kubernetes in Part 11. The operational levers are different, though. Quota is usually the first blocker, and node pools are the unit of hardware. The managed driver and networking stack take over parts of what the GPU Operator and Network Operator do elsewhere, as covered in 5.33.
Middle: A single-node job runs fine on the default VPC networking. Multi-node training is different, because inter-node bandwidth and latency dominate the run, as covered in Part 12. On GKE several pieces are involved. The details depend on the machine family and change between generations, so treat the list below as a map and read the current guide for your machine shape.

Opening: Picture an AI platform whose reserved GPUs sit in one place, whose Spot capacity sits in another, and whose on-premises DGX pool sits in a third. Platforms like this rarely stay on one cluster. Capacity comes from several regions and providers, including a neocloud such as CoreWeave or Nebius. Data sits in different places, and teams need isolation. A single huge cluster is also a single blast radius. Every extra cluster, though, multiplies the operational work: upgrades, policy, secrets, observability and networking. This chapter covers when to split, how to keep a fleet consistent, how work is placed across clusters, and what breaks.
Middle: If you use version control and tags instead, the parameters for each region sit at the root. You add a new tag for each region. The file contents at that tag then deploy to that region. Decide on the layout before you add the second region, so every region follows the same pattern.

Opening: Walk onto the data center floor where a large language model is training, and you are standing inside a supercomputer. Thousands of GPUs hum inside liquid-cooled racks, linked by a dedicated InfiniBand or RoCE fabric. Those accelerators read training shards from a parallel file system, and they take orders from a central batch scheduler. Almost every tool and habit that keeps such a cluster productive comes straight from high-performance computing. Slurm queues, collective communications, environment modules, pre-job health checks, node draining, and fair-share accounting were forged over decades by HPC engineers. Understanding what traditional HPC measures, and why a supercomputer is a single synchronized complex, lets you diagnose cluster-wide slowdowns. It also lets you speak with authority in production war rooms. You need Part 1 for Linux administration and Part 2 for networking basics. When you finish this chapter, you will be able to define a supercomputer from the operator's point of view. It is a single synchronized complex, not a price tag or a list rank. You will explain
Middle: Fog computing, also called fog networking or fogging, is a decentralized structure. It sits between data-producing devices, such as Internet of Things (IoT) devices, and the cloud. It extends the cloud architecture to the edge of the network, so the cloud is involved less. Moving processing units nearer the end devices makes better use of computing power, and it shortens task execution and processing time. Fog pushes intelligence down to the local network. Edge devices and gateways do the work. Data flows from the endpoints to a gateway for processing, and then flows back again.

Opening: Every AI cluster, whether it runs Slurm, Kubernetes, or both, is built from the same handful of node roles and networks. Picture a researcher who launches a heavy preprocessing script on the head node, or a remote IDE plugin that paralyzes a shared login server. Production incidents almost always trace back to the wrong workload running on the wrong node, or across the wrong network. An NFS service co-located on a compute node starves an AllReduce collective, and a management network that shares a switch with compute traffic causes the same kind of trouble. Knowing the standard anatomy lets you read an architecture diagram in one pass, ask the right questions in a design review, and place new services where they belong. It also gives you the working vocabulary of NVIDIA Base Command Manager, Slurm, and cluster procurement specifications, which the book covers in chapters 6.7 onwards and Part 8.
Middle: Finally, decide where the data lives. Plan long-term storage, scratch storage, and backups, and separate hot data from cold data. Users may also need pre- and postprocessing, so ask how quickly that data must reach the compute nodes. *Supercomputers for Linux SysAdmins* suggests reading its chapter on network file systems before you make the final storage decision.
Exercise sample: 1. Size the switch ports and power budget for 32 GPU compute nodes, each with 8 GPUs and 10.0 kW, plus 2 login nodes and 2 head nodes. A communication cost model (Hockney model and ring AllReduce) / 9. Load balance, domain decomposition, and halo exchanges / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: On Monday morning a research team asks why doubling their Slurm allocation from 64 GPUs to 128 GPUs cut their training epoch time by only 25 percent. The same mathematical laws that High-Performance Computing engineers have used for fifty years explain it. Those laws hold whether a parallel job scales from 8 GPUs to 8,000 GPUs. Amdahl's law shows why even a tiny unparallelized dataloader or checkpoint step caps your maximum speedup. Strong and weak scaling separate two goals. One is finishing a fixed training run faster. The other is growing the global batch size or model size along with the cluster. Message passing is what MPI and NCCL do across nodes. Shared memory is what OpenMP threads do on a CPU socket, or what GPUs do across an NVLink domain. If you know these concepts and can spot the core numerical libraries and parallel debuggers in user job logs, you can diagnose scaling walls. You can also separate userland bugs from cluster hardware faults.
Middle: Load balance adds a second problem. When subdomains carry unequal work, such as adaptive meshes or clusters of particles, static partitioning leaves ranks waiting at barriers. Those cases call for dynamic load balancing, or for graph partitioners such as METIS and ParMETIS. Distributed LLM training faces the same surface-to-volume and load-balance trade-offs. They show up in Tensor Parallelism, Pipeline Parallelism micro-batch bubbles, and Mixture-of-Experts token routing, which Part 14 covers.
Exercise sample: The exercises reuse the functions and measurements from the chapter, so you can check each answer against the text. 1. Take the 8-core strong-scaling run from Section 5. Its speedups are 1.85, 2.84, and 3.37 on 2, 4, and 8 workers. Use `karp_flatt()` to compute the experimental serial fraction for each, which should give 0.083, 0.135, and 0.196. Then explain why

Lab: test MCA precedence, `NCCL_SOCKET_IFNAME` matching, and single-rank MPI / 12. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a 64-node training job submitted on Friday afternoon. Slurm allocates the 512 GPUs right away, and the job status flips to running. Then nothing happens. For two hours every GPU sits at zero percent utilization while the job hangs silently inside initialization, and it burns thousands of dollars of compute quota. MPI is the foundational launch, bootstrap, and message-passing substrate of high-performance computing. It is also still the process-management and benchmarking backbone of distributed AI clusters. Even when PyTorch uses NCCL for high-bandwidth GPU collectives, jobs are regularly launched via Slurm with PMIx or `mpirun`. The jobs exchange rank and endpoint identity through MPI-style rendezvous. They validate fabric health with MPI-linked benchmarks such as the OSU Micro-Benchmarks and `nccl-tests`.
Middle: A `^` in front of a component list means exclude these. For example, in Open MPI 4 you can export `OMPI_MCA_btl=^tcp,self,openib`. Or you can pass `--mca btl_tcp_if_exclude lo,docker0` to stop Open MPI from routing over unwanted interfaces.

Opening: Every week on a shared cluster, users open tickets. One asks why their CPU preprocessing code runs twenty times slower than expected. Another asks why a freshly compiled binary crashes with `Illegal instruction` on half the nodes. A third asks why turning on `-O3` did not speed up a reduction loop. All three mysteries live inside the node-level compiler and threading layer. That layer decides whether a loop runs at full SIMD vector width. It decides whether threads sit on the same NUMA socket that owns their memory pages. It also decides whether an optimization flag reorders floating-point addition, and whether an executable emits AVX-512 instructions on an AVX2 node. Together these choices govern every host-side workload on an AI cluster.
Middle: Start with a single source file. `gcc myprog.c` produces `a.out`, unless you name the output with `-o myexe`. Multi-file projects take two steps. First, compile each `.c` or `.f90` file into a `.o` object file with `gcc -c *.c`. Then link the objects with libraries, for example `gcc -o myexe *.o -lm`. Here `-lzzz` links `libzzz.so`, `-I` adds header search paths, and `-L` adds library search paths. Keep these three cross-language and linker rules in mind:

Opening: Picture a shared GPU cluster that serves fifty researchers. Group A needs GCC 10.4, Open MPI 4.1, and CUDA 11.8 for a legacy molecular dynamics code. Group B needs GCC 15.1, Open MPI 5.0.7, and CUDA 12.6 to compile custom FlashAttention kernels for a foundation model. Suppose you install one global version into `/usr/bin` and `/usr/lib64`. Then half your users cannot work. Now suppose you tell everyone to edit their own `~/.bashrc` and install compilers into their home directories. Users will end up mixing a GCC 15 binary with a GCC 10 standard C++ library, or with an incompatible MPI runtime. Your ticket queue then fills with segmentation faults.
Middle: Suppose you want the finished system disk copied to the other nodes. One method is to connect each new disk to the reference node, one at a time, and copy the disk across. This is safe only if the disk and its controller support hot swap. Otherwise, connect the disk only while the node is powered off.

Opening: Suppose the controller of a GPU cluster dies in the middle of a training run. Which jobs keep running, and which ones are lost? Interviewers ask questions like these, and the answers depend on Slurm. Almost every large GPU cluster that is not Kubernetes-first runs Slurm. NVIDIA DGX SuperPOD reference designs ship with it, and BCM can deploy it for you. The GPU clouds that sell raw clusters to AI labs, such as CoreWeave, Nebius, Lambda, DGX Cloud and others, expose it to customers as the default way to run training jobs. Job descriptions for those roles list Slurm, InfiniBand and NCCL together as hard requirements. The interviews go straight to internals. Interviewers ask what runs where, what happens when the controller dies, how accounting limits actually get enforced, and why a node is `drain`ed. You cannot answer those questions by memorising `sbatch` flags. You need a clear model of the parts and how they talk to each other, and you need to have installed one at least once.
Middle: The data model is an **association tree**. Users belong to **accounts**, and accounts can be nested. An **association** is a tuple of cluster, account, user and optionally partition. Policy such as limits and shares attaches to associations and accounts. Once enforcement is on, every user must have at least one association before they can submit jobs.

Opening: Most "my GPU job is behaving strangely" tickets on an AI cluster can be traced to a handful of lines in `slurm.conf` and `cgroup.conf`. Picture a researcher who requests 2 GPUs on an 8-GPU node. They still see all 8 GPUs, because `ConstrainDevices=yes` was left out. Another job gets killed by the node kernel's OOM killer. That takes down three neighboring jobs on the same server, because `SelectTypeParameters` omitted `_Memory`. A newly provisioned DGX node may register with the controller and flip straight to `drain` (`invalid`). The cause is that `RealMemory` in `slurm.conf` was set higher than the memory the kernel reports after BIOS reservations. Getting these settings right is what turns a fragile cluster into a predictable production appliance. That covers node definitions, partition policies, `cons_tres` resource tracking, and cgroup containment. It also covers switch topology, which runs through `topology.conf` or `topology.yaml` and `SwitchAsNodeRank`.
Middle: Look closely at the `topology/tree` example. Even-numbered nodes, n0, n2, n4, and n6, sit on leaf switch s0. Odd-numbered nodes, n1, n3, n5, and n7, sit on leaf switch s1. Suppose a job receives n0, n1, n2, and n3. Slurm's default alphabetical ordering gives Rank 0 to n0 on s0, Rank 1 to n1 on s1, Rank 2 to n2 on s0, and Rank 3 to n3 on s1. Each nearest-neighbor pair, `0 <-> 1`, `1 <-> 2`, and `2 <-> 3`, now crosses the top spine switch.

Opening: A GPU cluster is shared and expensive, so somebody has to decide who gets it next. That decision is the scheduler's policy, and it is where organisational choices become code. The policy decides who gets priority before a deadline. It decides how a team that bought 64 GPUs is guaranteed them, and whether idle capacity can be borrowed. It also decides how one user is stopped from queueing 10,000 jobs, and how much each group is charged. Poor policy produces two classic failures. Large or low-priority jobs can starve and never run. Or limits and reservations are set wrongly, so GPUs sit idle while jobs wait and utilisation drops. This chapter explains the mechanisms well enough for you to design a policy, and to explain to a user why their job is pending.
Middle: Look at bob first. He has more usage than dave, yet he ranks below him, because biology as an account outranks physics. Within physics, bob has less usage than alice, so he ranks above her.

Opening: Suppose a job asks for one GPU and then sees all 8 GPUs on the node. Or two jobs land on the same GPU and both slow to a crawl. GPU scheduling is the main reason Slurm configuration for AI differs from classic HPC. Users must get exactly the GPUs they asked for, close to the right CPU cores and memory, isolated from other jobs, and counted in accounting. When any of these fails, the symptoms are easy to miss. Training can run 30 percent slower because its CPU threads sit on the wrong NUMA node. A 4-GPU job can also wait forever, because no node can satisfy its request. Finish 6.8 and 6.9 before you start. Parts 9 and 10 cover the GPU topology basics you need here, which are NVLink, PCIe and NUMA. When you have worked through this chapter, you should be able to set up GPUs as GRES, either by hand or with AutoDetect, including their types, device files, `Cores` and `Links`. You can check what
Middle: `gres/shard` lets several jobs take turns on a GPU. The docs say that "by default it does not fence the processes running on the GPU nor constrain its device memory." You declare it as `Gres=gpu:2,shard:64`, which gives 32 shards per GPU. Sharding suits homogeneous small jobs. The docs recommend limiting shards per node to the maximum number of simultaneous jobs. Newer versions add opt-in shard device-memory enforcement, which the same docs page describes.

Allocations, batch jobs and steps / The 6-stage Slurm job life cycle (`SCL` Chapter 15) / Simulating a multi-node GPU cluster on a single physical machine (`DAI` Chapter 8) / 2. A batch script / Summary of primary `sbatch` / `srun` keys (`SCL` Chapter 15 `Table 15-1`) / Rank-specific logging vs interleaved stdout (`DAI` Chapter 8) / Environment variables / Distributed-training rendezvous from Slurm variables / 3. srun / MPI and launching / 4. Interactive jobs / 5. Job arrays / 2D hyperparameter grid search with job arrays (`DAI` Chapter 8) / 6. Dependencies and pipelines / 7. Time limits, signals, checkpoints, requeue / 8. Monitoring, modifying, cancelling / 9. A linter for batch scripts / 10. Submission patterns for AI workloads / 11. Common mistakes / 12. Lab (self-checking linter lab tested; cluster parts untested) / 13. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: The user-facing interface to a GPU cluster is a handful of commands. Researchers lose hours to avoidable mistakes, such as these. A multi-node script runs everything on one node. An array of 1,000 jobs has no throttle, so it floods the file system. A requeued job overwrites its own log. A training run is killed at the time limit without a checkpoint. And `mpirun` is launched inside a batch script without binding. Operators write the documentation, templates, `job_submit` plugins and wrapper scripts that prevent these, and they answer the tickets when the mistakes happen anyway. This chapter gives you the working vocabulary for both sides. A script that works on one machine can behave very differently on a cluster. The reason is almost always where each command runs. Slurm separates three ideas, and you need all three to predict what a script will do.
Middle: You can combine conditions with a comma, which means all must hold, or with a question mark, which means any one is enough. Use only one separator type in each expression. For example, `afterok:20:21,afterany:23` uses commas, and `afterok:20:21?afterany:23` uses question marks.
Exercise sample: Work through these exercises on your own machine, and explain each answer in your own words. 1. Run `python3 code/part06/sbatch_lint_lab.py`, then work with the linter in `code/part06/sbatch_lint.py`. Explain the four hard ERRORs that the linter catches on `bad.sbatch`: the missing `#!` shebang, the mutually exclusive `--mem` and `--mem-per-gpu`, the incompatible `--cpus-per-gpu` and `--cpus-per-task`, and `--gpus-per-task` without an explicit task count. Then explain why `--requeue` requires `--open-mode=append` or `%j` in `--output`. 2. Simulate a 2-node GPU cluster on one workstation, following *Distributed AI Systems*, Chapter 8. Write the `slurm.conf` and `gres.conf`

Opening: Picture a 64-GPU job that hangs at start, a job that scales badly, or a job where one rank crashed and the other ranks sat waiting for six hours. Most tickets like these are launch-layer problems, not model problems. The rendezvous address may be wrong. Ranks may sit on the wrong NUMA node, which is the group of cores and memory attached to one CPU socket. NCCL may choose the management NIC, a `-K` may be missing, or library versions may not match. An engineer who can read a launch script and predict how it will fail is worth a great deal on an AI cluster. You need 6.4, 6.10 and 6.11, along with the basics of Part 10 and Part 12, which cover NCCL and the fabric. When you finish this chapter, you should be able to do the following.
Middle: Work from small to large. If you can reproduce the problem on 1 node, try 2 nodes next. Run `nccl-tests` with `srun` on the same allocation, using `all_reduce_perf -b 8 -e 8G -f 2 -g 1` (untested here). That separates fabric problems from application problems, covered in 6.28. Compare the result with the baseline bus bandwidth for your node type, and bisect by node subsets.

### part-06-hpc-slurm/13-containers-on-slurm-enroot-and-pyxis.md
Title: 6.13 Containers on Slurm: enroot and pyxis | words 6649 | code blocks 5
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Opening: Almost every serious GPU cluster runs its AI jobs from container images, such as NGC PyTorch or NeMo images, vendor stacks and team images. The framework, the CUDA libraries, NCCL and the Python packages must match each other. You cannot install all of them by hand on every node. On Slurm clusters, the reference solution is a pair from NVIDIA. enroot is an unprivileged container runtime. pyxis is a Slurm plugin that exposes enroot through `srun`. You will find this pair on DGX SuperPOD-style systems and on many neoclouds.
Middle: Images come from several sources: NGC (`nvcr.io#nvidia/...`, which needs an API key for some images), Docker Hub, GitHub Container Registry and private registries. You build images from NGC bases with a normal Docker, Podman or Buildah build, outside the cluster. Then you push the image to a registry and import it into squashfs.

Write the `ENROOT_RUNTIME_PATH`, `ENROOT_DATA_PATH`, `ENROOT_TEMP_PATH` and `ENROOT_CACHE_PATH` settings, so that container roots unpack at NVMe

### part-06-hpc-slurm/14-slurm-operations-prolog-epilog-health-checks-drain-reason-codes-troubl.md
Title: 6.14 Slurm operations: prolog, epilog, health checks, drain, reason codes, troubleshooting | words 7694 | code blocks 5
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Prolog and epilog / What to put in them / 2. Health checks / Design rules / A tested example / 3. Node lifecycle / 4. Unkillable steps and stuck jobs / 5. Logs and diagnostics / 6. Troubleshooting playbook / 6.1 GPU hardware and cross-layer failure catalog (Volume 20) / 7. Operational practices / Maintenance and user reservations (`scontrol` reservation lifecycle and flags) / 8. Lab (self-checking stub test run; live cluster parts untested) / 9. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: Suppose one node in a job that spans hundreds of GPUs starts to fail. A GPU falls off the bus, a link flaps, a mount goes stale, or the node runs out of `/tmp`. In a large GPU cluster something is always broken, so the real question is how you respond. A well-run cluster removes bad nodes from the pool quickly, cleans up after every job, and lets an operator say exactly why a job failed. One sick node wastes the whole job, which is why these habits matter so much at scale. The tools for this work are prolog and epilog scripts, a periodic health check, automatic draining with a reason, and disciplined diagnosis from logs. Slurm can run your own scripts at job boundaries. A prolog runs on a node before the work starts, and an epilog runs after the allocation is released. Before you write either one, you need to know which machine runs it, as which user, and at which moment. The table is a lookup
Middle: Follow the same method every time, so you do not skip a step. State the symptom exactly, with the job ID, the node and the time. Read `scontrol show` for the object involved, and find the first error in the relevant log near that time. Check the layer below, such as node health, network, storage or time. Change one thing at a time. Finally, record the cause and the fix in the ticket, and update the health check so the next occurrence is caught automatically.
Exercise sample: 1. Automate node health gating with stub binaries. Run `python3 code/part06/node_health_lab.py`, then read `code/part06/node_health.sh` and `node_health_lab.py` so you can see how `run_node_health_with_stubs(healthy=False)` injects mock `nvidia-smi` (`7` GPUs vs `EXPECT_GPUS=8`), `dmesg` (`Xid 79`), `df` (`14462 MB` vs `MIN_TMP_MB=20480`), `mountpoint` (`/scratch` unmounted) and `ibstat` (`State: Down`) binaries. Confirm that `node_health.sh` builds a single truncated `Reason` string (`cut -c1-200`) and exits non-zero. 2. Decide where you would place each task, in `Prolog`, `Epilog`, or `HealthCheckProgram` (`ANY,CYCLE`), and explain why: (a) creating `/tmp/slurm-$SLURM_JOB_ID`, (b) checking `dmesg` for new `Xid` errors after a job finishes

Title: 6.15 Slurm high availability, upgrades and scaling | words 6167 | code blocks 4

Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: Upgrades are mandatory too, for security fixes and for GPU features such as new GRES autodetect values or `MAX_TRES_GRES` billing. They carry real risk, because Slurm state and the accounting database are migrated in place and cannot be rolled back. Clusters also keep growing, to tens of thousands of GPUs and millions of short jobs for inference and evaluation. The pieces of the cluster are `slurmctld` (the scheduler, which holds the state), `slurmdbd` (accounting) with its database, `slurmd` on each node, `munge`, the shared storage behind `StateSaveLocation`, and name services. Each one fails differently, so learn the damage each failure causes before you design redundancy.
Middle: Notice the rollback row: configuration changes roll back easily, but an upgrade does not. Your controller HA is also only as strong as the services beneath it, so DNS, NTP or chrony, LDAP and NFS need the same HA level. Put the quarterly failover drill on the calendar now.

Finally, describe what happens if you attempt an out-of-window upgrade directly. 2. Write the pre-upgrade checklist and the ordered execution plan for an HA Slurm cluster. Opening: Picture two engineering teams that share one AI cluster. Researchers and HPC veterans want Slurm, with its gang-scheduled multi-node jobs, fair-share policy, accounting and `sbatch`. Platform teams want Kubernetes, with declarative infrastructure, GitOps, self-healing, multi-tenant services and one control plane for training, inference and data services. Neoclouds such as CoreWeave and Nebius, along with large AI labs, run Kubernetes everywhere, so they also need Slurm on top of it. Knowing how that stack is built, and what it costs, matters directly for roles at those companies. The NVIDIA, CoreWeave and Nebius job families all touch it. This chapter builds on 6.7 to 6.15, 5.29 and 5.31 to 5.33, which cover custom resource definitions (CRDs), operators and how to read operator code. It also builds on 5.35, which covers multicluster setups. When you finish, you should be able to do four things:
Middle: Worker pods should request whole nodes' GPUs, CPUs and memory, with `Guaranteed` QoS, the `static` CPU manager policy and the topology manager. That way Kubernetes does not oversubscribe or throttle them; see 5.9 and 5.11.

Next, check that an admin drain (`Reason="Xid79 GPU3 ticket INC123"`) is never overwritten or cleared when the Kubernetes node is uncordoned. Last, confirm that an operator-owned drain (`Reason="slurm-operator: node-cordoned"`) resumes to `IDLE` on uncordon by itself. 2. ### part-06-hpc-slurm/17-other-schedulers-pbs-torque-lsf-and-flux.md
Title: 6.17 Other schedulers: PBS, Torque, LSF and Flux | words 6506 | code blocks 8
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. What all batch schedulers share / 2. PBS: OpenPBS and PBS Professional / Request model: select and place / Torque / Strengths and weaknesses / Setting up Torque and customising its scheduler / 3. IBM Spectrum LSF (general knowledge, unverified) / 4. Flux / 5. Command and concept map / 6. A tested translator / 7. Migration plan (any scheduler to Slurm) / 8. Mixed environments and bridges / 9. Lab (self-checking converter lab tested; live scheduler installs untested) / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: Slurm runs most GPU training clusters, but you will not always work on one. A university or national lab may run PBS Professional or OpenPBS. A financial or EDA shop may run IBM Spectrum LSF, and LLNL-lineage systems run Flux. Some legacy clusters still run Torque. Customers often send you scripts written for another syntax. The concepts these schedulers share are the same, so learning them once makes it possible to translate between them. That skill matters when a site moves to Slurm or to a Kubernetes-based stack, which is a common migration project, and it comes up in interviews. When a job sits in a queue, the first question is which scheduler is holding it. Learn the shared parts once, and you can place any new scheduler quickly. Every batch scheduler has the same basic parts. Jobs wait in a queue, or a partition in Slurm terms. A server or controller holds the state. Each compute node runs a per-node daemon: MOM in PBS, `sbatchd` in LSF, a broker
Middle: The key idea is that every job can itself be a Flux instance, called a subinstance. `flux batch SCRIPT` "submits SCRIPT to run as the initial program of a Flux subinstance". When the script exits, the subinstance ends and its resources return to the enclosing instance. Commands inside the script, such as `flux run` and `flux submit`, refer to the subinstance. `flux proxy JOBID` connects a shell to a running subinstance, and `flux uptime` shows `depth` and `size`. Hierarchical scheduling lets users run their own scheduler policy and millions of tasks inside an allocation, without loading the system scheduler. That makes it a strength for ensemble and AI-workflow workloads.
Exercise sample: Work through these exercises in order. Each one asks you to explain or write something specific. 1. Start with the converter `code/part06/pbs2slurm.py` and its self-checking lab, `code/part06/pbs2slurm_lab.py`. Run the lab with `python3 code/part06/pbs2slurm_lab.py`. Then explain why `pbs2slurm.py` divides ncpus (64) by mpiprocs (8) to produce `#SBATCH --cpus-per-task=8`. Next, explain how it translates `-l place=scatter:excl`, `-J 0-9%3` and `-m abe`. Finally, explain why custom resources (`-l software=matlab`) and heterogeneous `+` chunks are emitted as `# TODO` comments for manual review. 2. Configure Torque queues, server parameters and the `pbs_mom` epilogue, using

Title: 6.18 Parallel storage concepts and the limits of NFS | words 7406 | code blocks 10
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Why shared storage / 2. NFS: what it is and where it ends / NFS server and mount essentials (general practice, untested here) / Minimal NFS server and client steps from the book / 3. Time synchronisation / `ntpd.conf` / `openntpd`, `chrony`, `ntpdate`, and `systemd-timesyncd` details / 4. Metadata, data and the parallel file system idea / 5. I/O patterns and what breaks / 6. Arithmetic you should be able to do / 7. A micro-benchmark and its lessons / 8. Choosing for an AI cluster (preview of Part 13) / 9. Lab / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a 4,096-GPU job that reaches the end of a training step and has to write a checkpoint. Every GPU waits while the write finishes. If the storage is slow, the job stalls for minutes at every checkpoint, and the GPUs sit idle the whole time. Training reads datasets, writes checkpoints and logs, and loads Python environments, all from shared storage. A different failure looks just as bad. A data loader starves for files, or a thousand ranks import Python packages at the same moment and bring the metadata server to its knees. Storage design is one of the highest-leverage and most misunderstood parts of an AI cluster.
Middle: A client first asks the metadata service where a file's data lives. That answer is the layout. The client then talks directly to many data servers in parallel, so bandwidth grows with the number of data servers and clients.
Exercise sample: 1. Run `python3 code/part06/iomix_lab.py`. Then use `ckpt_bytes(params, 14)`, `need_gbs(size, 60)` and `overhead(size, 10, 3600)`, which come from `code/part06/ckpt_math.py`, to check two results that you can verify yourself. First, a `70B` mixed-precision Adam checkpoint of `0.98 TB` needs `16 GB/s` to write in 60 seconds. On a `10 GB/s` NFS server it wastes `98 s`, which is `2.7%` of an hour. Second, a `405B` checkpoint of `5.67 TB` needs `94 GB/s`. At `10 GB/s` it wastes `567 s`, or `15.8%` of every hour. 2. Run `python3 code/part06/iomix.py --mb 64 --files 2000`.

### part-06-hpc-slurm/19-lustre-architecture-and-operation.md
Title: 6.19 Lustre architecture and operation | words 7661 | code blocks 17
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Architecture / 2. Creating a file system / Step-by-step Lustre creation (Installing Lustre (No HA) and HA), LNET port `988`, and OSS RAM sizing / Non-HA install order from the book / 3. Striping / The raid0 layout / Defaults and setting / Progressive File Layouts (PFL) and other layouts / Pools / Directory striping and DNE / 4. Quotas and capacity / 5. Failure and recovery model / 6. Monitoring and tuning / Diagnosing bottlenecks / 7. AI-specific guidance / 8. Lab / 9. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a "storage is slow" ticket landing on your desk in the middle of a training run. Lustre is the most widely deployed parallel file system in HPC, and many large AI training sites use it too. It underlies several vendor and cloud services. Its strengths are enormous aggregate bandwidth and a POSIX interface. Those strengths come with sharp edges. Striping defaults can cap the bandwidth of a big checkpoint at one disk. A metadata service can be overwhelmed by a data loader. Failures can look like hung nodes. Operators who understand striping, metadata and the management tools can prevent and diagnose most "storage is slow" tickets.
Middle: For example, `lfs quota -u alice /lustre` shows one user's usage. `lfs setquota -u alice -b 9T -B 10T -i 1M -I 1100K /lustre` sets soft and hard limits for blocks and inodes. The `-g` option covers groups and `-p` covers projects. Default quotas use `-U`, `-G` and `-P`. Pool-specific quotas use `--pool`, grace time uses `-t`, and `--delete` and `--reset` remove or clear limits.

Then explain why a 1 GiB file with `stripe_size=1 MiB` and `stripe_count=4` alternates 1 MiB chunks across OST slots `[0, 1, 2, 3, 0, 1, 2, 3]`, which gives 256 MiB per OST. Title: 6.20 BeeGFS, GPFS, WekaFS and VAST | words 6492 | code blocks 2
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. A common frame / 2. BeeGFS / 3. IBM Storage Scale (GPFS) / 4. PanFS (Panasas, now VDURA) / 5. WEKA (WekaFS) and other distributed filesystems (`SCL` Chapter 9) / Other Linux distributed filesystems, `CernVM-FS`, and `autofs` (`SCL` Chapter 9) / 6. VAST Data / 7. Comparison / 8. Selecting and validating / 8.1 The AI data path: GPUDirect Storage (`GDS`) and NVMe staging (Volume 15) / 9. Lab (self-checking efficiency & GDS lab tested; VM cluster parts untested) / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: You will rarely choose a file system from scratch. More often someone will ask you to operate it, size it or compare it. A neocloud might offer WEKA or VAST, a national lab might run Lustre or GPFS, a university might run BeeGFS, and an appliance vendor might ship PanFS, now called VDURA. Each system makes different trade-offs between metadata scaling, small-file performance, operational complexity, POSIX semantics, cost and ecosystem. The 6.19 chapter covered Lustre in depth. This chapter gives you the architecture, strengths and pitfalls of the alternatives, along with the questions to ask about each one. Keep the questions in section 1 in mind as you read, because they apply to every system here. Part 13, on AI storage, returns to these systems for AI pipelines, checkpointing and GPUDirect Storage.
Middle: For specialised read-only roles, chapter 9 highlights two tools. The first is CernVM-FS, written CVMFS for short, at `https://cvmfs.readthedocs.io/en/stable/index.html`. It is an HTTP-based, aggressively cached, read-only file system. It suits software trees, local container images and static reference datasets that must reach thousands of nodes. Read-only NFS mounts of `/home` on compute nodes also fit, when jobs write only to `/scratch`.

### part-06-hpc-slurm/21-parallel-i-o-and-checkpoint-patterns.md
Title: 6.21 Parallel I/O and checkpoint patterns | words 10578 | code blocks 5
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Parallel I/O in HPC: MPI-IO and HDF5 / Alignment rules of thumb / 2. Checkpoint layouts / Tested: sharded checkpoint and resharding / Tiering and asynchrony / Driver-level transparent GPU state checkpointing (`cuda-checkpoint` and `CRIU`) / 3. Crash-safe commits (tested) / 4. Restart and validation / 5. Data loading / Sizing storage for every GPU in the job / Sequential and random reads / Workers, pinned memory, and overlap / Finding a hidden loader bottleneck / Monitoring storage I/O / GPUDirect Storage: what it changes and what it does not / DeepSeek's 3FS: storage built for random reads / Using the right library for each transfer / Preparing LLM datasets offline with NeMo Curator / Decoding images and video on the GPU with DALI / Keeping the pipeline full / 6. Performance experiments (cluster, untested) / 7. Lab / 8. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: Suppose a node fails halfway through a long training run. How much work you lose depends on how the job saves its state. Training is a long series of fast compute phases, punctuated by I/O: reading data, and periodically writing state. Writing state periodically means a failure costs minutes rather than days. Checkpoint design decides several things for you. It sets how much GPU time each save consumes and how quickly a run recovers after a failure. It also decides whether a crash can corrupt the only good checkpoint, and whether you can resume on a different number of GPUs. The HPC world solved the shared-file and collective-write problems with MPI-IO and HDF5. AI frameworks re-implemented those ideas through sharded distributed checkpoints and asynchronous staging. This chapter covers both, and it gives you tested building blocks.
Middle: Deep learning frameworks give you high-level APIs for loading and preprocessing data. Those APIs reward tuning, so treat their defaults as a starting point. This section covers general strategies. The later sections on DALI and NeMo Curator cover NVIDIA's tools for advanced data pipeline management.

### part-06-hpc-slurm/22-provisioning-with-pxe-ipxe-dhcp-tftp-and-image-management.md
Title: 6.22 Provisioning with PXE, iPXE, DHCP, TFTP and image management | words 8052 | code blocks 6
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. The boot chain / 2. Network boot protocols / DHCP / TFTP and PXE / iPXE / UEFI HTTP Boot / 3.
