Cluster managers built on this foundation / Out-of-the-box stacks: when they fit / ROCKS: deciding and installing / PelicanHPC: a temporary cluster from a LiveCD / 8. Boot triage / 9. Lab / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a GPU node that fails and has to be replaced. The new machine must come up with the right OS image, drivers, firmware settings, name and identity, and it must do so within minutes. Nobody should have to touch a keyboard. A GPU cluster has hundreds to thousands of nodes, and each one must be installed, reinstalled, patched and replaced this way. That makes provisioning a system in its own right, with its own ways to fail. You can run into DHCP misconfiguration, boot storms, image drift or mismatched firmware modes. Several tools build on the mechanisms in this chapter. NVIDIA Base Command Manager (Part 8), xCAT and Warewulf (6.23), and MAAS and Foreman all rely on them. Learn the mechanisms first, because each of those tools is built on them.
Middle: Use the `Autopartition` option to partition the head node disk. You can partition by hand, but the automatic option is the safer default. Watch the network card step closely. The installer picks which card is internal and which is external, and its choice may differ from yours. For the internal network, select manual mode by clearing `configure using DHCP`.

Then explain how `dnsmasq_conf()` uses `dhcp-match=set:ipxe,175` and `option:client-arch` (`7` and `9`) to chain-load `ipxe.efi` / `undionly.kpxe` into `http://10.10.0.1:8080/boot.ipxe`. 2. Warewulf 4 / Architecture / Commands (from the quick reference) / 4. Confluent, MAAS, Foreman (general knowledge, verify) / 5. NVIDIA Base Command Manager (BCM) / 6. Selection matrix / 7. Operating practices that carry across managers / 8. Lab (self-checking xCAT/Warewulf/BCM lab tested; VM cluster parts untested) / 9. Opening: Picture a new GPU node arriving at the data center. Someone has to record its MAC address, give it a boot image, power it on and confirm that it comes up healthy. Chapter 6.22 explained the mechanisms behind that work. A cluster manager packages those mechanisms into one tool. It covers the node database, the DHCP, TFTP and HTTP services, image build and distribution, power and console control, discovery, health, user and software management, and integration with Slurm or Kubernetes. The manager you pick decides your daily workflows, how the cluster gets upgraded and who supports it. AI clusters from NVIDIA ship with Base Command Manager. Research clusters often run Warewulf, legacy sites run xCAT, and cloud-style fleets use MAAS or Foreman. As an infrastructure engineer, you should be able to operate whichever manager is in front of you and explain its trade-offs.
Middle: Warewulf has been around since 2001. The README calls it "a stateless, network-boot cluster provisioning system designed primarily for use in HPC clusters." The v4 documentation lists its design tenets. Warewulf aims to be lightweight, simple, flexible and agnostic, running on x86 and ARM across many distributions. It is secure, with SELinux supported, and it is open source.

Opening: Picture a Monday morning on a GPU cluster. A researcher cannot open files she wrote last week, and a new student cannot submit a job at all. Both problems start with identity, which means knowing who each user is on every machine. A GPU cluster is a multi-user, multi-team system that holds valuable data and compute. Every node has to agree on who a user is. The same numeric UID and GID must appear everywhere, or file permissions on shared storage stop meaning anything. Users must also be added and removed quickly, in a way you can audit. Resources have to be charged to the right project. None of this can slow a login or a job launch when you run thousands of nodes. Identity mistakes cause incidents that range from "permission denied on my own files" to unauthorised access after an employee leaves. Containers, Kubernetes and Slurm add further identity layers, and all of them must stay consistent. You should already know Part 1 (users, permissions, PAM and nsswitch)
Middle: If you run traditional local ext3 or ext4 file systems, or NFS servers running `rpc.rquotad`, the book outlines a longer path. Mount with `usrquota` and `grpquota` in `/etc/fstab`. Build the quota database in read-only or single-user mode, with `quotacheck -vugc /dev/sda6` or `quotacheck -a -M`. Enable enforcement with `quotaon -va`. Then manage block and inode soft and hard limits, and grace periods, with `setquota`, `edquota`, and `setquota -p foo -u bar -a`, which copies user `foo`'s quota to `bar`. Report with `repquota -s` (untested here).
Exercise sample: Work through these in order. You will need a test environment for the steps that touch live services, and you can do the others on your own machine. 1. **Generate the unified LDIF, Slurm associations and home quotas (`code/part06/identity_gen.py` and `identity_gen_lab.py`).** Run `python3 code/part06/identity_gen_lab.py`. Opening: Researchers meet the cluster through access paths. An SSH session, a browser-based Jupyter, a VS Code remote window and a web job composer are all paths in. Each one makes decisions for you: how secure the cluster is, how quickly a researcher can start working, and how many support tickets you will field. Security depends on the login node first. It sits close to the internet, and a weak bastion is the most common initial-access vector in compromised research clusters. Productivity depends on the path too. Can users get a GPU notebook in two clicks? When the answer is no, people find their own way. They run notebooks on login nodes or move data to cloud notebooks, and the support load grows.
Middle: Start with correspondence. Set up a virtual address, which is an alias on the mail server that forwards email to several addresses. Use it instead of a personal or work address, or a shared `IMAP` mailbox on the server. Support assistants can then start answering at once, and user requests stay separate from other mail. On Linux mail servers, add an entry to `/etc/aliases` and rebuild the alias database with `newaliases`:

Scaling the monitoring stack / 9. Lab / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a 2,000-GPU cluster that looks fine from across the room. Some of its parts may be failing quietly, and each one is expensive. Monitoring is how you keep that cluster working as one system instead of a heap of hardware. It answers four different questions. *Is it up?* is about health. *Is it healthy?* covers errors, thermals and the fabric. *Is it used well?* asks about utilisation, the queue and goodput. *Is it going to fail?* looks at trends and predictive signals. Monitoring also feeds chargeback, capacity planning and incident analysis. GPU clusters send different signals than web services do. You watch GPU errors such as Xid, ECC and remapped rows, NVLink and InfiniBand counters, thermal throttling, power, storage latency and scheduler state. Keep these four questions in mind.
Middle: Fifth, link every alert to remediation. Annotate each alert with a runbook URL. For known hardware faults, you can drain a node automatically through a webhook: Alertmanager calls a service that runs `scontrol update ... state=drain reason=...`. Test that automation for loops and false positives. 6.14 and 5.32 discuss the safety properties it needs: budgets, dry-run, and provenance of what you changed.
Exercise sample: Each exercise below asks you to run something, explain what you see, or write a config. Work through them in order, since the later ones build on the lab. 1. ### part-06-hpc-slurm/27-backup-and-data-protection.md
Title: 6.27 Backup and data protection | words 7551 | code blocks 12
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. What to protect / 2. Principles / 3. Classic tools (SCL chapter 20) / tar / rsync, batch updates, and hard-link snapshots / Bacula / 4. Deduplicating repositories: restic, borg, kopia / 5. Parallel file systems, object stores and AI-scale data / 6. Protecting the control plane / 7. Ransomware and insider risk / 8. Restore testing and metrics / 9. Lab / 10. Interview angles / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: A training cluster can feel disposable. Checkpoints can be regenerated, and nodes can be reimaged, so backups are easy to skip. Skipping them is a mistake. The cluster also holds things you cannot regenerate. These include user code and results, curated datasets and labels, and trained models with their lineage. They also include the configuration that makes the cluster work, and the databases that define identity and accounting. Think about what happens when one of those is gone. Losing the Slurm accounting database, the LDAP directory, the head-node image repository, or a project's only copy of a fine-tuned model costs weeks of work. Ransomware aimed at research and cloud environments adds a second reason. Immutable, tested backups are a security control as much as an operations task.
Middle: In chapter 20 of the Supercomputers for Linux SysAdmins book, `borgbackup` is highlighted alongside `rsync`. Borg stores each backup as internal links to unique, deduplicated objects in a special database. Removing an old backup therefore deletes only the chunks unique to that backup. It can use `sshfs` or native remote service transport, with encryption. The project is at `https://www.borgbackup.org/`, and the helper scripts are at `https://github.com/zhum/hpc-book-matherials`.
Exercise sample: Work through these after the lab. Each one asks you to write, run or explain a specific part of the chapter. 1. Run `python3 code/part06/backup_protection_lab.py`. Then trace how `verify_pack_repository()` shows why `restic check --read-data` catches a 2-byte silent bit-rot in a pack file that the default metadata-only `restic check` misses. 2. Write the GNU `tar` commands for these steps, which come from chapter 20 of the Supercomputers for Linux

Opening: A vendor hands you a new cluster with a slide that says it is fast. Before you accept it, you need your own numbers. Benchmarks are how you accept hardware, find the one sick node out of 1,000, compare fabrics and file systems, and check whether a driver upgrade changed anything. They also answer the question every training team eventually asks: is this run as fast as it should be? The same tools can fool you too. One run on one node, with warm caches, against an untuned baseline, with no measure of variance, on a workload that does not look like yours, gives a clean number that means very little. The skills in this chapter are designing a measurement, running it repeatably, checking it against physics, and interpreting the result. They apply from a CPU micro-test up to a 4,096-GPU all-reduce.
Middle: The book's example, Table 5-1, shows what this comparison looks like. The CPU-staged path reaches 8.0 GB/s of total throughput at 1.25 ms average latency, with 85% CPU utilisation. The GDS path reaches 9.6 GB/s at 1.00 ms, with 25% CPU utilisation. That is a 20% throughput gain and 20% lower latency, and it also frees host CPU cores. VAST Data's GDS benchmarks in the book report a 20% sequential read throughput boost on an A100 GPU, and 30% or more on an H100. To trace `cuFile` calls alongside CUDA and OS runtime activity on a timeline, run Nsight Systems with `nsys profile --trace=cuda,gds,nvtx,osrt` (untested here), and enable the `cuFile`

### part-06-hpc-slurm/29-containers-and-reproducibility-in-hpc-apptainer-and-enroot.md
Title: 6.29 Containers and reproducibility in HPC: Apptainer and enroot | words 7258 | code blocks 5
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. What a container is, and why HPC needed new runtimes / Layers and data mounts / Containers in short / 2. Apptainer / Singularity and Apptainer / Core commands / Definition files / Cache / GPUs / MPI / Security model (general knowledge, verify) / 3. Charliecloud, enroot and the comparison / 4. Reproducibility discipline / A linter (tested) / 5. Distribution and operations / 6. Lab / 7. Interview angles / 6.1 Troubleshooting guide: Why a "reproducible" container fails when moved between GPU clusters / Exercises / Exercises / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: Suppose a training run that worked last month now gives different numbers on a new cluster. You need to find out which layer changed. Chapter 6.13 showed how NVIDIA's enroot and pyxis run containers inside Slurm. Many clusters, including most academic and national-lab systems, use **Apptainer**, which was formerly known as Singularity. Many scientific groups publish their software as Apptainer or Singularity images. AI makes this sharper. CUDA, cuDNN, NCCL, PyTorch, drivers and Python wheels all interact, and a one-line drift can change performance or numerics. This chapter compares the runtimes and gives you a discipline that makes container images trustworthy. Decide which runtime fits your cluster first, then apply the discipline to its images.
Middle: Default binds can leak host state. Apptainer's defaults bind home, `/tmp`, `/proc`, `/sys` and `/dev`, so verify that list for your version. Those binds can break reproducibility. For example, `~/.local` Python packages silently override container packages. Use `--no-home`, `--cleanenv` or `--containall` where needed, or set `PYTHONNOUSERSITE=1`. Prefer explicit, minimal binds for datasets and outputs.
Exercise sample: 1. Use `code/part06/container_lint.py` and `container_lint_lab.py` to check definition files for reproducibility. Run `python3 code/part06/container_lint_lab.py`. Second, why does `apt-get upgrade` make an image non-reproducible across build dates? Third, why is the word-boundary regex `\b(ba)?sh\b` necessary, so that `echo "<hash> file" | sha256sum -c -` is not misclassified as piping into a shell? 2. Write a complete Apptainer `.def` file that starts with `Bootstrap: docker` and `From: nvcr.io/nvidia/pytorch@sha256:...`. It should copy a hash-locked

Getting the heat out: air cooling / 4.1 How much air? / 4.2 Conditions the room must meet / 4.3 Good habits for air-cooled rooms / 4.4 Airflow direction / 5. Liquid cooling / 6. Interview angles / Checkpoint / Sources and review notes
Opening: Suppose the facility team tells you a rack can hold only two of the GPU servers you ordered. Why would that be? A GPU server turns electricity into computation, and almost all of that electricity leaves the machine as heat. The more GPUs you pack into a rack, the more power you must bring in and the more heat you must carry out. Power, cooling and space are the three limits of every data center. They decide how many GPUs you can run before software even matters. You do not have to design buildings. You do need to speak to the facility team, understand why a rack holds so few servers, and know what happens when the power fails.
Middle: The book names real adopters. Microsoft uses two-phase immersion in production, Google uses cold-plate-like cooling for TPUs, and Meta plans rear-door and cold-plate cooling. The book also contains a small error worth knowing about. Its text expands the rear-door acronym AALC as "air-assisted liquid chromatography". The acronym appendix gives the correct name, **Air-Assisted Liquid Cooling**.
Exercise sample: Start by running the self-checking Python lab, `code/part06/facility_calc_lab.py`, on your own machine. ```bash python3 code/part06/facility_calc_lab.py ``` 1. Reproduce the NVIDIA DGX B200 rack power table, using `code/part06/facility_calc.py` and `facility_calc_lab.py`. Run `python3 code/part06/facility_calc_lab.py`. Then check one row by hand with $P_{\text{circuit}} = \sqrt{3} \times V_{\text{LL}} \times I \times \text{derating} \times \text{PF} / 1000$ (`PF = 0.95`). Two three-phase `415 V`, `60 A` circuits at `80%` breaker derating should deliver `65.6 kW` in total. Four DGX B200 servers at `14.3 kW` each demand `57.2 kW`, which leaves `8.4 kW` of stranded power.

Corrections to common BCM and SuperPOD interview myths / 8. Weak versus senior answers at a glance / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Across Chapters 6.1 through 6.30, you have studied each part of an AI supercomputer on its own. You learned MPI collectives and NUMA topology. You learned `slurmctld` and `slurmdbd` scheduling policy, and `gres.conf` and `cgroup.conf` v2 device isolation. You also covered Pyxis and Enroot rootless containers, NHC prolog/epilog gates, Lustre and WekaFS parallel storage, and PXE/iPXE and Warewulf/BCM provisioning. Then came LDAP/SSSD identity with `sacctmgr` TRES quotas, Prometheus exporters with Restic backup pipelines, HPL/IOR/NCCL acceptance benchmarks, and 130 kW liquid-cooled racks. In production, and in a senior infrastructure architect interview, none of those layers live in separate chapters. Suppose a new pallet of GPU servers arrives on the loading dock. Or an interviewer hands you a marker and asks you to design a 128-node training factory, from bare metal to a running `torchrun` job. The skill that matters is **dependency order and single-source-of-truth automation**. Install the scheduler before identity or shared storage is ready, and jobs launch only to crash immediately with UID mismatches or missing checkpoints. Hand-edit `slurm.conf`, `dnsmasq.conf`
Middle: Interviewers listen for the difference between a quick answer and a structured one. The table below sets a junior reply next to a senior one for five common questions. Read each pair and notice what the senior answer adds: a source of truth, a measurable check, or a named tool.

Opening: Picture a GPU server that stops answering in the middle of a long job. An eight-GPU server, such as an NVIDIA DGX H100 or HGX node, costs hundreds of thousands of dollars. It draws up to 10.2 kW, and it sits in a dense data center hall that may be thousands of miles from your desk. In a public cloud, a frozen virtual machine is easy to recover. You click "Reboot" in a web portal, and the hypervisor resets the guest from the outside. On bare-metal AI infrastructure, **there is no hypervisor underneath your host operating system**. Nothing outside the server can reset it for you.
Middle: 1. **Built-in BMC `iKVM` beats external KVM switches.** Modern BMCs include the video capture hardware inside the ASPEED chip. External rack KVM appliances need bulky dongles, and they cannot toggle motherboard power pins. 2. **A lack of a wire standard makes `iKVM` unsuitable for fleet automation.** Vendors have historically used proprietary HTML5 websockets, VNC variants or legacy Java applets. You cannot reliably script BIOS changes for 128 servers by clicking pixels in a browser window.

Opening: Picture a GPU server whose host operating system has frozen. You need to reset it, and you need to read its hardware state without logging in to that OS. Every modern GPU server gives you that path. Whether it is an 8-GPU NVIDIA DGX H100 or HGX node or a 72-GPU rack-scale GB200 NVL72, the server exposes its out-of-band hardware state through a **DMTF Redfish** service running on the BMC. Platform teams use that service every day. They inventory serial numbers and GPU VBIOS versions, watch inlet and SXM thermal sensors, stage BIOS tuning attributes, flash firmware bundles, and reset frozen nodes without touching the host OS. Now suppose your automation is a pile of memorized `curl` URLs. It will break the first time you move from one vendor's BMC to another. It can also break after a BMC firmware upgrade on the very same server. The fix is to learn the shape of the data model instead. You need to see how the single Service Root (`/redfish/v1/`) links to
Middle: A **Resource Collection** is a container of resources that share a base type, such as `ComputerSystemCollection`, `SensorCollection`, or `LogEntryCollection`. Request and response headers / 2.1 Essential request headers (sent by your client) / 2.2 Essential response headers (returned by the BMC) / 3. Optimistic concurrency control with `ETag` and `If-Match` / 4. OData query parameters: fetching less, more, or filtered data / 5.1 Query parameter reference (DSP0266 §7.3.1–7.3.8) / 5.2 Error handling rules for query parameters (DSP0266 §7.3.1) / 6. Lab: Running the 36-check protocol compliance suite (`code/part07/protocol_lab.py`) / 6.1 Three real protocol bugs caught while building the lab / 7. Production checklist for safe fleet-wide `PATCH` operations / Exercises / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: An automation script that powers down GPU nodes, changes BIOS settings, or rotates BMC certificates across a 1,024-GPU cluster can do real damage. Its safety depends on how well you understand the **Redfish wire protocol**. Redfish runs over ordinary HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2, so the requests look familiar. The meaning of each reply is less obvious, and the DMTF Redfish Specification (DSP0266 v1.21.0, Sections 6, 7, and 8) spells it out. The status codes carry more meaning than success or failure. A `200 OK` means a change is complete. A `202 Accepted` means a background job has started and is not finished. A `200 OK` can also carry `@Message.ExtendedInfo` warnings, which tell you only part of your change landed. A `400 Bad Request` means nothing was applied, while a `412 Precondition Failed` means someone else changed the resource first, and a `401 Unauthorized` means your credentials were not accepted.
Middle: One rule in DSP0266 §7.6 trips up many scripts. Suppose a single `PATCH` carries three properties. `AssetTag` is writable, `SerialNumber` is read-only, and `Bogus` is not a property at all. The body looks like this: `{"AssetTag": "A2", "SerialNumber": "X", "Bogus": 1}`. What the BMC does depends on how many of those properties succeed.
Exercise sample: Work through these with the lab open in another window. Each one turns a rule from this chapter into something you can see on your own screen. 1. Trace all 36 protocol assertions. Sort the 36 checks into five groups: unauthenticated discovery, conditional `GET` and `ETag` concurrency, query parameters, partial versus failed `PATCH`, and session lifecycle. 2. Reproduce a `412 Precondition Failed` collision with `curl`. Start `python3 code/part07/redfish_mock.py --profile dgx --port 18441`. Protections against guessing and stale passwords / 5. The lab / 6. Safe handling in your own scripts / 6.1 Anatomy of a session-table exhaustion outage / 6.2 How the Privilege Registry governs multi-team operations / 6.3 Designing zero-touch factory password rotation on Day 0 / 6.4 External directory integration and certificate lifecycle / Exercises / 8. Interview angles / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a node that will not boot after a firmware update. The only way in is through its BMC. A BMC can turn a server off, rewrite its firmware and change its settings. Whoever holds the BMC password controls the machine below the operating system, where your normal security tools cannot see. Now picture a fleet of 1,000 BMCs that all share one factory password. One leaked password puts the whole fleet at risk. This chapter shows how Redfish decides who you are and what you may do. It also shows how to set that up so your automation stays safe. Every Redfish request can carry a password and a command across the network. Anyone who can watch that traffic could read them, so the traffic must be encrypted before anything else. The spec says services shall support **TLS 1.2 or later**. They shall also offer only cipher suites marked "Recommended" by the IANA TLS registry. Any request that uses HTTP Basic authentication must use HTTPS. Operations with sensitive data,
Middle: Each embedded BMC has a small, fixed table of active sessions in RAM. The Redfish specification leaves the maximum number of simultaneous open sessions up to the firmware implementation. While a session stays idle, the BMC must keep it until `SessionTimeout` expires. Take the default timeout of thirty minutes. If the scraper logs in twice a minute without logging out, the arithmetic is simple: two logins a minute for thirty minutes is sixty sessions. One script therefore consumes sixty session slots per BMC.
Exercise sample: Work through these in order. Each one asks you to read, run or write something, so try it yourself before you check the answers. 1. Check that an unknown username (`nosuchuser`), a wrong password for a valid user (`admin`), and a correct password on a locked account (`viewer` after `3` failed attempts) all return the same HTTP `401 Unauthorized` status and the same generic message (`DSP0266` §13.3.2.3). Opening: A GPU node stops answering in the middle of a training run. Reading its state is the easy part. The hard part is doing something about it: restarting the node, updating its firmware, or cutting its power. Those operations take time, can fail halfway through, and can be refused outright. A cluster tool therefore needs three things. It needs a way to ask for an operation, a way to wait for it, and a way to hear about trouble without asking every machine every second. When you are on call, you want all three working before the next page arrives. A fan can fail, or a power supply can drop out, and the tool should learn about it as it happens. Redfish has a concept for each job: **actions** for asking, **tasks** for waiting, and **events** for hearing about trouble.
Middle: 1. The first group covers discovering the action from the resource, bad parameters, a read-only `PATCH`, privileges, and the NoOperation answer. 2. The second group covers the 202 and task-monitor flow with a simulated clock, plus progress and cancel. 3. The third group covers subscriptions: create, receive, filter, unsubscribe, and delivery failure. 4. The fourth group covers server-sent events with a filter.

Opening: Picture a BMC that returns a property you did not expect, or a script that stops working after a firmware update. Both problems have the same cure: the published contract. Every Redfish answer follows that contract. If you can read it, you never need a vendor manual to ask what a property means, whether you can write it, or whether it is new or old. You can also test any tool or BMC against the contract, which catches mistakes before they reach production. This chapter shows you how. Suppose you open the DMTF files for the first time and find three formats that seem to hold the same data. That is by design. Redfish describes its data model in the schema, and the spec publishes that same model in three formats. Each format suits a different kind of tool, so you should know all three.
Middle: The `mockups` folder is a free library of realistic Redfish trees. It covers rack servers, liquid-cooled servers, PDUs, power shelves, cooling units, storage, CXL, telemetry and more. You can serve any of them with the mock and practise your scripts against it.

Opening: Picture a GPU server that runs hot in the middle of a training job. You need that GPU's temperature, the state of its power supplies and the BMC firmware version, and you need them from a machine you have never logged into before. Nearly every Redfish task starts in one of three places: the `Systems` collection (the computer), the `Chassis` collection (the physical box) and the `Managers` collection (the BMC). A GPU server is more than one computer. It may hold a host, a GPU baseboard and maybe some DPUs. Once you understand how these three families connect, you can find that temperature, a power supply's status or a firmware version on any server, even one you have never seen. When you look at a server, you are really asking three different questions. Each question has its own family, and knowing which family to open saves you from searching the wrong place. The table gives each question, the family that answers it and a plain picture of what that family
Middle: The layout differs between makers, so a robust tool tries the standard route first and falls back to a second one if it fails. Build your own lookup the same way. Section 8 of chapter 7.8 returns to this idea.

Opening: Picture a training job that has slowed by a third since yesterday. Nothing crashed, and the operating system logs nothing unusual. "No data" is a finding, and you should report it.

What Redfish does not tell you / 6.1 Anatomy of a silent PCIe link downgrade in distributed training / 6.2 Diagnosing optical and copper `Port` speed mismatches / 6.3 Proactive NVMe and boot drive lifecycle management / 6.4 Integrating `rf_hardware.py` into Day-0 and Day-2 automation / 6.5 Correlating Redfish PCIe and NIC inventory with Linux `lspci`, `ethtool`, and `mlx5` / 6.6 Handling OEM topology quirks and `FabricAdapters` / Exercises / 8. Interview angles / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Suppose a GPU's PCIe link has trained down to a quarter of its full speed. Nothing crashes, and no alert fires. The job simply runs slower, or one slow node drags a whole training run behind it. The same thing happens when a network port negotiates 200 Gbps instead of 400, or when a boot drive is about to die. These problems are quiet, and that is what makes them expensive. A cluster team needs a cheap, automatic way to ask every machine whether all of its links run at full speed. The BMC can answer that question through Redfish, even before the operating system is installed. When your script asks for a drive or a network card, it first has to know which branch of the tree to search. You will find hardware under both families from chapter 7.7. The system side shows the **logical** view, and the chassis side shows the **physical** one.
Middle: The second silent problem in AI fabrics is a high-speed port that links up, but at a fraction of its rated speed or lane width. You will find these ports under `NetworkAdapter/Ports`. Modern 400 Gbps and 800 Gbps InfiniBand and Ethernet links are built from parallel lanes. Each lane is a SerDes circuit, which converts data to and from a serial signal. A typical link uses 4 lanes of 100 Gbps PAM4 signaling, or 8 lanes of 100 Gbps.

Opening: A 512-GPU training job slows down, and the cause turns out to be four replacement nodes. Those nodes ran an older GPU firmware. The user's notes tell this story. The firmware throttled under sustained load and cut the job's throughput by 14 percent. Treat those numbers as an illustration, not a measurement. A GPU cluster is many parts that must work together. Those parts include the BIOS, the BMC, the GPU firmware, the NVSwitch firmware, the NIC firmware, the PCIe switches and more. If one node runs older firmware than the rest, it may be slower, or it may fail in odd ways. Everyone then waits for the slowest node.
Middle: 1. **Decide the baseline.** Take it from the vendor's validated bundle for your platform, and write it to a file in version control. 2. **Read the vendor's update guide** for this exact platform and release. Check the prerequisites, the known issues, the order of components, and which activation each component needs. 3. **Take a snapshot** of the firmware inventory, the health data and, if you can, the vendor's support bundle. This is your before picture. 4. **Drain** the node in the scheduler (chapter 6.14) and wait until its jobs end. If the guide says so, stop the monitoring agents and GPU tools too. 5. **Test on one node** first, then

Opening: A job dies with a CUDA error, and the operating system logs show nothing useful. The cause may still be on the machine, recorded by a hardware event that only the BMC saw. The BMC is the small controller that watches the server's hardware separately from the operating system. It can log a memory error, a power supply drop, a fan failure, an over-temperature trip or a PCIe fault. Its memory survives the crash, and even a power loss on the host. When you ask a vendor for help, they will want a **diagnostic bundle** collected from the BMC. Reading these logs and collecting that bundle is therefore a core skill for anyone who runs GPU servers.
Middle: Fix the clock on every BMC with NTP before the machine goes into service. Then alert on Critical entries and on the log-health warnings from section 4. Collect bundles before any repair that might reset the BMC or erase state. Tie logs to jobs as well, because the scheduler knows which job ran on a node when an event happened. Finally, keep sample logs from healthy nodes. Normal chatter is what makes the unusual entry stand out.

Opening: Clicking through a web page works for one server. With 1,000 servers you need code, and code that talks to BMCs has to be careful. A bad retry loop can lock accounts. An unbounded loop can overload the management switches. A script that dies on the first failed host leaves half a fleet changed and the other half untouched. This chapter shows the same few operations in three languages, then the rules that keep any of them safe at scale. Suppose a script turns off certificate checks so that it can reach a BMC. It will now talk to anything that answers at that address, including an impostor. Real BMCs use HTTPS, and the mock does too now. `redfish_mock.py --tls CERT KEY` serves TLS 1.2 or newer, as the spec requires. The script `tls_setup.py` creates a throw-away certificate authority (CA) and a server certificate for `127.0.0.1`:
Middle: A test against one happy-path mock server only proves that your JSON field names are spelled right. It says nothing about failure isolation. That is why `code/part07/fleet_lab.py` starts fifteen separate HTTPS mock servers at once and injects six failure modes:

Opening: Suppose a GPU server stops answering on its management network after a firmware update. The vendor asks which firmware bundle you applied, and before you can answer, you need to know who owns the box. Chapters 7.2 to 7.12 taught Redfish in general. A GPU cluster is less general than that. The server carries a GPU baseboard with its own controllers and NVLink switch chips, plus DPUs, a very large power draw and a vendor-specific management stack. Who builds the machine decides how it is managed. In a DGX SuperPOD, NVIDIA's reference designs say how the management network is built and which BMCs exist. In an HGX server built by an OEM, a different company owns the BMC. Knowing **who owns what** decides whom you call and which firmware bundle you may apply.
Middle: A profile cannot count devices, so it will not confirm that there are "eight GPUs". It also cannot judge whether readings make sense. For counts, use `rf_hardware.py` and `rf_inventory.py`. For firmware, compare against a baseline (chapter 7.10).

Opening: Picture a rack of 128 GPU servers that has just arrived. Racking them is the easy part. The hard part is getting every server into the same state, with the same BIOS, firmware and boot setup, then proving it and keeping it that way for years. The notes put the stakes plainly: a distributed training job runs at the speed of its slowest GPU. One node with a different BIOS setting, or with older firmware, becomes a cluster-wide problem. This chapter takes the primitives of chapters 7.2 to 7.13 and turns them into a repeatable **node lifecycle**. You need chapters 7.5 and 7.7 to 7.12 before you start. When you finish this chapter, you should be able to change BIOS settings through Redfish and explain the difference between pending and live values. You should also use the attribute registry to check a setting before you send it, and use one-time and continuous boot override correctly.
Middle: Prevention matters more than recovery. Update the BMC in a first small wave, keep the recovery procedure written down, and never update BMC firmware on a whole rack at once. Keep `Manager.Reset` in mind too, from chapter 7.7. A restart request over Redfish needs a working BMC, so this scenario needs the in-band route.

Opening: The BMC is the most privileged computer in the rack. It can power the server on and off, mount a virtual disk, open a console, rewrite firmware and read hardware state. It works below the operating system, where your normal security tools cannot see it. The Security volume of the notes (volume 18) puts the risk plainly. If the BMC is compromised, the server is lost. If the firmware is compromised, no operating system reinstall helps. GPU clusters also hold valuable data and expensive hardware, so they attract attackers. This chapter closes Part 7 with the controls that matter, and it shows tests for the ones you can run yourself.
Middle: The databases hold the keys. `PK` is the platform key. `KEK` is the key exchange key, and it controls updates to the other two databases. `db` lists the allowed signatures, and `dbx` lists the forbidden ones. Each database has `Certificates` and `Signatures` collections. The action `SecureBoot.ResetKeys` accepts `ResetAllKeysToDefault`, `DeleteAllKeys` or `DeletePK`.

Common first mistakes / 7.1 Mental model: How CMDaemon reconciles database state into Linux files / Exercises / 9. Interview angles / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a cluster with hundreds of nodes and one change that has to reach all of them. Someone has to answer a few hard questions. How does a blank node get its operating system? How do you change a setting on 500 nodes at once? How do you power-cycle a rack, and how does the scheduler learn what each node is? BCM is NVIDIA's answer. It is the management software of NVIDIA's DGX SuperPOD reference designs, and the SuperPOD architecture documents name Base Command Manager and Mission Control among the in-cluster services. If you know the BCM objects and rules, you can read any BCM cluster and predict what a change will do. That is the goal of this chapter. Learn the objects first, and the rest of the cluster becomes much easier to read.
Middle: Some clusters run Kubernetes and Slurm side by side. When one queue backs up while the other sits idle, someone has to move nodes. That is the practical reason BCM separates categories from configuration overlays. Categories hold the software image and boot settings, and overlays hold the workload manager roles. NVIDIA Base Command Manager Auto Scaler shows the idea in action.

Interview angles / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a new cluster on the day the hardware arrives. The racks are in, and someone is ready to click through the installer. The install is the one step you cannot casually redo. The manual says the head node disk layout is hard to change later. It also says the license is tied to a MAC address, the fixed hardware address of a network card. Network choices decide how every node boots. If you plan first, you install once. If you click through, you may end up rebuilding the cluster. You install BCM on one machine first: the **head node**. Everything else depends on it. The head node then provisions every other node over the network, which chapter 8.4 covers. So planning BCM really means planning the head node and the network it sits on.
Middle: BCM ships with SELinux disabled. The manual says to enable it only if your organisation's rules demand it. The policies need custom work, and they make problems harder to trace. If you do enable it, follow the manual's order. Set the mode to permissive first, and make sure `cmd` runs unconfined. Then set the mode in the `selinuxsettings` submode, at the node, category or partition level.

Opening: Picture a GPU node that resolves names differently from its neighbours. Nothing has crashed, and the node still boots. Someone changed a setting at one level when the change belonged at another. After the install, most of your work in BCM is changing a setting and deciding how far that change should reach. A GPU cluster has several networks: management, compute fabric, storage and BMC. It also has several kinds of node: login, CPU and GPU. Some settings must be identical on hundreds of machines. When you finish, you should be able to find and change the cluster-wide settings, and you will know which of them send you email. You should also explain the default networks and the four places an internal network setting can come from. From there you can configure interfaces, static routes, bridges, VLANs and bonds, and avoid the bond mistakes the manual warns about.
Middle: Uplinks need care. A port that connects to another switch must be marked as an uplink, as in `set switch01 uplinks 15 16`. That tells CMDaemon to ignore the port during MAC-to-port learning. Otherwise, transit traffic corrupts the node port mapping.

Opening: Picture a rack of new GPU servers powering on. Half of them sit at a boot prompt, and the rest come up with last month's software. Provisioning exists to prevent that kind of day. BCM's job is to make 500 nodes identical. Each node boots, receives the same software image, and joins the cluster without anyone logging in to it. A GPU cluster is only as good as its worst node. When provisioning goes wrong, you get nodes that will not boot, nodes that quietly lost their local data, or nodes running an old image. This chapter follows a node from power-on to a working cluster member, step by step. It also points out where each step can break.
Middle: By default, BCM provisions nodes over Ethernet. The reason is that the `rdma` init script (or `openibd` on SLES) loads the InfiniBand kernel modules only after the node-provisioning stage finishes. To provision at InfiniBand speed during the pre-init stage, you need two changes. This assumes `ibnet` is already configured, as in chapter 8.3.

Opening: Picture a setting that must change on a whole rack of GPU servers. Doing it by hand, one node at a time, is slow and easy to get wrong. Everything in chapters 8.3 and 8.4 was done through `cmsh` or Base View. Most real work on a cluster is repetition, though: the same change to 8 nodes, or 512, or one rack. `cmsh` is built for that repetition. Its habits, such as commit, listing a range and the dry run, are what separate a calm change from an outage. In an interview, "show me how you would change a setting on a rack" is a `cmsh` question. When you have Base View open in one window and `cmsh` in another, which program actually answers you? Both of them talk to CMDaemon on the head node, and never straight to the daemons on other nodes. The diagram shows the path.
Middle: Source names are sorted alphabetically first. Run `--dry-run` first, then `commit`. A `refresh` throws away the proposal, and any other pending change with it. Dry-run every rename, and commit only once the proposed names match what you expected.

Common first mistakes / 9.1 Architecting least-privilege management profiles for multi-team AI clusters / 9.2 Enterprise identity architectures: Local LDAP vs Direct External LDAP vs `syncrepl` / 9.3 Single Sign-On (SSO) with Keycloak for Base View, User Portal, and Mission Control / 9.4 Step-by-step incident response: Revoking compromised credentials and rotating cluster secrets / 9.5 Hardening SSH and login-node boundaries (`usernodelogin` and `AllowUsers`) / 9.6 Auditing user, certificate, and profile state across the cluster / Exercises / 11. Interview angles / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a GPU cluster that many teams share. Researchers need accounts that work on every node. Operators need to power-cycle a node without being able to read anyone's data. Auditors will eventually ask who changed what, and when. BCM handles all of this with a few simple ideas: one user directory for the whole cluster, certificates for management access, and profiles that say what each certificate may do. Once you know these ideas, you can give a person exactly the access they need and no more. Before you change any setting, know which part of the system you are touching. The diagram shows how a management request travels from a person to the cluster, and where the cluster's user directory fits in.
Middle: Picture a systems engineer, a NOC operator, a help-desk technician and a platform developer, each holding a copy of `/root/.cm/admin.pem` or the `admin` profile. That setup is hard to audit and easy to break. One mistyped `remove` or `power off` in `cmsh` can wipe a software image or drop power to an entire Scalable Unit. BCM avoids this with its profile and token architecture, described in section 6.5 of the manual. You can split management authority into narrow roles without sharing `root` at all.
Exercise sample: Work through these in order. Each one builds on the chapter's checks and tools. 1. Run `python3 security_lab.py`. The first is an operator who can only power-cycle nodes. The second is an auditor who can see everything but change nothing. The third is a researcher who can see only their own jobs. Opening: It is three in the morning and a GPU node has stopped answering. You cannot SSH in, because the operating system is the part that hung. A GPU server is more than an operating system. Under it sit a BMC, a BIOS, GPU and switch firmware, power feeds and cooling. When a node hangs, the way back in runs through the BMC, so you power-cycle the machine from there. When you roll out new GPU firmware, a wrong activation step can leave the old firmware running while everyone believes it was updated. BCM puts all of these jobs under one tool, so you can handle a whole rack at a time. Part 7 of this handbook explains the Redfish protocol underneath. This chapter shows how BCM wraps it.

Run `firmware status -n node001` to see every component with its version and state. 4. Run `firmware flash <package>` on the node. The status shows `flashing` with a progress percentage, and then the versions in transition, for example `96.00.70.00.01 -> 96.00.74.00.01`. 5. Opening: Picture a GPU node that is up and healthy, yet no job ever lands on it. Or a queue that is missing a node you added last week. On most GPU clusters there is a scheduler, and for training clusters it is usually Slurm. BCM does not just install Slurm and leave it alone. It generates `slurm.conf`, `gres.conf` and `cgroup.conf` from objects you set in cmsh, so nodes, queues and GPUs stay consistent as the cluster changes. Once you know where each Slurm setting lives in BCM, you can track down the cause quickly. The three cases are a node missing from a queue, a GPU that jobs cannot see, and a Slurm that has quietly gone unconfigured after an upgrade. Before you change any setting, you need to know which scheduler BCM is managing. BCM 11 supports **Slurm** (versions 25.05 and 25.11 in the manual I read), **IBM Spectrum LSF 10.1**, or none. Slurm arrives as BCM packages, while LSF is picked up from the vendor. This chapter follows
Middle: You need to pull a node out of service for a reimage, but jobs are still running on it. Draining handles that. The running jobs finish, and the node accepts no new ones. You can drain a node, wait, and then reimage it safely.

Opening: If you run a GPU cluster, you will probably have two systems side by side. Many clusters run Kubernetes for inference, notebooks and platform services, and Slurm for training. BCM can deploy a whole Kubernetes cluster onto bare-metal nodes it already manages. One wizard handles the control plane, etcd, the CNI, the GPU operator, the gateways and per-user policy. The same nodes can often belong to either world, so you need to know what the wizard changes on a node and where each setting lives. Before you start, you should have worked through chapters 8.1 to 8.8 and know the basics of Kubernetes. Part 11 goes deeper if you want more. When you finish this chapter, you should be able to:
Middle: Kgateway runs an operator, and that operator is the control plane. The proxy pods are the data plane, and the operator creates one set per Gateway. Installing the operator alone exposes nothing. A Gateway, with a GatewayClass, must exist first.

Opening: A researcher messages you on a Monday morning. Their training job will not start, and the error mentions a container. Before you can help, you need three answers. Which tool ran the container? Where did the image come from? Who is allowed to become root through that tool? Almost all AI software now arrives as a container image, pulled from NGC or from a company registry. On a BCM cluster, each of those three questions has a specific answer. The answers also differ depending on whether the work runs as a Slurm job or as a Kubernetes service. This chapter walks through each answer, so you can tell which path a failing job took.
Middle: 1. Giving users `docker` access instead of a Slurm or Kubernetes path. 2. Expecting Docker to be integrated with Slurm in BCM. 3. Leaving Harbor with the default `Harbor12345` password. 4. Using a Docker Hub proxy URL without the port. 5. Putting Harbor and Keycloak on the same head node. 6. Letting every node pull a big image from NGC at the same moment. 7. Forgetting `authn.nvidia.com` in the Enroot credentials. 8. Leaving a shared Enroot cache open to everyone. 9. Forgetting to reinstall Pyxis after upgrading Slurm. 10. Assuming BCM supports Apptainer.

Opening: Picture a training run that spans hundreds of GPUs. When one GPU fails, the others can stall while they wait for it. The sooner you find the bad node and drain it, the less compute gets wasted. BCM's monitoring collects thousands of numbers per node. Some of those numbers become pass or fail checks, and some can run actions when something changes. Your real skill is deciding what to watch, what is only information, and what should act on its own. Before you start, you need chapters 8.1 to 8.7 (objects, nodes, power) and 8.8 (drain). Check that list against what you already know, and go back to an earlier chapter for anything you cannot yet do.
Middle: The Base View window is where you will spend most of your monitoring time, so it helps to know what each part does. The top-right bar holds eight icons. From left to right they are: (1) **Cluster Overview**, (2) **Job Accounting**, (3) **Monitoring** (visual graphs), (4) **Chargeback**, (5) **Events**, (6) **Background Tasks**, (7) **Package Updates** (the GUI equivalent of `packages --outdated` in `cmsh`), and (8) the **Menu (`≡`)** icon, which holds the dark mode toggle and logout.

Opening: Suppose the head node stops answering. It holds CMDaemon, the database, the DHCP and TFTP services, the LDAP directory, the master image copies and often the Slurm controller. While it is down, you cannot provision, change or monitor anything. Running jobs usually keep going, but nothing new can be managed. A second head node costs one server, so the hardware is the easy part. The harder part is making sure a failover happens **when needed and never when not**. A wrong failover can corrupt shared storage. The rest of this chapter keeps coming back to that risk.
Middle: The failover parameters live in `partition failover base`. In Base View you find them at Cluster > Partition[base] > Settings > Failover. *Dead time* is how long a head can go without an answer before the active head is treated as dead and a quorum starts. *Warn time* is when BCM logs a warning, and *Init dead* replaces Dead time while a head is just starting. *Keep alive* sets the check interval, and *Quorum time* sets how long the quorum poll waits. *Failover network* names the heartbeat network. The *Mount script* and *Unmount script* handle shared storage. The *Prefailover script* runs on both heads once the decision is made, before

Opening: Think about a bad night on a cluster. Someone makes a change at 3 a.m., and in the morning you have to find out what changed and how to undo it. Building a cluster is a project with an end. Running it is a job that never ends. Packages need updating, nodes fail, racks get added, and disks die. BCM gives you the tools for all of this, but much of the safety is habit: back up first, change little, check the result, and know the way back. This chapter collects those habits along with the exact tools the manual provides. This chapter assumes you have worked through the rest of Part 8. When you finish it, you should be able to read BCM version numbers and follow the update rules, including the ones for HA. You will be able to run a command on many nodes without causing a power surge or cutting off your own tool. You will know how to ask for help the way BCM support
Middle: Hardware matching works like this (14.6). Add the new nodes to a category, then save one node as the reference with `node-hardware-profile -n node129 -s newbunch`. That stores the profile under `/cm/shared/apps/cmd/hardware-profiles/`. Next, enable the `hardware-profile` data producer with an interval, and add a `nodeexecutionfilters` category filter. The manual uses an interval of 600 s. After that, CMDaemon alerts you when a node does not match the reference. The reference can itself be faulty. If it is, most nodes get reported as bad, and that is how you notice the problem.

Opening: Picture a training cluster in a government data centre with no route to the internet. A driver update stalls halfway through, and the package it needs sits on a server the cluster cannot reach. There, `yum update` and `docker pull` do not work at all. Everything has to be carried in on media, checked, and installed from local copies. Many AI clusters sit in places like this, including regulated industries, government and defence. The work is mostly preparation. If one package, image or chart is missing from the bundle, you find out halfway through an installation, and by then you cannot simply carry on.
Middle: 1. **Zone 1: The Connected Staging Host (Internet-facing lab).** - Keep a dedicated virtual machine or bare-metal host whose **OS distribution, minor version and CPU architecture (`x86_64` or `aarch64`) match your air-gapped head node**. - Copy `/etc/apt/sources.list.d/cm-*.list` and `ubuntu.sources` (or `/etc/yum.repos.d/cm.repo` and `epel.repo`) from your cluster. That way the staging host pulls the exact BCM point-release packages your head node expects. - Log Skopeo in to Docker Hub with `skopeo login docker.io`, and to NGC with `skopeo login nvcr.io`, before you run `$K8S_AG_SCRIPTS/download_container_images.sh`. That way you never hit the `100`-pull unauthenticated Docker Hub rate limit partway through downloading two hundred container layers. - Always pass `--kube-version=<major.minor>`, or `-c cm-kubernetes-setup.conf`, explicitly

Title: 8.15 BCM in an AI factory: DGX SuperPOD integration | words 6069 | code blocks 1

Interview angles / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a design review for a new SuperPOD row, where nobody can say whether the building can feed it. BCM cannot answer that on its own. In a DGX SuperPOD, BCM is one layer of a stack that starts with the physical floor. Power, cooling and cables decide how many GPUs you can run. BCM, which runs inside NVIDIA Mission Control, is how you manage what those parts support. An engineer who knows the numbers can sanity-check a design in minutes. That engineer can also explain why each BCM feature exists, because it maps to a physical component. Start with the floor, and only then plan the software.
Middle: This section ties together three earlier threads: the physical facility lessons of Chapter 6.30, the out-of-band Redfish architecture of Part 7, and the Base Command Manager control plane of Chapters 8.1 through 8.14. Picture a cloud provider deploying an NVIDIA DGX SuperPOD. The build might use air-cooled **DGX B200** systems, which draw `14.3 kW` per `10U` node, with up to `32` nodes or `458 kW` per Scalable Unit. It might instead use liquid-cooled **DGX GB300 NVL72** racks. Each of those has `18` compute trays, `9` NVLink switch trays and `8` `33 kW` power shelves, which gives `72` GPUs per rack. Eight such racks make `576` GPUs and about `~1.2 MW`

Title: 8.16 Mission Control, DGX Cloud and autonomous recovery concepts | words 7977 | code blocks 15
Headings: Why this matters for AI infrastructure / Prerequisites and outcomes / 1. Autonomous recovery concepts and what BCM exposes / 2.1 The four capabilities of Mission Control's autonomous recovery / 2.2 The two recovery profiles in BCM / 2.3 Head-node HA and databases that are not replicated by default / 2.4 How this connects to BCM monitoring and training checkpoints / 3. NVIDIA Run:ai under BCM / 3.1 Architecture and installation types / 3.2 How the setup wizard changed in BCM 11.33 / 3.3 Operator dependencies, prerequisites, and wizard validations / 3.4 Saving the configuration, deploying, verifying, and post-installation / 4. DGX Cloud and AWS topology awareness in BCM / 5. Lab: checking Mission Control, Run:ai and recovery rules / Common first mistakes / Exercises / Interview angles / Checkpoint questions (20) / Sources and review notes
Opening: Picture a new DGX SuperPOD on its first day. An operations engineer tries to flash rack firmware with the standard Base Command Manager tools, and the flash fails with a license error. The rack management controls they expect also seem to be missing from the administrator manual. The reason is that a SuperPOD does not run Base Command Manager by itself. NVIDIA packages it inside a wider software suite called NVIDIA Mission Control. That suite combines BCM with NVIDIA Run:ai, Slurm, and built-in validation, telemetry, and autonomous recovery components. Inside the BCM documentation, Mission Control shows up as a license edition, a set of Base View pages, nonuser security profiles, and a separate manual. You need to know which workflows live in standard BCM tools and which belong to Mission Control. The standard tools are `cmsh`, `cm-kubernetes-setup`, `cm-runai-setup`, and `cmha-setup`. Knowing the split keeps you from searching the wrong manual. It also keeps you from breaking a cluster's self-healing and high-availability setup.
Middle: A long cluster installer run over an unstable SSH session can stop halfway. The BCM 11.33 single-run wizard bug can do the same. On Multi-Node NVLink platforms such as GB200 and GB300, stopping after the base wizard also leaves multi-node GPU memory exchange non-functional. It stays that way until the Dynamic Resource Allocation driver is installed.

Opening: An AI factory is not a pile of interchangeable servers with GPUs plugged into PCIe slots. When an organization buys the wrong accelerator family or bolts high-wattage GPUs into a generic whitebox chassis without co-design, the failure is immediate and expensive. Training jobs stall on PCIe switch bottlenecks, and high-concurrency inference violates latency targets because memory bandwidth was ignored on the spec sheet. Under sustained load, uncoordinated cooling quietly throttles cluster throughput in the middle of the night. :::info Corpus scope note Standalone NVIDIA DGX H100 and H200 System User Guides and NVIDIA DGX OS 6 User Guide manuals are not in the local book corpus. System internals, management commands, and facility rules in this chapter come from our hardware portfolio notes and *AI Systems Performance Engineering*. They also draw on *Data Center Best Practices with DGX B200* and the *DGX GB300 SuperPOD Reference Architecture*. Later chapters in Part 9 and Part 10 expand 8-GPU node internals, HGX platforms, Grace Superchips, NVL72 racks, facility cooling, and GPU microarchitecture. :::
Middle: When an operator flashes a standalone NIC or BIOS update downloaded from a component vendor onto a DGX node, link training timings drift out of alignment and GPUs drop off the PCIe bus. Managing a DGX fleet combines Out-of-Band control when the host OS is down with In-Band health daemons while jobs run. Part 7 covers Redfish protocol internals, and Chapter 9.2 covers the dual-BMC hierarchy and 46-component firmware inventory.
Exercise sample: 1. Work out the memory needed to serve a 13B model in FP16 for 32 users at 4,096 tokens of context. Pick three checks and state in one sentence each which rule from the chapter they test.

Opening: A distributed training job suddenly slows to half its expected throughput, or a server returns from maintenance with all eight GPUs stuck in a pending firmware state. Engineers who treat an eight-GPU server as a single motherboard cannot diagnose either failure. Inside its 8U enclosure, an NVIDIA DGX H100 or DGX H200 is a multi-board system. It is split across separate compute, accelerator, storage, and power trays. Two independent Baseboard Management Controllers and 46 distinct firmware images govern those physical zones.
Middle: A single misconfigured environment variable or container device assignment can route GPU network traffic across the wrong processor socket and cut multi-node training speed in half. Senior infrastructure engineers never trust cabling diagrams on paper alone. Instead, they verify how the Linux kernel and NVIDIA driver see the physical board traces by running `nvidia-smi topo -m`.

Opening: An enterprise buys a fleet of eight-GPU servers from a reseller at a 15 percent discount compared to an NVIDIA DGX. Once multi-node training starts, the cluster runs 40 to 50 percent slower than expected and rear GPUs throttle in a cold room. Although the NVIDIA DGX is the golden reference appliance for an AI factory, most data center SXM GPUs do not sit inside NVIDIA-branded servers. Instead, they run inside servers built by enterprise Original Equipment Manufacturers. Common examples include the Dell PowerEdge XE9680, Supermicro SYS-821GE, HPE ProLiant XD, and Lenovo ThinkSystem. They also run inside custom cloud racks engineered by hyperscalers like AWS, Microsoft Azure, and Google Cloud.
Middle: Even when an OEM HGX server has a balanced PCIe switch tree, it can still fail in production if rear GPUs overheat or if a single rack power feed trips during a storm. You must validate thermal airflow, proposal evidence quality, and single-feed electrical survival before deployment.

Opening: For fifteen years, accelerated computing lived with a painful compromise. Even as GPU High Bandwidth Memory soared into the multi-terabyte-per-second range, the host CPU stayed stranded behind a narrow PCIe bus. Whenever a recommender model looked up giant embedding tables in host RAM, the GPU sat idle. Long-context language models offloading key-value caches or graph networks sampling massive datasets paid the exact same PCIe tax. Engineers watched expensive accelerators stall while waiting for bytes to crawl across an I/O bus designed for network cards and storage drives. To remove this wall between the CPU and GPU, NVIDIA designed its own data center processor. The Grace CPU pairs 72 Arm Neoverse V2 cores with fast on-package LPDDR5X memory. It bonds directly to Hopper and Blackwell GPUs over NVLink-C2C at 900 GB/s bidirectional with full hardware cache coherence and a single virtual address space. Understanding how Grace, GH200, GB200, and GB300 Superchips work is essential for running modern rack-scale clusters. You must also learn what they do not solve automatically. GPU-to-GPU caches
Middle: In a GB200 or GB300 NVL72 compute tray, two Superchips sit side-by-side and are bridged by a coherent NVLink-C2C link. A single Linux OS kernel manages both Grace CPUs and all four GPUs as one node.

Opening: For nearly a decade, the boundary of an NVLink domain was the sheet-metal lid of an eight-GPU server. Inside that single box, GPUs exchanged tensors at ultra-high speed over an internal NVSwitch baseboard. The moment a model needed a ninth GPU in the next server, communication dropped onto slower network cards. For frontier Mixture-of-Experts and long-horizon reasoning models, that eight-GPU ceiling became a brick wall. Splitting expert-parallel and tensor-parallel groups across nine separate servers forced synchronous collectives across slower host NICs. Real-time inference Time to First Token ballooned past five seconds, and per-GPU decode throughput crawled at a few tokens per second. The NVIDIA GB200 NVL72 and DGX GB300 NVL72 solve that problem by changing the fundamental unit of computing. The rack, rather than the individual server, is now the computer. NVIDIA disaggregates the Grace CPUs and Blackwell GPUs across 18 compute trays. It links them through 9 NVLink switch trays over a passive rear copper backplane. That backplane delivers 130 TB/s of aggregate NVLink 5 bandwidth so the entire
Middle: Each Blackwell B200 or B300 GPU exposes 18 fifth-generation NVLink ports running at 100 GB/s bidirectional per port. That doubles the 50 GB/s link speed of Hopper NVLink 4. Because the number of NVLink 5 ports per GPU equals the exact number of NVSwitch ASICs in the rack, every GPU wires one dedicated link to each of the 18 NVSwitch ASICs through the rear copper backplane.

Opening: Scaling from one 8-GPU node to thousands of GPUs exposes every flaw in custom cluster engineering. If your networking team invents a bespoke spine-to-leaf ratio, jobs stall on multi-node collective operations. If your storage team buys a generic file server without testing massive checkpoint bursts, training pauses for ten minutes at a time. When facilities technicians run fiber through patch panels, InfiniBand optical budgets fail and links drop during bring-up. To replace one-off cluster construction with repeatable industrial manufacturing, NVIDIA created the DGX SuperPOD reference architecture. A SuperPOD is a physical twin of NVIDIA's own internal research supercomputers, built from pre-engineered Scalable Units, five logical network segments carried over four physical fabrics, and certified Standard and Enhanced storage tiers. Mastering how a Scalable Unit is sized across node-based and rack-scale generations, why a management appliance displaces one compute node, why partial deployments still require the full network fabric, and how to balance storage tiers is the foundation of AI factory design.
Middle: If user home directories, container builds, and cluster logs share the same file system as multi-terabyte model checkpoints, metadata storms from interactive users will stall training jobs. Conversely, running parallel training checkpoints over a generic enterprise NFS appliance will overwhelm its network ports and freeze the cluster.

Opening: An air-cooled NVIDIA DGX B200 packs eight Blackwell SXM6 GPUs into a single ten-unit chassis drawing 14.3 kW and demanding massive cold-aisle airflow. When you deploy two or four of those nodes per rack across a full Scalable Unit, traditional data center intuition fails in ways that surprise experienced facility engineers. For example, on a traditional dual-corded enterprise server, plugging half the power supplies into PDU A and half into PDU B increases uptime. On a DGX B200, that exact same dual interleaved wiring is a serious mistake that is not recommended by NVIDIA. Because a DGX B200 requires at least five of its six internal PSUs energized to operate, losing either PDU in an interleaved rack drops three PSUs on every server and crashes the entire rack. Similarly, spacing racks farther apart to dilute a dense heat plume can push InfiniBand cable runs past the physical reach of copper or optical cables. Mastering the balance across Power, Cooling, and Space while aligning facility, IT, network, and application teams is
Middle: When hot exhaust air recirculates around the sides or through empty rack slots into the cold intake, inlet temperatures spike above 30°C and trigger thermal throttling. Because supplemental rear-door heat exchangers and in-row coolers are not typically suitable for DGX B200 racks, facility teams must prevent exhaust recirculation through room-level containment and sealing.

Opening: Designing a liquid-cooled cluster for frontier reasoning models from scratch often leads to mismatched switch port counts and storage bottlenecks. Infrastructure engineers feel this pain when a multi-megawatt data hall sits idle because leaf switches lack uplink capacity or building telemetry cannot reach the cluster manager. The NVIDIA DGX GB300 SuperPOD Reference Architecture solves this problem by packaging Grace CPUs and Blackwell Ultra B300 GPUs into a repeatable factory blueprint. Earlier SuperPOD generations connected air-cooled nodes over NDR InfiniBand. The GB300 SuperPOD instead combines hybrid liquid-cooled DGX GB300 NVL72 racks with Quantum-X800 InfiniBand, Spectrum-4 Ethernet, and NVIDIA Mission Control. This modular design scales smoothly from a single Scalable Unit up to a two-layer fabric of sixteen Scalable Units.
Middle: Undersizing shared storage turns every model checkpoint and multimodal dataset load into an expensive cluster-wide stall where hundreds of GPUs sit idle waiting on disk I/O. AI researchers and platform operators feel this bottleneck whenever checkpoint saves eat into training uptime or storage firmware upgrades force a maintenance window. Separating high-throughput training storage from user home directories and matching array bandwidth to workload intensity keeps GPUs saturated.

Opening: In a traditional enterprise data center, a server uses two bonded network ports to carry all traffic over a single oversubscribed Top-of-Rack switch. That shared pipe mixes web requests, database queries, NFS storage mounts, Prometheus scrapes, and SSH logins. If you try to run a thousand-GPU AI factory on a converged network like that, the cluster fails immediately. A single checkpoint burst starves the Kubernetes control plane, or a noisy container pull triggers head-of-line blocking during a synchronous `ncclAllReduce` collective. An NVIDIA DGX SuperPOD prevents traffic cross-contamination by physically isolating five logical network segments across four distinct switch fabrics: 1. **The Scale-Up Multi-Node NVLink Fabric.** This fabric carries intra-rack GPU-to-GPU memory access at line rate across blind-mate copper backplanes. 2. **The Scale-Out Compute Fabric.** This non-blocking, rail-optimized InfiniBand fabric is dedicated exclusively to inter-node GPU collectives. 3. **The Storage and In-Band Ethernet Fabric.** Built on Spectrum-4 SN5600D switches, this fabric uses two separate, unbonded ports on each node's BlueField-3 DPU. One port carries lossless RoCEv2 storage while the second carries
Middle: Separating the 400 Gbps Storage Network on Port 1 from the In-Band Management Network on Port 2 of the physical BlueField-3 ports gives you three major operational advantages: - **Zero TCP interference on RoCEv2.** Heavy container image pulls from NGC or multi-gigabyte log flushes over the In-Band network can never consume buffer headroom on the lossless RoCEv2 storage port. - **Zero complex host policy routing.** Each physical interface sits in its own subnet and VLAN without fragile source-based routing rules on the host operating system. - **Future High Availability via HBN.** Dedicating one physical port to Storage and one to In-Band removes LACP link bonding on a single segment. Opening: Most enterprise teams do not start with a massive data center facility built for a multi-unit SuperPOD. Instead, organizations usually buy a single eight-GPU server for a data science team or a smaller departmental pod of two to sixteen nodes. When engineers treat a small pod like a single workstation or overbuild it like a massive cloud cluster, the project stalls. Either expensive GPUs sit idle behind single-GPU scripts, or a missing commissioning check crashes a two-week training job on day three. Designing and onboarding these smaller deployments requires three distinct architectural choices. First, you can eliminate the spine switch layer altogether when your node count fits inside a single layer of rail leaf switches. That leaf-only topology cuts optical transceiver and switch costs. It also keeps same-rail latency to a single switch hop.
Middle: Passing `nvidia-smi` and a thirty-second smoke test does not mean a new BasePOD or 128-GPU cluster is ready for production. Most multi-week training postmortems trace back to a physical or operational defect that existed on day one. Common day-one defects include a missing blanking panel causing hot-aisle recirculation, a GPU reseated into the wrong PCIe slot after an RMA, or a single rail landing on an oversubscribed uplink. Other clusters fail when an intermittent bus drop triggers after two days of heat soak, or when a Prometheus alert rule contains a misspelled metric name.

Opening: Software engineers grow up treating compute as an elastic cloud API where calling a function makes a hundred instances appear. In an AI factory, that cloud abstraction collides head-on with the laws of thermodynamics and electrical engineering. A legacy enterprise rack holds forty CPU web servers and draws very modest power. Roll a four-node rack of DGX H100 or DGX B200 servers into that legacy room without re-engineering the facility. Or roll in a single DGX GB200 or GB300 NVL72 rack. Two failures happen within minutes. First, the rack triggers an electrical brownout or trips its branch breakers. High-density GPU racks demand three to ten times more current than legacy branch circuits and power distribution units were rated to carry continuously. Either the upstream breakers trip under sustained load, or the bus voltage drops until the server power supplies latch off.
Middle: A rear-door heat exchanger fits 20 to 40 kW racks, but it is not suitable for the DGX B200 because of static pressure limits. Air-cooled DGX B200 racks require 4,290 to 8,580 CFM of high-velocity air, while OEM HGX servers in waterless rooms can use internal closed-loop liquid cooling. For 60 to 150 kW racks such as the GB200 and GB300 NVL72, direct-to-chip cold plates cool the Grace CPUs, Blackwell GPUs, and NVSwitches. Rack manifolds and dripless quick-disconnects carry the coolant to a Coolant Distribution Unit, while hybrid air cooling handles lower-power tray components.

Opening: An AI factory filled with thousands of liquid-cooled GPUs and fast InfiniBand switches is inert silicon until the software stack binds it into one coherent machine. If a researcher launches a standard single-process Python script on a fifty-million-dollar cluster, the job runs on one CPU core. The rest of the factory sits idle. Worse, if one node in a multi-node job boots with an older kernel driver or a mismatched PCIe device order, the whole run stalls. The same hang happens when a plain Kubernetes job places all pods except one, leaving the cluster stuck until a communication timeout kills the job thirty minutes later. 1. Cluster provisioning, firmware, and fabric management run through NVIDIA Mission Control, Base Command Manager, UFM Enterprise, and NMX-M. Together, they keep every compute tray, NVLink switch tray, and InfiniBand switch on an identical golden image and firmware matrix with automated node draining and self-healing. 2. Host kernel driver and containerized CUDA runtime alignment matches the host OS `nvidia.ko` driver branch to the user-space libraries.
Middle: In Kubernetes, as covered in Volume 21 Chapter 6 Part 3.2, three low-level building blocks form a multi-node job. 1. A Headless Service with `clusterIP: None` creates predictable DNS A-records for every pod matching the job selector. Each pod resolves at `<job-name>-<index>.<service-name>`, so Pod 0 resolves at `llama-70b-training-0.llama-train`. 2. An Indexed Job with `completionMode: Indexed`, `parallelism: 8`, and `completions: 8` injects `JOB_COMPLETION_INDEX` from 0 to 7 into each pod. You pass this index to `torchrun --node_rank=$JOB_COMPLETION_INDEX --master_addr=llama-70b-training-0.llama-train`. 3. Pod anti-affinity paired with `nvidia.com/gpu: 8` ensures each pod claims an entire 8-GPU node at 1 pod per node. The pod also receives RDMA and InfiniBand devices injected by the NVIDIA Network

Opening: When a data science lead walks into a budget meeting claiming that Kubernetes GPU allocation is nearly full, a Senior Solutions Architect does not guess. That lead may ask for five million dollars to buy sixteen more HGX H100 servers immediately. The same rigor applies when a startup CFO asks how many GPUs the company must buy over the next three years and when the cluster will run out of data center power. 1. Supply-chain lead times span three to nine months or more, so you cannot treat bare-metal GPUs or liquid-cooled racks like elastic cloud VMs. If you wait until your cluster is almost full to place an order, the business starves for six months waiting on delivery. Conversely, if you panic and buy a full three-year projection on the first day, millions of dollars of silicon sit idle. 2. The Allocation versus Utilization illusion, often called the Empty Hotel problem, hides massive waste inside Kubernetes clusters. Kubernetes `nvidia.com/gpu` allocation measures how many rooms are booked rather than how
Middle: To cut a runaway cloud GPU bill in half within thirty days, as detailed in Volume 19 Chapters 3 and 6 and Volume 21 Chapter 12 Part 2, align each workload tier with its matching financial contract.

Opening: Building an on-premises AI factory takes months of facility construction and multi-megawatt utility power contracts. Even teams that own physical SuperPODs regularly need elastic burst capacity for urgent training deadlines. As a result, infrastructure engineers spend much of their time deploying and operating workloads across cloud GPU platforms. Renting GPUs in the cloud is fundamentally different from renting standard CPU virtual machines. If a cloud provider hides the physical leaf-spine switch layout from your scheduler, multi-node jobs land on distant racks. Worse, routing high-speed RDMA traffic through a hypervisor virtual switch can cut training throughput by half or more.
Middle: Running an extra virtual machine layer inside a cloud provider adds operational friction when your entire software stack already runs in containers. CoreWeave pioneered the Kubernetes-native GPU cloud model to eliminate that redundant virtualization layer.
